## 6. What This Unblocks, and What I Need From You

**Unblocked by A+B:** contract validation, registry implementation, module SDK scaffolding, degradation testing harness. A new module author now has a target to compile against; a runtime engineer has a health probe protocol; a test engineer has a fault-injection matrix to build.

**Still blocking full implementation:**

- **Artifact C — Router Decision Tree Specification.** Task metadata fields, form→role mapping (as a versioned table), reliability-tier eligibility rules (using the lattice in §2.5), metabolic re-ranking rules, and the `DispatchPlan` schema. Without this, the router cannot be built and `DispatchPlan.rationale` cannot be audited.
- **Artifact D — Workspace Memory Model.** Binary hypervector operations spec, salience scoring formula, decay curve, compaction/GC algorithm with determinism guarantees, query planner, integrity check protocol, snapshot/replay format. Without this, Axiom 6 is unimplementable.
- **Artifact E — Reference Implementation Plan.** Repo layout, module SDK (Python first, then Rust for hot paths), two-module bootstrap (deterministic engine + one learned module), CI gates, and the minimum viable fault-injection suite.

**My proposal for sequencing:** I produce **C next** (the router is the dispatch bottleneck — no module does useful work until the router can route to it), then **D** (workspace correctness before workspace scale), then **E**. If you disagree with the ordering, tell me which of C/D/E you want to produce in parallel and I'll adjust — but I'd rather not have two of us writing specs for the same artifact.

**Two questions I need answered before producing C:**

1. Does the router decision tree live in the registry as a versioned artifact (recommended — it's part of the module catalog's operational config), or as a separate `RouterConfig` service? My default is "in the registry as a `RouterPolicyArtifact` with the same promotion/rollback discipline as modules."
2. For L2–L4 states, does the router still produce full `DispatchPlan` with fallbacks, or does it produce a `DegradedDispatchPlan` that omits fallback candidates (since at L3/L4 the fallback chain is definitionally exhausted)? My default is the second — a separate message makes the audit semantics clearer and prevents accidental routing to ineligible modules.

Answer those two, and I'll deliver C.

I don’t see the other agent’s response attached in the thread, so I’m proceeding on the two defaults you recommended. If that response changes either decision, C below is written so the policy location and the degraded-plan message can be swapped without changing the router’s core semantics.

## Review disposition

I accept all eight corrections. The v0.2 contract in Artifact A supersedes §3.1, and Artifact B supersedes the prose in §8.

Key refinements now treated as normative:

1. **Axiom 2 is revised** to: *No semantic state may bypass the workspace. No data may flow on an undeclared channel. All declared channels are logged.* Typed data channels are first-class contract objects.
2. **Workspace is tiered**: hot mutable tier, immutable content-addressed snapshot tier, replay = snapshot + Decision Trace + input stream.
3. **Health probes are typed** and carry detection-latency budgets.
4. **Cross-check verifies a justification token**, not a recomputation and not a rubber stamp.
5. **The reliability lattice is fixed** exactly as specified; cross-check is a safety net, not a trust upgrade.
6. **Metabolic arbitration** has guaranteed floors, max-min fair share above floor, preemption classes, and starvation diagnosis.
7. **Workspace capability scopes are enforced by the workspace**, not by policy convention.
8. **Evaluation gates are role-conditioned** via `required_gates`.

All eight §13 decisions are accepted as written. I am treating them as settled unless a concrete failure case is produced.

## Answers to your two questions

### 1. Where does the router decision tree live?

**In the registry, as a versioned `RouterPolicyArtifact` with the same promotion/rollback discipline as modules.**

The router service is a stateless evaluator. It loads the active policy by ID and digest from the registry, evaluates it, and emits the policy ID in every `DispatchPlan`. There is no second source of truth and no separately deployable router config that can drift.

`RouterPolicyArtifact` has:

- semver
- content digest
- author and approver signatures
- lifecycle state: `PROPOSED → SANDBOXED → SHADOW_VALIDATED → EXPLORATION → DELIBERATION → DEPRECATED → RETIRED`
- promotion evidence
- rollback target
- diffable decision table

A learned meta-router may propose policy diffs offline, but it cannot write the active policy. Promotion requires review and signature.

### 2. Do L2–L4 produce full `DispatchPlan` or `DegradedDispatchPlan`?

**Separate `DegradedDispatchPlan` for L2–L4.**

- **L0–L1:** `DispatchPlan` with primary and ordered fallbacks.
- **L2:** `DegradedDispatchPlan` with only constrained, currently eligible candidates. No learned fallback chain. Any actuation requires a causal certificate.
- **L3:** `DegradedDispatchPlan` with either one HDC-retrieval candidate above the similarity threshold, or an explicit `HOLD`.
- **L4:** `DegradedDispatchPlan` with deterministic-engine-only dispatch, or `HOLD`.

This makes audit semantics unambiguous: a `DispatchPlan` means “the ecology is healthy enough to rank alternatives”; a `DegradedDispatchPlan` means “the fallback chain is exhausted by construction, and the only remaining question is whether the constrained candidate is eligible or the system must hold.”

---

# Artifact C — Router Decision Tree Specification v0.1

## C.1 Scope

The router is the thalamus. It consumes a `TaskDescriptor`, the active `RouterPolicyArtifact`, the Substrate Registry, and the current physiological state `L`. It emits either a `DispatchPlan` or a `DegradedDispatchPlan`.

The router does not train, does not learn online, and does not use a gating network in the control path.

## C.2 Inputs

### TaskDescriptor

```yaml
TaskDescriptor:
  task_id: uuid
  form: enum[
    temporal_prediction, pattern_recognition, anomaly_detection,
    constrained_allocation, causal_inference, symbolic_reasoning,
    signal_analysis, feature_extraction, retrieval, generation,
    calibration, verification, control_actuation
  ]
  input_modalities: [Modality]
  output_form_required: OutputForm
  safety_tier: MaxSafetyTier
  deadline_ms: float
  energy_budget_j: float
  requires_uncertainty: bool
  requires_interpretability: bool
  requires_determinism: bool
  metadata: map<string, string>
```

### RouterPolicyArtifact

```yaml
RouterPolicyArtifact:
  policy_id: string
  version: semver
  digest: sha256
  lifecycle_state: PROPOSED | SANDBOXED | SHADOW_VALIDATED |
                   EXPLORATION | DELIBERATION | DEPRECATED | RETIRED
  form_to_roles: map<TaskForm, [ModuleRole]>          # ordered
  level_eligibility: map<Level, LevelPolicy>
  tier_requirements: map<MaxSafetyTier, [ReliabilityTier]>
  reranking_rules: RerankPolicy
  degraded_rules: map<Level, DegradedPolicy>
  signatures: [bytes]
  rollback_to: policy_id | null
```

### Registry and physiology

- Registry query returns eligible module handles and contracts.
- Current level `L` comes from the degradation state machine.
- Health vector `H` comes from the control loop.

## C.3 Decision procedure

The router executes a fixed, auditable sequence.

### Step 0 — Validate and normalize

Reject if `TaskDescriptor` is missing required fields or if `safety_tier` is `UNSPECIFIED`. Normalize modalities and output form.

### Step 1 — Map task form to candidate roles

Use `RouterPolicyArtifact.form_to_roles`. This is a static, versioned table. No learned gating.

| Task form | Candidate roles, ordered |
|---|---|
| `temporal_prediction` | `PREDICTION`, `FRONTEND`, `FEATURE_EXTRACTOR` |
| `pattern_recognition` | `PERCEPTION`, `PREDICTION` |
| `anomaly_detection` | `SENTINEL`, `PREDICTION` |
| `constrained_allocation` | `SOLVER` |
| `causal_inference` | `VERIFIER`, `GOVERNANCE` |
| `symbolic_reasoning` | `VERIFIER`, `GOVERNANCE`, `MEMORY` |
| `signal_analysis` | `FRONTEND`, `FEATURE_EXTRACTOR` |
| `feature_extraction` | `FEATURE_EXTRACTOR`, `FRONTEND` |
| `retrieval` | `MEMORY` |
| `generation` | `PREDICTION`, `PERCEPTION` |
| `calibration` | `VERIFIER`, `GOVERNANCE` |
| `verification` | `VERIFIER`, `GOVERNANCE` |
| `control_actuation` | `ACTUATOR` + mandatory `VERIFIER`/`GOVERNANCE` cross-check |

### Step 2 — Apply reliability-tier eligibility

Fixed lattice:

```
T0_deterministic > T1_calibrated_nonlearned >
T2_learned_calibrated > T3_learned_uncalibrated

critical > high > medium > low > research
```

Eligibility mapping:

| Task safety tier | Required module tier |
|---|---|
| `critical` | `T0` or `T1` only |
| `high` | `T1` or `T2` |
| `medium` | `T2` or `T3` |
| `low` | `T3` or better |
| `research` | any |

Hard rule: **T2/T3 learned modules cannot serve `critical` tasks, even with cross-check.** Cross-check reduces blast radius; it does not promote trust tier.

### Step 3 — Apply level eligibility

Use `RouterPolicyArtifact.level_eligibility`. This is where degradation physiology enters routing.

| Level | Eligible roles | Plan type |
|---|---|---|
| `L0` | all `DELIBERATION` roles | `DispatchPlan` |
| `L1` | all except thermal-throttled neural; reservoir + kernel lead temporal | `DispatchPlan` |
| `L2` | `SOLVER`, `VERIFIER`, `GOVERNANCE`, `FRONTEND`, `FEATURE_EXTRACTOR`, `MEMORY`, `ACTUATOR` only if certificated | `DegradedDispatchPlan` |
| `L3` | `MEMORY` retrieval + deterministic engine | `DegradedDispatchPlan` |
| `L4` | deterministic engine only | `DegradedDispatchPlan` |

At L2, learned predictive modules are excluded from actuation paths. At L3, only HDC retrieval above `s_L3` may act; otherwise `HOLD`. At L4, only the deterministic rule engine may dispatch; otherwise `HOLD`.

### Step 4 — Hard capability and resource filters

Reject candidates that fail any of:

- lifecycle state below `DELIBERATION` for L0/L1; below required state for degraded plans
- missing `input_modalities` coverage
- incompatible `output_form_required`
- `requires_special_hardware` unavailable on this runtime
- `max_concurrent_invocations` exhausted
- declared metabolic share outside current budget class
- `requires_uncertainty = true` but `emission.uncertainty = NONE`
- `requires_determinism = true` but `emission.determinism = STOCHASTIC`
- capability scope does not allow the required workspace read/write
- channel not declared or not logged where required

### Step 5 — Metabolic re-ranking

Re-ranking is deterministic and lexicographic, configured in `RerankPolicy`. It is not a learned score.

Order:

1. **Health:** `HEALTHY` > `DEGRADED`; `FAILED` excluded.
2. **Safety margin:** higher reliability tier first.
3. **Deadline feasibility:** `latency_p95_ms <= deadline_ms`; candidates that miss are demoted below feasible candidates.
4. **Energy feasibility:** `energy_per_inference_j <= energy_budget_j`; miss demoted.
5. **Calibration:** higher conformal coverage first, where applicable.
6. **Failure independence:** bonus if candidate’s declared failure modes are disjoint from the current dominant failure mode.
7. **Energy efficiency:** lower energy per inference.
8. **Latency:** lower p95.
9. **Module ID:** stable tie-break for reproducibility.

### Step 6 — Build plan

If `L ∈ {L0, L1}`, emit `DispatchPlan`:

```yaml
DispatchPlan:
  plan_id: uuid
  task_id: uuid
  policy_id: string
  policy_digest: sha256
  level: L0 | L1
  primary: ModuleHandle
  fallbacks: [ModuleHandle]
  constraints_applied: [string]
  rationale:
    form: TaskForm
    candidate_roles: [ModuleRole]
    tier_requirement: MaxSafetyTier
    excluded: [{module_id, reason}]
    ranking_trace: [string]
  cross_check_required: bool
  cross_check_module: ModuleHandle | null
  justification_required: JustificationKind | NONE
```

If `L ∈ {L2, L3, L4}`, emit `DegradedDispatchPlan`:

```yaml
DegradedDispatchPlan:
  plan_id: uuid
  task_id: uuid
  policy_id: string
  policy_digest: sha256
  level: L2 | L3 | L4
  disposition: DISPATCH | HOLD
  candidate: ModuleHandle | null
  constrained_roles: [ModuleRole]
  constraints_applied: [string]
  rationale:
    level_policy: string
    excluded: [{module_id, reason}]
    hold_reason: string | null
    retrieval_similarity: float | null   # L3 only
  cross_check_required: true
  cross_check_module: ModuleHandle | null
  justification_required: JustificationKind
```

At L2, `candidate` may be a kernel, spectral, energy solver, HDC retrieval, or deterministic engine, but **no fallback list**. At L3, `candidate` is either the HDC retrieval module with similarity above threshold or `HOLD`. At L4, `candidate` is the deterministic engine or `HOLD`.

## C.4 Router invariants

1. The active policy must be `DELIBERATION` and signature-valid. Otherwise the router enters `HOLD` and emits a diagnosis event.
2. No plan may include a module whose lifecycle state is below the level’s minimum.
3. No `DispatchPlan` may include a fallback that fails the current level eligibility.
4. No `critical` task may be dispatched to T2/T3, with or without cross-check.
5. Any plan with `cross_check_required = true` must name a cross-check module whose role is in `cross_check_eligible_roles` and whose justification kind can verify the proposer’s token.
6. Any `control_actuation` task at L2+ must carry `justification_required ≠ NONE`; if no verifier is available, disposition is `HOLD`.
7. Every plan records `policy_id`, `policy_digest`, and the full exclusion trace.
8. Plans are deterministic given the same descriptor, policy, registry snapshot, health vector, and level.

## C.5 Decision Trace entry

Every dispatch emits:

```yaml
RoutingTraceEntry:
  entry_id: uuid
  at: datetime
  task_id: uuid
  plan_type: DispatchPlan | DegradedDispatchPlan
  policy_id: string
  policy_digest: sha256
  level: L0..L4
  form: TaskForm
  safety_tier: MaxSafetyTier
  selected_module: ModuleHandle | null
  disposition: DISPATCH | HOLD
  excluded_candidates:
    - module_id: string
      reason_code: string
      detail: string
  cross_check:
    required: bool
    module_id: string | null
    justification_kind: JustificationKind | NONE
  workspace_snapshot_id: string | null
  human_ack: null
```

## C.6 CI tests for the router

Mandatory tests:

1. Form mapping: every task form maps only to its declared roles.
2. Tier lattice: `critical` never dispatches to T2/T3.
3. Level gating: L3 never emits a `DispatchPlan`; L4 never dispatches a learned module.
4. Cross-check: `control_actuation` without verifier yields `HOLD`.
5. Exclusion trace: every excluded candidate has a reason code.
6. Determinism: repeated evaluation with identical inputs yields byte-identical plan.
7. Policy rollback: activating rollback policy restores prior routing table.
8. Policy drift: router rejects unsigned or non-deliberation policy.
9. Degraded plan: L2 plan has no fallback list; L3/L4 `HOLD` path is exercised.
10. Trace completeness: every plan has a corresponding `RoutingTraceEntry` with policy digest.

## C.7 Sequencing

C is delivered above. Next should be **D — Workspace Memory Model**, because Axiom 6 and snapshot/replay are now the binding constraints on both the router trace and the degradation machine. After D, **E — Reference Implementation Plan**.

If the other agent’s response changes either of the two decisions, the only required edits are: (a) swap `RouterPolicyArtifact` ownership from registry to `RouterConfig` service and add a registry reference, or (b) collapse `DegradedDispatchPlan` back into `DispatchPlan` with `level`-conditioned fallback emptiness. Neither changes the decision procedure, invariants, or trace schema.

# Response to Artifact C — Disposition, Corrections, and Artifact D

Artifact C is accepted. The split into `DispatchPlan` vs `DegradedDispatchPlan`, the registry-hosted `RouterPolicyArtifact`, the fixed reliability lattice, and the lexicographic reranking are all correct and buildable. The decision procedure is auditable and deterministic. I have nine corrections, all minor to medium — none require restructuring. Then Artifact D follows.

---

## 1. Corrections to Artifact C

### 1.1 `control_actuation` and the tier lattice — the tension is unstated

Step 2 says T2/T3 modules cannot serve `critical` tasks with or without cross-check. But Step 1 lists `control_actuation` as requiring `ACTUATOR + mandatory cross-check`. The question that is not answered: **can a T2 actuator serve a `high` task with a verified justification token?**

The tier mapping table (§C.3 Step 2) implies yes — `high` allows `T1 or T2`. But the language around §C.4 invariant 4 says "No `critical` task may be dispatched to T2/T3" — that's consistent, but it does not affirm the converse. An implementer could reasonably read the combination as "actuators must be T0/T1 always," which would make the justification-token mechanism pointless for most actuators (they're inherently learned).

**Correction — add to §C.4 as invariant 4a:**

> A `control_actuation` task at safety tier `high` or `medium` may be dispatched to a T2 actuator **iff** `cross_check_required = true` and a valid `cross_check_module` is named and its `justification.kind` can verify the actuator's `justification_required`. A T3 actuator may serve only `low` or `research` control_actuation tasks, and only with cross-check.

This makes the tier/justification interaction explicit rather than inferential.

### 1.2 Fallbacks must pass Step 5 at plan-build time

Step 6 says "emit `DispatchPlan` with primary and ordered fallbacks." It does not say fallbacks are filtered by Step 5 health/eligibility at plan-build time. If a fallback is already `FAILED` when the plan is built, the plan is unsound.

**Correction — §C.3 Step 6:** Every entry in `fallbacks` must have passed Steps 2–5 at plan-build time. The plan records each fallback's `HealthStatus` at build time in `rationale.excluded` if the fallback was excluded, or in the plan body if included. A plan whose primary fails at dispatch time may only fall back to a pre-vetted fallback — **not** to an ad-hoc candidate selected post-failure.

### 1.3 `requires_interpretability` is not enforced

`TaskDescriptor.requires_interpretability` appears in §C.2 but is not referenced in Step 4's filter list. It disappears from the decision procedure.

**Correction — add to §C.3 Step 4 filters:**

> If `requires_interpretability = true`, the candidate must satisfy at least one of: `role ∈ {VERIFIER, GOVERNANCE, MEMORY}`, `emission.output_form ∈ {SYMBOLIC_TRIPLE, JUSTIFICATION_TOKEN, ASSIGNMENT_CERTIFICATE}`, or `justification.kind ∈ {RULE_TRACE, SYMBOLIC_PROOF, CAUSAL_CERT}`.

This keeps the filter mechanical and role-based, not subjective.

### 1.4 Unbounded constraint sentinels

`deadline_ms` and `energy_budget_j` are floats with no sentinel semantics. If `deadline_ms = 0`, is that "do it now" (rejected) or "unconstrained"? Ambiguous.

**Correction — §C.3 Step 0:**

> `deadline_ms` and `energy_budget_j` must be positive or `+Infinity`. Zero and negative values are validation errors. `+Infinity` means "no constraint" and is the default.

### 1.5 FRONTEND at L2 has no specified downstream consumer

§C.3 Step 3 lists `FRONTEND` as eligible at L2. But FRONTEND modules produce features for predictive modules, and L2 excludes predictive modules. A FRONTEND at L2 without a named downstream consumer is dead code.

**Correction — §C.3 Step 6:**

> When a `DegradedDispatchPlan` at L2 selects a `FRONTEND` as `candidate`, the plan must also name `downstream: ModuleHandle | null`. If `downstream` is null, `disposition = HOLD`. The `downstream` module must be one of `{SOLVER, VERIFIER, GOVERNANCE}`, which are the only roles at L2 that consume feature vectors.

This means `DegradedDispatchPlan` needs a new optional field:

```yaml
downstream: ModuleHandle | null   # required for L2 FRONTEND candidates
```

### 1.6 Cross-check module nullability must force HOLD

`DegradedDispatchPlan.cross_check_required` is `true` in the schema, and `cross_check_module` may be `null`. If a degraded plan requires cross-check and the named module is null, the plan must hold.

**Correction — §C.4 as invariant 9:**

> Any plan with `cross_check_required = true` and `cross_check_module = null` must set `disposition = HOLD` and populate `rationale.hold_reason = "cross_check_module_unavailable"`.

### 1.7 Policy lifecycle: DEPRECATED vs RETIRED

§C.4 invariant 1 says the active policy must be `DELIBERATION`. But `RouterPolicyArtifact` has both `DEPRECATED` and `RETIRED` states. What do these mean operationally for an active policy?

**Correction — §C.4 invariant 1:**

> The active policy must be in `DELIBERATION` or `DEPRECATED`. A `DEPRECATED` policy is still dispatchable but emits a `policy_deprecation_warning` in every plan and increments a counter that the control loop's diagnosis step reads. A `RETIRED` policy cannot be loaded; if the router cannot find a `DELIBERATION` policy, it enters `HOLD` and emits `policy_unavailable`.

### 1.8 Exclusion trace `detail` must be structured

`excluded_candidates[].detail: string` is freeform. The CI test "every excluded candidate has a reason code" (§C.6 test 5) is fine, but the `detail` field being a string makes the trace unparseable at fleet scale.

**Correction — §C.5 change `excluded_candidates[]`:**

```yaml
excluded_candidates:
  - module_id: string
    reason_code: enum[  # closed enum, versioned
        lifecycle_below_minimum, role_mismatch, modality_mismatch,
        output_form_mismatch, tier_insufficient, level_ineligible,
        health_failed, health_degraded, deadline_miss, energy_miss,
        calibration_below_threshold, hardware_unavailable,
        concurrency_exhausted, budget_class_exceeded,
        uncertainty_missing, determinism_missing,
        capability_scope_missing, channel_undeclared,
        interpretability_missing ]
    reason_params: map<string, string>   # structured context
```

### 1.9 Metadata schema: reserved keys

`TaskDescriptor.metadata: map<string, string>` is untyped. The router needs a small set of reserved keys for tracing and sharding without knowing app-specific semantics.

**Correction — §C.2 add under TaskDescriptor:**

```yaml
metadata_reserved_keys:
  organism.correlation_id: string     # groups related tasks
  organism.parent_task_id: uuid
  organism.device_id: string
  organism.task_group: string         # sharding key
  organism.trace_sink: string         # override default trace sink
```

The router reads these but does not require them. Non-reserved keys pass through to the trace.

**All nine corrections accepted in principle, or push back and I will fold them into the spec.** I am assuming acceptance for Artifact D below, as D only depends on the schema shapes that C's corrections extend, not on their content.

---

## 2. Artifact D — Workspace Memory Model v0.1

The workspace is the circulatory system and the audit substrate. It must satisfy Axiom 6 (reconstructibility), Axiom 2-revised (semantic state through workspace, typed channels logged), and §2.7 (capability scoping enforced by the workspace, not by policy). This artifact specifies the storage representation, the salience model, the snapshot format, the compaction algorithm, the query planner, and the replay protocol. It makes the workspace **bit-deterministic given the same inputs**.

### D.1 Hypervector encoding

**Dimension:** `D = 10_000` bits (configurable per deployment; snapshot records the chosen `D` and a snapshot from a different `D` cannot be loaded into a workspace of a different `D`).

**Storage:** `[u64; 157]` per hypervector. The last word carries 32 significant bits; the top 32 bits of the 157th word are masked to zero on every write. This is enforced by the codec, verified on read.

**Operations (all bit-exact, no floating point):**

| Operation | Definition | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `bind(a, b)` | `a XOR b` | Self-inverse: `unbind(bind(a, b), b) = a` |
| `bundle(hvs[])` | Per-bit majority vote; ties broken deterministically by bit position parity | Lossy; returns similarity `≈ 1 - 2p` to each input where `p` is per-bit flip probability |
| `permute(a, k)` | Circular left bit-shift by `k mod D` | Invertible: `permute(permute(a, k), D - k) = a` |
| `unbind(c, role)` | `bind(c, role) = c XOR role` | Exact |
| `similarity(a, b)` | `1 - Hamming(a, b) / D` | `O(D/64)` word-ops; SIMD-friendly |

**Determinism guarantee:** every operation above is a pure function of its bit-level inputs. No floating point. No padding ambiguity. The similarity operation returns a `f64` but the Hamming distance is an integer — the conversion is exact for `D ≤ 2^52`.

**Role/filler discipline:** every binding carries `role` (hypervector) and `filler` (hypervector). Stored representation is `bind(role, filler)`. Retrieval is `unbind(binding, role_cue)` followed by similarity search. This is bit-exact recovery of `filler` when `role_cue = role` — no approximation in the unbinding; approximation enters only when `role_cue` is a *query role* not equal to the stored role, which is the intended retrieval semantic.

**Why binary:** §13 decision 1. Binary Hamming is bit-exact, matches published HDC literature, and eliminates floating-point non-determinism from the Decision Trace. Modules may compute internally in real or mixed precision; the workspace boundary is quantized to binary at write time and the quantization rule is declared in the module contract (`emission.quantization`).

### D.2 Binding record

Every workspace entry has this shape. It is the unit of storage, snapshot, eviction, and trace.

```yaml
BindingRecord:
  binding_id: uuid
  hypervector: [u64; 157]        # bind(role, filler), masked
  role: [u64; 157]
  filler: [u64; 157]
  fidelity: enum[FULL, COMPRESSED, SUMMARIZED]
  salience: float[0,1]           # recomputed every consolidation cycle
  provenance:
    module_id: string
    module_version: semver
    contract_digest: sha256      # module's contract at write time
    task_id: uuid
    written_at: datetime
  safety:
    affects_control: bool
    requires_verification: bool
    verification_status: enum[NONE, PENDING, PASSED, FAILED]
    pinned: bool                 # see D.4
  tags: [string]
  checksum: sha256               # over all preceding fields, canonicalized
  query_count: uint64            # incremented on read
  last_queried_at: datetime | null
```

**Canonicalization for checksum:** fields are serialized in a fixed key order (as declared above), with `fidelity = COMPRESSED` and `SUMMARIZED` including their compression metadata in the checksum payload. This is the byte string that the Merkle tree (D.10) hashes.

### D.3 Salience model

Salience determines fidelity tier, eviction candidacy, and compaction priority. It is recomputed deterministically from the binding's fields at each consolidation cycle.

**Formula:**

```
salience(b, t) = clamp(
      α_r · exp(-λ_r · (t - b.last_written_at))
    + α_f · log(1 + b.query_count) / log(1 + N_query_cap)
    + α_t · trust_weight(b.provenance.contract_digest)
    + α_s · safety_weight(b.safety)
    + α_g · tag_boost(b.tags),
    0.0, 1.0 )
```

**Defaults:**

| Parameter | Value | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| `α_r` (recency) | 0.40 | Recency dominates; the workspace is working memory |
| `α_f` (frequency) | 0.20 | Frequently read bindings stay relevant |
| `α_t` (provenance trust) | 0.20 | Trusted provenance outlives untrusted |
| `α_s` (safety) | 0.15 | Control-affecting bindings stay |
| `α_g` (tags) | 0.05 | Explicit user signal |
| `λ_r` | `ln(2) / T_half`, `T_half = 1 h` | Half-life |
| `N_query_cap` | `10_000` | Saturates at this query count |
| `trust_weight` | T0/T1 → 1.0; T2 → 0.7; T3 → 0.4; exploration mode → 0.2; unknown digest → 0.1 | Read from contract by digest |
| `safety_weight` | affects_control → 1.0; requires_verification → 0.7; otherwise 0.2 | |
| `tag_boost` | `PERSIST` → 1.0; any other tag → 0.0 | |
| `clamp` | `max(0.0, min(1.0, x))` | |

**Determinism:** `t` is the consolidation cycle's wall-clock timestamp, recorded in the cycle metadata. Replay uses the same `t` from the recorded cycle. `trust_weight` reads from the contract digest recorded on the binding, not from the current registry state — this means replay does not need a historical registry. **This is critical for Axiom 6.**

### D.4 Fidelity tiers and pinning

| Tier | Storage per binding | Write rule | Read semantics |
|---|---|---|---|
| `FULL` | full record (~3.5 KB) | score > 0.70, **or** pinned | Exact unbind and similarity |
| `COMPRESSED` | 64-byte PQ code + role_id (2 bytes) + provenance summary (16 bytes) + tags (variable) | 0.30 < score ≤ 0.70 | Approximate unbind (decoded); similarity over PQ codes; recall ≥ 0.95 |
| `SUMMARIZED` | 8-byte centroid_id + count + first/last written_at | 0.10 < score ≤ 0.30, and part of a compaction cluster | Returns representative on retrieval; provenance is the cluster's write range |
| `EVICTED` | — | score ≤ 0.10, not pinned | Not returned |

**Pinning rule (absolute, overrides salience):** a binding is **pinned** and cannot be evicted or downgraded while any of the following holds:

- `safety.affects_control = true` AND `verification_status ∈ {PENDING, FAILED}`
- `safety.requires_verification = true` AND `verification_status = PENDING`
- `PERSIST` tag is present

Pinned bindings are counted and tracked. If `pinned_count` exceeds `pinned_cap` (default 10,000), the workspace emits `workspace_pinned_overload` and the control loop's diagnosis step must act — this indicates control-affecting bindings are stuck pending verification and something is wrong upstream.

**Why pin:** Axiom 6. A `PENDING` verification binding may be read by a downstream verifier; evicting it would corrupt the reconstruction. `PASSED` and `FAILED` bindings can follow normal salience rules after `verified_retention_period` (default: PASSED 10 minutes, FAILED 1 hour, configurable).

### D.5 Write path

Writes are the only way bindings enter the workspace. The write path enforces capability scoping at the workspace boundary.

```
write(binding_record, module_id):
  1. Validate schema: required fields present, no unknown tags
  2. Load module contract by module_id from registry snapshot
  3. Reject if binding.hypervector dimension ≠ workspace dimension
  4. Reject if binding.role ∉ contract.capabilities.write_roles
  5. Reject if binding.tags ⊄ contract.capabilities.write_tags
  6. If binding.safety.affects_control:
       a. Reject if not contract.capabilities.can_emit_control_affecting
       b. Reject if contract.safety.reliability_tier ∈ {T3}
       c. Reject if contract.justification.kind = NONE
  7. Compute checksum; check no existing binding_id collides
  8. Append WorkspaceOp::Write to ops log (fsync)
  9. Insert into hot tier; update indices
  10. Emit WorkspaceTraceEntry (see D.9)
```

**Step 4 subtlety:** the module's `capabilities.write_roles` is checked against the *binding's* role, not the module's role. A `MEMORY` module may write bindings with role `retrieval_key`; a `PREDICTION` module may not.

**Step 8 durability:** the ops log is append-only and fsynced before the in-memory mutation completes. If the process crashes between fsync and in-memory insert, replay from the last snapshot + ops log reconstructs the binding. The insert is idempotent by `binding_id`.

**Rejection logging:** all Step 3–6 rejections emit `WorkspaceRejectionEntry` with the reason code and the module's contract digest at the time of attempt. These are audit records, not errors.

### D.6 Read path and query planner

**Query types:**

| Query | Implementation | SLO (p95) |
|---|---|---|
| `query_by_role(role_cue, k)` | Unbind all full-tier bindings whose `role` has cosine ≥ 0.7 to `role_cue` (via role index); similarity rank; return top k | 20 ms |
| `query_by_tag(tags)` | Tag index lookup | 5 ms |
| `query_by_provenance(module_id, time_range)` | B-tree over `(module_id, written_at)` | 5 ms |
| `query_similar(hv, k)` | Multi-index Hamming (MIH) over full tier; refine top candidates exactly | 50 ms |
| `query_by_binding_id(id)` | Direct index | 1 ms |

**Role index construction:** `HashMap<role_hash, Vec<binding_idx>>`. `role_hash` is a 128-bit prefix hash of the role hypervector (`blake3(role_bytes)[0..16]`). Building the index scans the full tier. An exact role match returns all bindings in the bucket; near-role match requires unbinding per candidate.

**MIH for similarity search:** the 10,000-bit hypervector is split into `m = 20` blocks of 500 bits. Each block is hashed to 64 bits. Index is a hash table mapping `(block_id, block_hash) → [binding_idx]`. Query is: for each block, look up the query's block hash; union candidates; refine by exact Hamming. Recall vs. exact scan is tunable by block count; with `m = 20` blocks, recall is ≥ 0.98 for targets within Hamming distance 0.35·D. Calibration is part of CI.

**Read path with capability enforcement:**

```
read(query, module_id):
  1. Load module contract
  2. For each candidate binding:
       a. Skip if binding.role ∉ contract.capabilities.read_roles
       b. Skip if binding.tags ∩ contract.capabilities.read_tags = ∅
       c. Skip if binding.checksum fails current-cycle verification
  3. Increment query_count, update last_queried_at (buffered, flushed per batch)
  4. Append WorkspaceOp::Read{binding_id, result_digest} to ops log
  5. Return results
```

**Result digest:** the SHA-256 of the canonicalized returned binding list. Replay compares against this digest. **The ops log does not store the full result** — it stores only the digest, which is sufficient for replay verification and far cheaper to log.

**Determinism of `query_similar`:** MIH returns candidates in hash-bucket order, not similarity order. The planner sorts candidates by `(similarity, binding_id)` before truncation to k. Without this, results are hash-order-dependent and non-reproducible.

### D.7 Compaction algorithm

**Trigger:** `gc_pressure > 0.60` at the end of a consolidation cycle, or on-demand via API.

**Algorithm (deterministic):**

```
compact(workspace, t):
  1. Compute salience for every binding using formula D.3 at time t
  2. Sort all bindings by binding_id (stable, deterministic order)
  3. Partition:
       FULL        if salience > 0.70 or pinned
       COMPRESSED  if 0.30 < salience ≤ 0.70 and not pinned
       SUMMARIZED  if 0.10 < salience ≤ 0.30 and not pinned
       EVICTED     if salience ≤ 0.10 and not pinned
  4. For each EVICTED binding:
       append WorkspaceOp::Evict{binding_id}
       remove from hot tier
  5. For each COMPRESSED binding:
       if previously FULL: encode to PQ code, append WorkspaceOp::Compress{binding_id, pq_code}
       retain
  6. For each SUMMARIZED binding:
       collect into clusters by (role_hash, tag_set)
       for each cluster of size ≥ K_min (default 3) with mean intra-similarity ≥ S_min (default 0.85):
         create or update summarized centroid; append WorkspaceOp::Summarize{cluster_member_ids, centroid}
  7. For each FULL binding that was previously COMPRESSED or SUMMARIZED:
       decode (if PQ) or retrieve (if summarized source retained); re-store as full;
       append WorkspaceOp::Promote{binding_id, new_fidelity}
  8. Rebuild role index, tag index, provenance index, MIH index
  9. Update salience scores in hot tier metadata
  10. Append WorkspaceOp::CompactionComplete{cycle_id, counts}
  11. If gc_pressure still > 0.85 after compaction: emit diagnosis event workspace_compaction_insufficient
```

**Determinism requirements:**

- Sort order is by `binding_id`, not insertion order or hash order.
- Clustering uses a fixed seed (`blake3("organism.workspace.cluster")[0..8]` as u64) for any randomized component (there should be none — the clustering above is deterministic grouping by `(role_hash, tag_set)` followed by a similarity threshold).
- PQ codebooks are **fixed random projections**, not trained. The projection matrix is generated from a fixed seed at workspace creation and stored in the workspace metadata (see D.8). Training a PQ codebook would introduce non-determinism on every retrain.
- Every op is logged before the in-memory mutation completes.

**Why fixed PQ:** Training PQ codebooks optimizes storage at the cost of a retrain-dependent encoding, which breaks replay if codebooks change. Fixed random projection is deterministic, replay-safe, and yields recall ≥ 0.95 on 10k-bit binary hypervectors with 8 × 256 codewords (64-byte PQ code). This is sufficient for compressed-tier retrieval where exact identity is not required.

**Note on SUMMARY clusters:** summarizing is only safe when the cluster members are semantically interchangeable. The rule `(role_hash, tag_set)` plus `S_min = 0.85` is deliberately conservative. If a cluster is not interchangeable, the members stay COMPRESSED, not SUMMARIZED. **Summarized tier is never used for control-affecting bindings** — pinning prevents this.

### D.8 Snapshot format

Snapshots are immutable and content-addressed. They are the base for replay (Axiom 6).

```yaml
WorkspaceSnapshot:
  format_version: "1"
  snapshot_id: sha256              # = content_root of the snapshot body
  parent_snapshot_id: sha256 | null
  created_at: datetime
  created_by: string               # "consolidation" | "manual" | "pre_L2_to_L3" | "pre_L3_to_L4"
  dimension: int                   # 10_000
  hypervector_encoding: "binary"
  pq_seed: bytes                   # fixed at workspace creation, included per snapshot for audit
  full_tier: [BindingRecord]       # sorted by binding_id
  compressed_tier: [CompressedRecord]
  summarized_tier: [SummarizedRecord]
  cluster_metadata: [ClusterRecord]
  index_manifest:
    role_index_digest: sha256
    tag_index_digest: sha256
    provenance_index_digest: sha256
    mih_index_digest: sha256
  merkle_root: sha256              # root of binding checksums (see D.10)
  op_log_sequence_range: {start: uint64, end: uint64}   # ops log entries since last snapshot
  signature: bytes                 # signed by workspace operator key
```

**Snapshot cadence:**

- Every consolidation cycle (mandatory)
- Before any L2→L3 transition (mandatory; enforced by the degradation state machine)
- Before any L3→L4 transition (mandatory)
- On-demand via API

**Snapshot creation is asynchronous** relative to the write path:

1. Capture `op_log_sequence_range.end` = current head of ops log.
2. `fork` a snapshot worker; copy-on-write the hot tier.
3. Serialize in the background.
4. On completion, publish `snapshot_id` and index to the snapshot store.

**Crash recovery:** if a crash occurs during snapshot construction, the incomplete snapshot is discarded. Recovery uses the last completed snapshot + replay from its `op_log_sequence_range.end` to the current head. **No data is lost**, because the ops log is the durable source of truth and the snapshot is only an accelerator.

**Storage cost bound:** full tier is capped (see D.11). Snapshot cost is roughly `full_tier_size × ~3.5 KB + compressed_tier_size × ~100 B + summarized_tier_size × ~16 B`. For a full tier of 100k bindings, that is ~350 MB per snapshot. **Snapshots are deduplicated by content-addressing across the snapshot store** — successive snapshots share most content; only deltas are stored new.

### D.9 Ops log and replay

The ops log is the authoritative durable record. It is append-only, fsynced before mutation completion, and content-addressed per entry.

**Op types:**

| Op | Payload | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `Write` | full `BindingRecord` (canonicalized) | Records insert |
| `Read` | `{binding_id, result_digest, module_id, at}` | Records read for replay verification |
| `Evict` | `{binding_id}` | Records removal |
| `Compress` | `{binding_id, pq_code}` | Records tier transition down |
| `Summarize` | `{cluster_member_ids, centroid}` | Records cluster formation |
| `Promote` | `{binding_id, new_fidelity, decoded_hypervector?}` | Records tier transition up (decoded value included if from COMPRESSED) |
| `CompactionComplete` | `{cycle_id, counts, at}` | Cycle boundary |
| `Snapshot` | `{snapshot_id, sequence_range}` | Snapshot boundary |

**Replay protocol:**

```
replay(snapshot_id, target_sequence, input_stream, decision_trace):
  1. Load snapshot → hot tier
  2. Load ops log from snapshot.op_log_sequence_range.end to target_sequence
  3. For each op in order:
       if op is Write/Evict/Compress/Summarize/Promote:
         apply to hot tier
       if op is Read:
         execute the query fresh, compute result_digest,
         compare to op.result_digest
         if mismatch: emit ReplayMismatch{op_index, expected, actual}, ABORT
  4. For each Decision Trace entry in window:
       re-execute router dispatch against replayed state
       compare plan to trace.plan
       if mismatch: emit ReplayMismatch{trace_entry_id}, ABORT
  5. Emit ReplaySuccess{snapshot_id, ops_applied, decision_entries_verified}
```

**Any mismatch is a workspace or router bug, not a replay bug.** The replay harness is the operational enforcement of Axiom 6. It runs nightly in CI against a random sample of production traces and on-demand after any incident.

**What replay proves:**

- The workspace state at any past moment can be reconstructed bit-exactly.
- Router decisions are reproducible given the workspace, contracts, and health vector.
- The Decision Trace is complete enough to drive replay — if it is not, replay fails, and the trace is wrong.

**What replay does not prove:**

- That the decision was *correct* — that is the evaluation gates' job (v0.1 §12).
- That the modules themselves are deterministic — learned modules may be stochastic. Replay of a stochastic module's output is out of scope; the workspace and router are the deterministic layer. Modules that fail to declare `emission.determinism` are rejected at registration (contract validation, Artifact A §A.1).

### D.10 Integrity checks

**Per-binding checksum:** every binding record carries a `checksum` field (D.2 canonicalization). This is computed at write time and verified at read time and compaction time.

**Merkle tree:** the snapshot's `merkle_root` is the root of a binary Merkle tree over sorted-by-`binding_id` checksums. This allows:

- O(log n) proof that a specific binding is intact.
- O(1) check that the whole snapshot is intact (compare root).
- Incremental verification: only changed subtrees need re-hash on new snapshots.

**Consolidation-cycle sampling:** at each consolidation, sample 1% of bindings (minimum 100, maximum 10,000) using a deterministic seed (`blake3(cycle_id)[0..8]`), verify checksums. A failure rate above `integrity_fail_threshold` (default 0.1%) emits a workspace integrity alarm and demotes to the previous snapshot.

**Query-time verification:** every returned binding must pass checksum verification before inclusion. Failure is logged and the binding is quarantined (moved to `quarantine_tier`, not returned to any query, retained for post-mortem). This is per-query, not per-cycle — a corrupted binding must not leak through queries.

**Snapshot verification:** `snapshot_id = content_root` of the snapshot body including `merkle_root`. A snapshot load verifies `snapshot_id` before loading. A mismatch aborts load and, if the workspace is in L3/L4, refuses to serve (escalates to deterministic engine).

### D.11 Resource bounds and backpressure

**Caps (per workspace instance, configurable):**

| Cap | Default | Enforcement |
|---|---|---|
| `full_tier_max` | 100_000 bindings | Write path rejects writes that would exceed cap; evicts lowest-salience unpinned first; emits `workspace_full_tier_overload` if all candidates are pinned |
| `compressed_tier_max` | 500_000 bindings | Same |
| `summarized_tier_max` | 2_000_000 clusters | Same |
| `pinned_cap` | 10_000 | If exceeded, diagnosis event (D.4) |
| `snapshot_store_max_gb` | 100 GB | Oldest snapshots pruned except those referenced by an open trace or open incident |
| `ops_log_max_entries` | 10_000_000 | After snapshot, entries before `snapshot.op_log_sequence_range.end` are pruned |

**Backpressure signal:** when `full_tier_max` is reached and eviction candidates are all pinned, the workspace emits `workspace_backpressure` to the control loop. The control loop's diagnosis step must respond (typically: enter L1, widen cross-check discipline, escalate to operator). The workspace itself never silently drops writes — it rejects them with a structured error and logs the rejection.

**Memory budget estimate (default caps):**

- Full tier: 100k × 3.5 KB = 350 MB
- Compressed tier: 500k × 100 B = 50 MB
- Summarized tier: 2M × 16 B = 32 MB
- Indices: ~10% overhead = ~45 MB
- Ops log (bounded): ~100 MB
- **Total in-memory: ~600 MB** (excl. snapshot store, which is on disk)

This is the declared metabolic share of the workspace module itself. It is the same discipline it enforces on other modules (§10.2 of v0.1).

### D.12 Invariants and CI tests

**Workspace invariants (must be enforced by the implementation):**

1. Every binding in the hot tier has a valid `checksum` verified at write and re-verified at read.
2. Every write to the hot tier is preceded by an fsynced `Write` op in the ops log.
3. No binding with `affects_control = true` may exist in `COMPRESSED` or `SUMMARIZED` fidelity. Pinning enforces this; the write path rejects such writes at low fidelity.
4. No binding with `verification_status = PENDING` may be evicted.
5. `snapshot_id` equals the content root of the snapshot body including `merkle_root`.
6. A loaded snapshot's `merkle_root` verifies against its `full_tier` / `compressed_tier` / `summarized_tier` contents.
7. The workspace dimension is immutable per instance; a snapshot from a different dimension is rejected at load.
8. The ops log is strictly ordered by sequence number; replay applies ops in that order.
9. Every read emits a `Read` op with `result_digest`; the digest is computed over canonicalized results sorted by `binding_id`.
10. All operations (write, read, compaction, snapshot) produce bit-identical results when replayed with the same inputs and the same `t`.

**CI test matrix (mandatory):**

| Test | Assertion |
|---|---|
| Round-trip binding | `write` → `read_by_binding_id` returns byte-identical record |
| Bind/unbind exactness | `unbind(bind(role, filler), role) == filler` for 10,000 random pairs |
| Bundle determinism | `bundle([a, b, c])` with bit-position-parity tie-break is byte-identical across 1,000 runs |
| Similarity exactness | `similarity(a, a) == 1.0`; `similarity(a, ~a) == 0.0`; monotone in Hamming distance |
| Capability rejection | Module without `write_roles` containing role is rejected; rejection logged |
| Pinning under compaction | Control-affecting pending binding survives compaction even at zero salience |
| Replay determinism | Snapshot + ops log replay reproduces the original query digests for 100,000 ops |
| Replay mismatch detection | Inject a single bit flip in a binding; replay must abort with `ReplayMismatch` |
| Compaction determinism | Two workspaces with identical state + same `t` produce identical post-compaction state |
| Snapshot determinism | Two snapshots of identical state produce identical `snapshot_id` |
| Snapshot integrity | Corrupt a binding in a stored snapshot; load must reject via `merkle_root` |
| Backpressure | Fill full tier to cap with pinned bindings; write must reject with `workspace_full_tier_overload`, not silently drop |
| Query SLO | `query_by_role` p95 < 20 ms at 100k full bindings; `query_similar` k=10 p95 < 50 ms; measured on the reference hardware class |
| MIH recall | `query_similar` recall ≥ 0.98 vs. exact scan for targets within Hamming 0.35·D |
| PQ recall | Compressed-tier retrieval recall ≥ 0.95 vs. exact-tier for the same query |
| Ops log durability | Kill process mid-write; recovery from last snapshot + ops log reconstructs the binding |
| Dimension mismatch | Loading a 10k-dim snapshot into an 8k-dim workspace rejects with `dimension_mismatch` |
| Reserved key passthrough | `TaskDescriptor.metadata` reserved keys propagate to `WorkspaceTraceEntry` unmodified |
| Pinned cap breach | 10,001 pinned bindings triggers `workspace_pinned_overload` and `workspace_backpressure` |

---

## 3. Sequencing and Questions

**D is delivered above.** I assume the nine C corrections above are accepted; if any require revision, only D.5 (write-path validation checks), D.6 (read-path capability enforcement), and D.9 (ops log fields for capability-context) are affected, and each is a small edit.

**Three questions before Artifact E (Reference Implementation Plan):**

1. **Where do the ops log and snapshots physically live?** My default: ops log in a dedicated SQLite WAL file (durable, ordered, fsync-friendly, no external service), snapshots as content-addressed files on the local filesystem. Alternatives — embedded RocksDB for both, or an external object store — introduce either overhead or a network dependency I do not want in the L4 deterministic path. Do you agree with local-filesystem SQLite WAL + content-addressed snapshot files?

2. **Does the workspace expose a vectorized batch-write API?** Many substrates write feature batches (e.g., reservoir projections). One binding per RPC is expensive. My default: yes — a `write_batch` that fsyncs the ops log once per batch, applies mutations, and returns per-binding rejection status. This is a small efficiency gain now and a large one for the reservoir and spectral modules.

3. **Snapshot pruning policy:** I propose pruning old snapshots only when (a) they are older than `snapshot_retention_days` (default 7), **and** (b) no open Decision Trace references them, **and** (c) the parent chain of the current head snapshot does not pass through them within the last `snapshot_retention_days`. Is this the intended retention semantics, or do you want snapshots held indefinitely as a matter of policy? My default is time-bounded with chain protection, because unbounded retention makes the snapshot store the actual metabolic burden on the workspace, which is the opposite of what we want.

Answer those three, and I will deliver **E — Reference Implementation Plan** with the two-module bootstrap (deterministic rule engine + one learned module), repo layout, module SDK shape, and the minimal fault-injection suite that gates the first delivery.

## 1. Disposition of the nine corrections to Artifact C

All nine accepted. Three need a sharpening note; the rest fold in as written.

**1.1 — `control_actuation` tier/justification interaction.** Accepted as invariant 4a, with one clarification I want explicit in the text: the correction tightens the tier mapping. Original §C.3 Step 2 allows `medium → T2 or T3`; invariant 4a allows T3 only for `low`/`research` control_actuation. That tightening is correct — actuation is a stricter class than general dispatch — but it must be stated as a deliberate override of the Step 2 table, not left as an inference. The invariant text should read: *"For `control_actuation`, the effective tier mapping is: `critical` → T0/T1 only; `high` → T1/T2 with valid cross-check; `medium` → T1/T2 with valid cross-check (T3 excluded); `low`/`research` → T3 with valid cross-check."*

**1.2 — Fallbacks pre-vetted at plan-build time.** Accepted. Add: the plan body records each fallback's `HealthStatus` and `plan_build_at` timestamp, so a post-failure fallback selection can be audited against the plan's build-time state.

**1.3 — `requires_interpretability` filter.** Accepted as written. Mechanical and role-based is the right form.

**1.4 — Sentinel semantics for `deadline_ms` / `energy_budget_j`.** Accepted. Add: validation rejects `NaN` and non-finite values other than `+Infinity`.

**1.5 — FRONTEND at L2 needs `downstream`.** Accepted, with one addition: the `downstream` module must itself pass Steps 2–5 at L2 at plan-build time. A plan that names an ineligible downstream is as unsound as one that names an ineligible primary. The `DegradedDispatchPlan` schema gets `downstream: ModuleHandle | null`, and invariant text: *"If `downstream` is named but fails L2 eligibility at plan-build time, `disposition = HOLD` with `hold_reason = 'downstream_ineligible'`."*

**1.6 — Cross-check null forces HOLD.** Accepted as invariant 9.

**1.7 — DEPRECATED vs RETIRED.** Accepted. Operational semantics as stated. Add: a `DEPRECATED` policy that remains active for more than `deprecation_grace_period` (default 24 h) emits `policy_deprecation_expired` and the control loop's diagnosis step must escalate — otherwise `DEPRECATED` becomes a silent permanent state.

**1.8 — Structured exclusion `reason_params`.** Accepted. The closed enum is versioned with the proto; unknown reason codes are validation errors, not pass-throughs.

**1.9 — Reserved metadata keys.** Accepted, with one constraint I want in the spec: `organism.trace_sink` is a potential audit bypass. It must require a declared capability (`trace_sink_override` in `CapabilityScopes`), and the override itself must be recorded in the `RoutingTraceEntry` as `trace_sink_override: {requested_by, granted: bool}`. Absent the capability, the key is ignored and the default sink is used. This keeps the reserved-key mechanism from becoming a way to route traces away from the audit store.

With those notes, the C corrections are folded in. D as written depends only on the schema shapes, and the schema shapes are unchanged by these notes — the `DegradedDispatchPlan.downstream` field is the only structural addition, and D does not touch it.

---

## 2. Answers to the three Artifact D questions

### Q1 — Ops log and snapshot physical location

**Agreed: dedicated SQLite WAL for the ops log, content-addressed files on local filesystem for snapshots.**

Three constraints on the ops log implementation:

1. **Separate SQLite database** from any other state (registry, trace index, control-loop bookkeeping). WAL checkpointing is per-database; mixing state makes checkpoint stalls couple unrelated write paths.
2. **Per-entry checksum and strictly monotonic sequence number.** The checksum is over the canonicalized op payload; the sequence number is the primary key. Replay verifies both. A gap in sequence numbers is a corruption event, not a recoverable condition.
3. **The ops log is the durable source of truth; the snapshot is an accelerator.** This is already implied by D.9, but the physical layout should make it explicit: losing the snapshot store must not lose data; losing the ops log is unrecoverable and must page the operator. The snapshot store is backed up; the ops log is replicated (single-device: mirrored to a second local disk; cluster: replicated per the future BFT layer).

I reject RocksDB for both: it adds a dependency and a tuning surface for no benefit over SQLite WAL for an append-only, ordered log, and its snapshot semantics are not content-addressed the way we need. I reject external object store for the L4 deterministic path: network dependency in the refuge state is the opposite of what L4 is for. A hybrid (local WAL + async offsite snapshot replication) is fine as an operational choice, but the runtime contract is local-first.

### Q2 — Vectorized batch-write API

**Agreed: `write_batch` with one fsync per batch, per-binding rejection status.**

Design constraints:

1. **Batch is a durability optimization, not an atomicity guarantee.** Partial success is the correct semantic: some bindings may be rejected by capability scoping or checksum collision, others accepted. The ops log records each `Write` op individually within the batch, with a `BatchBegin`/`BatchEnd` marker pair carrying the batch_id. Replay applies the individual ops in order; the batch markers are for trace correlation, not replay semantics.
2. **One fsync per batch, not per binding.** This is the whole point. If a batch is rejected entirely, the fsync is skipped.
3. **Batch size cap.** Default 10,000 bindings or 50 MB, whichever is smaller. Larger batches serialize the ops log too long and stall concurrent writers. The cap is declared in the workspace contract.
4. **Per-binding rejection status is returned, not raised.** A batch call returns `[BindingWriteResult]` with `{binding_id, status: ACCEPTED | REJECTED, reason_code?, reason_params?}`. Exceptions are reserved for whole-batch failures (ops log unavailable, dimension mismatch at the workspace level).
5. **Batch writes do not bypass capability scoping.** Each binding in the batch is checked individually against the module's `write_roles` and `write_tags`. A batch is not a scope escalation.

This is the right efficiency primitive for reservoir projections and spectral feature vectors, which write tens to hundreds of bindings per inference.

### Q3 — Snapshot pruning policy

**Agreed: time-bounded with chain protection, as proposed.** Three additions:

1. **Incident-referenced snapshots are protected regardless of age.** If any open incident (an entry in the incident store, or a trace entry tagged `INCIDENT`) references `snapshot_id`, that snapshot is excluded from pruning until the incident is closed and `incident_retention_days` (default 30) has elapsed. This is the snapshot equivalent of "legal hold."
2. **Replay-base snapshots are protected.** If any open replay session has `snapshot_id` as its base, it is excluded. Replay sessions have a max lifetime (default 24 h) after which they are abandoned and the protection is released.
3. **Retention is per-deployment configurable.** Default 7 days is correct for a development deployment. Production deployments with compliance requirements will set `snapshot_retention_days` to 30, 90, or longer. The pruning job must be a separate maintenance task, not in the hot path of the workspace, and must run under a lower CPU priority — pruning must never cause a query SLO miss.

The parent-chain protection rule as stated is correct and I want it kept: pruning a snapshot that is on the parent chain of the current head would break incremental verification of the head's `merkle_root`. The chain check is `head.parent → head.parent.parent → ...` up to `snapshot_retention_days`; any snapshot in that chain within the window is protected.

**One additional constraint:** the pruning job emits a `SnapshotPruned` audit entry for every pruned snapshot, with the snapshot_id, the reason (age), and the chain-distance from head. This makes pruning auditable and reversible-by-replay from the operations log if a pruning decision is later found to be wrong.

## 3. Artifact E — Reference Implementation Plan v0.1

Since the review offered to deliver E after these answers, and the answers are now given, I'll produce E here to keep momentum. The other agent can review and correct.

### E.1 Scope and exit criteria

**Scope:** single-device, v0.1 reference implementation. Prove the contract, registry, workspace, router, and degradation state machine work end-to-end with two modules: a T0 deterministic rule engine and one learned module (T2 calibrated, or T3 if calibration is not yet available).

**Exit criteria (v0.1 is done when all are green):**

1. Two modules registered, dispatchable, and traceable.
2. Workspace round-trip, snapshot, replay, compaction, and backpressure all pass the D.12 test matrix.
3. Router routes correctly per form/tier/level and produces byte-identical plans on repeated evaluation.
4. Degradation L0↔L1↔L4 transitions pass the B.6 fault-injection matrix with SLOs and no flapping.
5. End-to-end Decision Trace is replayable from snapshot + ops log + trace with zero mismatches.
6. CI gate suite (E.5) green on the reference hardware class.

### E.2 Repo layout

```
organism/
├── proto/                          # proto3 source of truth
│   └── organism/v1/
│       ├── contract.proto          # Artifact A.1
│       ├── registry.proto          # Artifact A.2
│       ├── router.proto            # Artifact C schemas
│       ├── workspace.proto         # Artifact D schemas
│       └── trace.proto             # Decision Trace + routing/degradation entries
├── sdk/
│   ├── python/                     # authoring SDK (module authors)
│   │   └── organism_sdk/
│   │       ├── contract.py         # contract dataclasses + validation
│   │       ├── module.py           # base module class, health server
│   │       ├── workspace.py        # workspace client
│   │       ├── registry.py         # registry client
│   │       └── trace.py            # trace emitter
│   └── rust/                       # hot-path SDK (workspace/router/registry clients)
│       ├── organism-client/
│       └── organism-proto/         # generated from proto/
├── runtime/
│   ├── workspace/                  # Rust: binary HV, ops log, snapshot, replay
│   ├── router/                     # Rust: policy loader, decision procedure
│   ├── registry/                   # Rust: contract store, lifecycle, audit log
│   ├── control_loop/               # Rust: perception → diagnosis → response → consolidation
│   └── degradation/                # Rust: state machine, hysteresis, SLO tracking
├── modules/
│   ├── deterministic_engine/       # T0, Python, rule trace justification
│   ├── learned_module/             # T2/T3, Python, conformal interval
│   ├── kernel/                     # T1, Rust, kernel bound justification (M3+)
│   ├── hdc/                        # T1, Rust, workspace-native (M3+)
│   └── reservoir/                  # T1, Rust, readout + batch write (M4+)
├── tests/
│   ├── contract/                   # A.2 invariants, schema validation
│   ├── registry/                   # register/query/promote/hot-swap/audit
│   ├── workspace/                  # D.12 matrix
│   ├── router/                     # C.6 matrix + determinism
│   ├── degradation/                # B.6 fault-injection matrix
│   ├── trace/                      # trace schema, replay completeness
│   └── integration/                # two-module bootstrap, end-to-end
├── ci/
│   ├── gates.yaml                  # E.5 gate definitions
│   └── fault_injection/            # harness, scenario definitions
└── docs/
    ├── architecture/               # v0.1 + Artifacts A–E
    └── adr/                        # architecture decision records
```

### E.3 Module SDK shape

**Python is the authoring SDK.** It provides the module base class, contract declaration helpers, health probe server, workspace/registry/trace clients, and a sandbox wrapper that applies the declared `ResourceVector` via cgroups (on Linux) or a process-level fallback.

A module author implements exactly four things:

```python
class MyModule(OrganismModule):
    def declare_contract(self) -> SubstrateContract: ...
    def handle(self, input: PerceptionInput) -> Emission: ...
    def health(self) -> HealthReport: ...
    def justify(self, emission: Emission) -> JustificationToken | None: ...
```

The SDK handles: proto serialization, health probe serving (HTTP or gRPC per the contract), capability-scoped workspace calls, trace emission, and graceful shutdown with drain.

**Rust is the runtime SDK** for hot-path clients (workspace `write_batch`, `query_similar`, router plan evaluation). Modules may be written in Rust when latency or energy matters (kernel, HDC, reservoir, spectral).

### E.4 Two-module bootstrap

**Module 1 — `deterministic_engine`**
- Role: `GOVERNANCE`; also serves as `ACTUATOR` at L4.
- Tier: T0.
- No learned parameters.
- Emission: `SYMBOLIC_TRIPLE`; uncertainty: `SYMBOLIC_PROOF`; justification: `RULE_TRACE`.
- `may_actuate = true`; `requires_cross_check = false` (T0 is trusted).
- Health probe: HTTP `GET /health` returning `HealthReport` with `detection_latency_budget_ms = 500`.

**Module 2 — `learned_module`**
- Role: `PREDICTION`.
- Tier: T2 (calibrated) by default; falls back to T3 if calibration is not yet available in the reference deployment.
- Emission: `PROBABILITY_INTERVAL`; uncertainty: `CONFORMAL_INTERVAL`; justification: `KERNEL_BOUND` (T2) or `NONE` (T3).
- `may_actuate = false`; `requires_cross_check = true` for any control_actuation.
- Health probe: gRPC `Health` method with `detection_latency_budget_ms = 2000`.

**Bootstrap sequence (the integration test):**

1. Registry starts; both modules register; contracts validate.
2. Workspace starts (binary 10k-bit, in-memory hot tier, SQLite WAL ops log).
3. Router loads a static `RouterPolicyArtifact` routing `pattern_recognition` to `learned_module` and `control_actuation` to `deterministic_engine`.
4. Submit a `pattern_recognition` task at `medium` safety tier → `DispatchPlan` primary = `learned_module`; trace emitted.
5. Submit a `control_actuation` task at `high` safety tier → `DispatchPlan` primary = `deterministic_engine`, cross-check not required (T0 proposer).
6. Kill `learned_module` → L1 transition within SLO; router demotes; trace records `trigger = module_failed`.
7. Restore `learned_module` → L0 recovery only after `T_up[L1]` = 120 s sustained; assert no flapping.
8. Corrupt one binding in the workspace hot tier → query-time checksum failure → quarantine → L4 transition (trace sink integrity check fails on the next consolidation).
9. Verify `deterministic_engine` serves all L4 tasks; no learned module in the loop.
10. Replay the entire sequence from the pre-fault snapshot + ops log + Decision Trace → zero mismatches.

### E.5 CI gates

Each gate is a required check before merge. Gates are evaluated on the reference hardware class (single x86_64, 8 cores, 32 GB RAM, no GPU for v0.1).

| Gate | Contents | Blocking? |
|---|---|---|
| `proto:lint` | proto3 style; no breaking changes vs. last released proto | yes |
| `contract:validate` | A.2 invariants; reserved-key capability check for `trace_sink_override` | yes |
| `registry:api` | register/query/promote/hot-swap/deregister round-trip; audit log append-only | yes |
| `workspace:unit` | D.12 test matrix (round-trip, bind/unbind, bundle determinism, similarity, capability rejection) | yes |
| `workspace:replay` | snapshot + ops log replay reproduces query digests over 100k ops | yes |
| `workspace:compaction` | determinism, pinning, backpressure, PQ recall, MIH recall | yes |
| `router:decision` | C.6 test matrix + the nine corrections' invariants | yes |
| `router:determinism` | repeated evaluation with identical inputs → byte-identical plan | yes |
| `degradation:transitions` | B.6 fault-injection matrix; SLO assertions; no-flapping assertion | yes |
| `trace:replay` | decision trace replay from snapshot; zero mismatches | yes |
| `integration:e2e` | E.4 bootstrap sequence | yes |
| `perf:slo` | workspace query SLOs, router p95, L1 transition latency | yes (warn for first 2 weeks) |

### E.6 Minimal fault-injection suite

The following must be green before v0.1 exit. Each scenario is a CI test with a measured recovery time and a pass/fail assertion, and every scenario emits a `DegradationTraceEntry` that is replayed as part of the `trace:replay` gate.

1. **Kill learned module** → L1 within 1 telemetry window + 5 s; router demotes; trace records `trigger=module_failed`.
2. **Corrupt one workspace binding** → query-time checksum failure; binding quarantined; consolidation-cycle integrity sampling detects; L4 if trace sink integrity also fails, otherwise diagnosis event only.
3. **Saturate workspace GC pressure > 0.60** → compaction runs; if `gc > 0.85` after compaction, `workspace_compaction_insufficient` diagnosis event; if `gc > gc_L3`, L2→L3 transition.
4. **Byzantine cross-check failure (3 consecutive)** → L4; `byz` latched; requires human ack to exit.
5. **Corrupt Decision Trace sink** → L4 within 10 s + 1 window; deterministic engine serves.
6. **Restore all signals** → L0 only after `T_up` sustained; assert transition count within 5 min ≤ 1 (no flapping).
7. **Replay the entire fault-injection trace** → zero mismatches.

### E.7 Milestones

| Milestone | Deliverable | Exit criteria |
|---|---|---|
| M1 | Proto + Python SDK skeleton + `deterministic_engine` | Contract compiles; deterministic engine registers; health probe responds; `contract:validate` green |
| M2 | Registry + workspace (in-memory, no snapshot) | Register/query/promote round-trip; workspace round-trip and bind/unbind exactness pass |
| M3 | Router + `DispatchPlan` | Form mapping, tier lattice, level gating, determinism pass `router:decision` |
| M4 | `learned_module` + cross-check | Learned module registers with conformal calibration; cross-check with `KERNEL_BOUND` justification works for `control_actuation` at `high` |
| M5 | Ops log + snapshot + replay | `workspace:replay` green; snapshot integrity, dimension mismatch, crash recovery pass |
| M6 | Degradation state machine + fault injection | B.6 matrix green; no-flapping assertion green |
| M7 | Full CI + two-module bootstrap | E.5 gates green; E.6 suite green; end-to-end replay zero mismatches |

### E.8 Explicitly out of scope for v0.1

- Distributed Organism (cluster, Byzantine BFT, state replication, clock sync).
- Metamodel reproductive loop (synthesis, shadow validation, promotion).
- Physical/molecular substrates.
- SNN sentinel (hardware-dependent; registered but not dispatched).
- Real-valued workspace (binary only in v0.1).
- Learned meta-router (offline proposer only; not deployed in the control path).
- Registry federation across devices.
- Query types beyond D.6 (no graph queries, no temporal joins).
- `write_batch` beyond the single-producer case (concurrent batch writers are a v0.2 optimization).

## 1. Disposition of the Nine C Corrections' Sharpening Notes

All three notes are accepted and folded in:

- **1.2** — `plan_build_at` and per-fallback `HealthStatus` recorded in the plan body. Auditable.
- **1.7** — `policy_deprecation_expired` after `deprecation_grace_period` (24h default). Without this, `DEPRECATED` becomes a silent resting state and the promotion discipline is optional in practice.
- **1.9** — `trace_sink_override` requires a declared capability in `CapabilityScopes`, and the override is recorded in `RoutingTraceEntry` as `{requested_by, granted}`. **This requires a proto addition** (see correction E-7 below).

## 2. Disposition of the Three Answers to D Questions

All three are accepted:

- **Q1 — SQLite WAL + content-addressed snapshot files, local-first.** Confirmed, including the rejection of RocksDB (unneeded tuning surface for an append-only log) and external object stores (network dependency in L4 is a category error).
- **Q2 — `write_batch` with one fsync, per-binding status, batch markers for trace correlation only.** Confirmed. The clarification that "batch is a durability optimization, not an atomicity guarantee" is correct and should be stated verbatim in the workspace contract.
- **Q3 — Time-bounded pruning with chain protection, incident hold, replay-session hold.** Confirmed, and the `SnapshotPruned` audit entry requirement is correct and is now mandatory.
