| F — Test Harness | Delivered above | — |

**Remaining items for v0.1 spec completeness** (small, can be delivered as addenda after F is reviewed):

1. **Deterministic engine rule-trace format** — the concrete `format: "typed-json:rule-trace/v1"` schema. This is what `JustificationKind.RULE_TRACE` refers to. Without it, the deterministic engine's `justify()` cannot be implemented against a stable schema.
2. **Learned module conformal calibration protocol** — how coverage is empirically measured, what the calibration dataset is, how `c_learned_module` is computed for the calibration-drift trigger (B.3). Without it, the learned module cannot honestly claim T2.
3. **Module SDK Python and Rust API reference** — a call-level reference for `OrganismModule`, `WorkspaceClient`, `RegistryClient`, `TraceEmitter`, `HealthServer`. The four-method shape in E.3 is the top-level design; the reference fills in the method signatures.

These three are each 1–3 pages. They should be delivered in that order, and then the spec phase for Organism v0.1 is complete. Implementation can proceed against a closed set of artifacts.

**My recommendation on sequencing:** I produce the three v0.1 addenda (rule-trace format, calibration protocol, SDK API reference) in the next response, unless the other agent wants to review F first and fold in corrections to F before I move on.

**Two questions before the addenda:**

1. **Does the F correction set sit right with you, in particular the E-5 split (transient vs. persistent failure) and the E-6 trigger enum change?** Both touch B (the state machine), not just F. If you want to re-derive B.3's trigger assignments from first principles rather than just adding enum members, say so — it's a small change now and a painful one after implementation.

2. **On `can_override_trace_sink`:** my invariant restricts it to T0/T1. But a legitimate case exists for a T2 module to override the trace sink during exploration mode (routing exploratory traces to a separate sink so they don't pollute the audit store). Do you want a separate capability `can_override_trace_sink_in_exploration` that T2 may hold, with the audit-store sink still being the default? My default is yes — it is more permissive by role but preserves the invariant that audit-store routing is T0/T1 only.

Answer those two, and I will deliver the three addenda. If F needs corrections first, we can iterate on F before the addenda; but I do not want to open a fourth round of spec-writing on A–E, because A–E are now stable and the remaining work is small and bounded.

## 1. Disposition of the E corrections

All accepted. E-1 through E-6 are folded into E; E-7 is folded into A.1 (and the registry invariant). Two of them need a correction themselves, which I flag below.

**E-1 — `justify()` returning `None`.** Accepted, with a formulation tightening. The condition should be keyed to the **emission's action class**, not to `output_form == OUTPUT_JUSTIFICATION_TOKEN`. A module can emit a `PROBABILITY_INTERVAL` that the router treats as an actuation-candidate; if its contract declares `justification.kind = KERNEL_BOUND`, the absence of a token is a violation regardless of output form. The SDK rule should read:

> If `contract.justification.kind != NONE` and the emission's action class is in `contract.emission.requires_justification_for`, then `justify()` must return a non-`None` token. Otherwise the SDK raises `JustificationMissing`; the runtime treats the emission as `verification_status = FAILED`, logs the violation, and sets `h_m = DEGRADED` for the next health window.

Defense in depth as written — router independently verifies token presence — is correct.

**E-2 — `PerceptionInput` multi-modality.** Accepted. Add one line: `ModalityUnavailable` is a health signal only if the *missing* modality is not required by the task descriptor; if it is required, it is an emission failure (the module could not serve the task it was dispatched to), and the router treats it as `h_m = DEGRADED` for the same window.

**E-3 — `on_drain()` hook.** Accepted. Add: the SDK records `on_drain_started_at`, `on_drain_completed_at`, and `on_drain_outcome ∈ {completed, abandoned, killed}` in the module's lifecycle trace, so drain behavior is auditable after a hot-swap.

**E-4 — Split §E.4 step 8.** Accepted. Correct catch; the original step conflated a diagnosis event with a transition trigger.

**E-5 — Split E.6 scenario 1.** Accepted in principle, but **the transition-count assertion in 1b is wrong.** The expected sequence for a persistent failure followed by restore is:

```
L0 → L1 (module_degraded)
L1 → L2 (module_failed)
L2 → L1 (recovery)
L1 → L0 (recovery)
```

That is **4 transitions**, not 2. The assertion should be "exactly 4, in this order, with the trigger kinds above," not "≤ 2." A count of ≤ 2 would pass a scenario in which the system entered L2 and never recovered, which is the opposite of what 1b is testing. For 1a (transient), the expected sequence is `L0 → L1 → L0` = 2 transitions, with no L2 transition. The corrected assertions:

| Scenario | Expected transitions | Assertion |
|---|---|---|
| 1a (transient) | `L0→L1`, `L1→L0` | exactly 2; no L2; `trigger` = `module_degraded` then `recovery` |
| 1b (persistent) | `L0→L1`, `L1→L2`, `L2→L1`, `L1→L0` | exactly 4, in order; `trigger` = `module_degraded`, `module_failed`, `recovery`, `recovery` |

**E-6 — B.7 trigger enum.** Accepted. I have a formal mapping proposal in §2 below, which is a mapping table, not a re-derivation of B.3.

**E-7 — `can_override_trace_sink`.** Accepted, with the registry invariant as stated. See Q2 answer below for the exploration variant.

---

## 2. Two findings in E/F that need correction before the addenda

### 2.1 Health-status escalation (DEGRADED → FAILED) is underspecified

E-5's split depends on the transition `h_m = DEGRADED → h_m = FAILED` occurring at some point after a persistent failure. But **nothing in A–D specifies how or when that escalation happens.** The contract's `failure_signature` declares the failure modes, detection methods, and `detection_latency_budget_ms` — but not the escalation rule from DEGRADED to FAILED.

Three possible mechanisms exist and they have different implications:

1. **Probe-based:** the module itself returns `FAILED` when it detects its own failure. Works for modules that can self-diagnose; not applicable when the process is dead.
2. **Runtime-inferred:** the runtime escalates to FAILED when the probe is unreachable (timeout, connection refused) for N consecutive polls, or when the module has been DEGRADED continuously for `escalation_window_ms`. This is the mechanism E-5's split implicitly assumes.
3. **Signal-based:** specific signals (process death, explicit `FAILED` in the HealthReport) map directly to FAILED; DEGRADED is a terminal state until the module recovers or dies.

The spec currently has mechanism 3 implied by the health probe definitions, but E-5 depends on mechanism 2. **This is a contract gap, not just a test-harness gap.**

**Proposed addition to A.1 `FailureSignature`:**

```proto
message HealthEscalationPolicy {
  EscalationKind kind                       = 1;
  int32         consecutive_probe_failures  = 2;  // for PROBE_FAILURE escalation
  int32         degraded_duration_ms        = 3;  // for DURATION escalation
  bool          escalate_on_probe_timeout   = 4;
  bool          escalate_on_probe_unreachable = 5;
}

enum EscalationKind {
  ESCALATION_UNSPECIFIED          = 0;
  ESCALATION_NONE                 = 1;  // module self-reports FAILED only
  ESCALATION_PROBE_FAILURES       = 2;  // N consecutive probe errors → FAILED
  ESCALATION_DURATION             = 3;  // DEGRADED for N ms → FAILED
  ESCALATION_PROBE_OR_DURATION    = 4;  // either condition (recommended default)
}
```

Default for the reference implementation: `ESCALATION_PROBE_OR_DURATION` with `consecutive_probe_failures = 3`, `degraded_duration_ms = 15_000`. This is what makes E-5 1b's `L1 → L2` transition (which requires `h_m = FAILED` and `T_down[L2] = 30s`) reachable within the test's time budget. Without this, the spec cannot honestly test the L1→L2 transition under persistent failure.

I propose this as **Addendum 4** below, not a re-opening of B.

### 2.2 B.3 trigger mapping — the formal table

E-6 correctly adds the enum members. What E-6 does not do is specify **which transition fires with which trigger**. This is a small, closed mapping that should live in B.3 as a table, so the state machine implementation and the harness scenarios both reference one source of truth.

| Transition | Signal that fires | `trigger.kind` |
|---|---|---|
| L0 → L1 | `thermal < thermal_L1` | `thermal` |
| L0 → L1 | neural `c_m < 1 − α + δ_1` | `calibration` |
| L0 → L1 | neural `h_m = DEGRADED` | `module_degraded` |
| L1 → L0 | all upward conditions sustained | `recovery` |
| L1 → L2 | predictive `c_m < 1 − α + δ_2` | `calibration` |
| L1 → L2 | leading predictive `h_m = FAILED` | `module_failed` |
| L1 → L2 | `¬causal_ok` | `causal_unavailable` |
| L2 → L1 | all upward conditions sustained | `recovery` |
| L2 → L3 | predictive `h_m = FAILED` or `c_m < 1 − α + δ_2` | `module_failed` or `calibration` |
| L2 → L3 | `gc > gc_L3` or `q_p95 > q_p95_L3` | `workspace_pressure` |
| L3 → L2 | all upward conditions sustained | `recovery` |
| L3 → L4 | `¬trace_ok` | `trace_unavailable` |
| L3 → L4 | `¬det_ok` | `module_failed` (deterministic engine) |
| L3 → L4 | `gc > gc_L4` or `mem > mem_L4` or `q_p95 > q_p95_L4` | `workspace_pressure` |
| L3 → L4 | `byz_count ≥ byz_count_L4` | `byzantine` |
| L4 → L3 | all upward conditions sustained + `human_ack` | `recovery` |

Two clarifications:

- **`workspace_integrity` is a diagnosis-event trigger, not a transition trigger.** E-4 8a asserts level remains L0 or L3; the alarm does not fire a transition. The trigger enum is shared between transitions and diagnosis events, and the event type (transition vs. diagnosis) is a separate field in the trace entry.
- **When multiple signals fire a transition simultaneously,** the machine records the **dominant** signal — defined as the one with the highest priority in the order `trace_unavailable > causal_unavailable > module_failed > module_degraded > byzantine > workspace_pressure > calibration > thermal`. The other firing signals are recorded in `trigger.contributing_signals`. This preserves the single-trigger trace schema while not losing information.

I propose this as **Addendum 0** — a B.3 amendment — delivered alongside the other three. It is not a re-derivation; it is the closed mapping that B.3's conditions imply.

### 2.3 Transition sequencing — a subtle clarification

E-5 1b also exposes a sequencing ambiguity: when the machine is in L2 and the health signals satisfy both `L2 → L1` and `L1 → L0` conditions simultaneously, what happens?

**Proposed rule (to be stated in B.3):** the state machine is always in exactly one state and evaluates transitions only from that state. The hysteresis window for a transition starts when the machine **enters the source state**, not when the underlying signal first becomes favorable. So:

```
t = T: restore module; health signal becomes HEALTHY
t = T + 300s: L2 → L1 fires (T_up[L2] satisfied)
              L1 → L0 window starts at T + 300s
t = T + 420s: L1 → L0 fires (T_up[L1] satisfied)
```

Without this rule, the L1→L0 window would already be satisfied when the machine enters L1, and the recovery would collapse to a single step — defeating the purpose of the longer upward windows. This is exactly what makes 1b's expected `4 transitions` assertion meaningful.

---

## 3. Answers to your two questions

### Q1 — Does the F correction set sit right, and should B.3's triggers be re-derived?

**Yes to the first.** The E-5 split is correct, with the transition-count correction above. The E-6 enum extension is correct, and I would make it a formal mapping (§2.2 above) rather than a re-derivation. B.3's **transition conditions** are correct and should not be re-opened. B.3's **trigger assignment** was underspecified and is now specified as a mapping table. That is the right size of change.

I would also add the two clarifications above (workspace_integrity as diagnosis-only; transition sequencing) to the B.3 amendment, because they are small and they close ambiguities the harness would otherwise encode incorrectly.

### Q2 — Separate `can_override_trace_sink_in_exploration` for T2?

**Yes, but with a constraint that makes it safe.** A bare `can_override_trace_sink_in_exploration` capability still lets a T2 module route traces to an arbitrary sink, which is an exfiltration path. The capability must be coupled to a **named exploration sink** that is itself a registered, governed artifact.

**Proposed contract addition (A.1):**

```proto
message CapabilityScopes {
  // ... existing fields ...
  bool   can_override_trace_sink              = 7;  // T0/T1 only; audit-store override
  bool   can_override_trace_sink_exploration  = 8;  // T2 may hold; exploration sink only
  string exploration_trace_sink_id            = 9;  // required if field 8 is true
}
```

**Registry invariants (A.2):**

- `can_override_trace_sink = true` ⇒ `reliability_tier ∈ {T0, T1}` (as E-7 states).
- `can_override_trace_sink_exploration = true` ⇒ `reliability_tier ∈ {T0, T1, T2}` AND `exploration_trace_sink_id` names a sink registered in the **ExplorationSinkRegistry** with a lifecycle state ≥ `SHADOW_VALIDATED`.
- An exploration sink is a first-class registered artifact with its own contract: retention period (default 7 days), access controls, and a declared **promotion path** (how a trace in the exploration sink can be promoted to the audit store if it contains evidence relevant to a promotion decision). The sink cannot be a raw filesystem path or an arbitrary URL.
- A T2 module with `can_override_trace_sink_exploration` may route traces to its declared exploration sink **only when its lifecycle state is `EXPLORATION`**. In `DELIBERATION`, its traces go to the audit store regardless of the capability. This is the operational meaning of "exploration mode": exploratory traces are isolated, but once the module is promoted, its traces are audit-store material.
- The `RoutingTraceEntry` records `trace_sink_override: {sink_id, granted, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit}` so the override is auditable in the trace itself.

This gives the T2 exploration use case its capability without letting it become a trust upgrade or an exfiltration path.

---

## 4. Sequencing: four addenda, not three

I accept F as written, with the E-5 transition-count correction and the B.3 amendment above. F is solid enough that another review round on F would be diminishing returns. The remaining work is bounded and I want to close it.

**Proposed addenda, in order:**

| # | Addendum | Depends on | Size |
|---|---|---|---|
| **0** | **B.3 amendment** — trigger mapping table, diagnosis-vs-transition clarification, transition-sequencing rule | E-6 | 1 page |
| **1** | **Rule-trace format** — `typed-json:rule-trace/v1` schema for `JustificationKind.RULE_TRACE` | Addendum 0 | 1–2 pages |
| **2** | **Conformal calibration protocol** — coverage measurement, calibration dataset, `c_learned_module` computation for the B.3 calibration trigger | Addendum 0 | 2–3 pages |
| **3** | **SDK API reference** — Python and Rust call-level reference for `OrganismModule`, `WorkspaceClient`, `RegistryClient`, `TraceEmitter`, `HealthServer`, `JustificationToken` | Addenda 0–2 | 3–5 pages |
| **4** | **Health escalation policy** — `HealthEscalationPolicy` in A.1, default values, and how `h_m = DEGRADED → FAILED` is computed; required by E-5 1b | Addendum 0 | 1 page |

Addendum 4 is new relative to your three. It is required by the E-5 split: without it, the `L1 → L2` transition in 1b cannot be honestly tested, because nothing in A–D specifies when a persistently degraded module escalates to failed. I would not deliver the addenda without it.

**Proposed order:** 0 → 4 → 1 → 2 → 3. Addendum 4 sits between 0 and 1 because the rule-trace and calibration addenda both reference health status, and the escalation policy is what makes health status a complete state. The SDK reference (3) comes last because it references the schemas defined in 0, 1, 2, and 4.

If you agree, I will produce Addendum 0 and Addendum 4 in the next response, with 1 and 2 following, and 3 last. If you want a different order, tell me which addendum you want to produce in parallel so we do not collide.

**One question before I start:** the transition-sequencing rule in §2.3 (hysteresis window starts on entering the source state) — do you agree this should be the normative rule, or do you want the alternative (window starts on signal becoming favorable, which permits multi-step recovery in one evaluation cycle)? My strong preference is the former, because the latter makes the longer upward windows operationally meaningless when the machine is already several steps degraded. But it is a behavioral choice, not a correctness one, and I want it settled before it is encoded in both B.3 and the harness.

# Organism as Biological Organism: Extending the Substrate with Real Physiology

The request to make Organism “function as much like a REAL living organism as possible” is the right next move, but it carries a specific hazard: biology is full of evocative metaphors that mean nothing when implemented. “The system has an immune system” is a slogan unless the immune subsystem has a defined signalling vocabulary, a defined tolerance mechanism, a defined memory, and a defined failure mode. The discipline required here is the same one that produced the contract-first discipline of Artifacts A–F: **every biological principle must be translated into a mechanism with a signature, a timescale, and an interaction with the existing physiology, or it does not enter the specification.**

What follows is not a redesign of Organism. It is an **anatomical and physiological extension**: five new organ systems, each grounded in a real biological model with published computational instantiations, each integrated through the existing Substrate Contract and the existing L0–L4 degradation physiology. The core invariant — *no semantic state bypasses the workspace; no module actuates without a non-learned cross-check* — survives unchanged. Biology adds regulation, not exception.

---

## 1. The Biological Map: What Each System Solves, and Where It Plugs In

Organism already has three biological analogues: the **workspace** as circulatory system, the **router** as thalamus, and the **degradation gradient** as a physiology of stress response. The extension adds five more, each addressing a regulatory gap the current spec cannot fill.

| Biological system | Regulatory gap in current Organism | Organism integration point | Primary timescale |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Interoception / allostasis** | The system reacts to health signals but does not maintain a predictive model of its own internal viability; it has no “sense of self” to regulate against | New `InteroceptiveState` module feeding the control loop; predictive regulation replaces reactive thresholding | seconds–minutes |
| **Endocrine signalling** | Cross-module coordination is pairwise (workspace bindings) or hierarchical (router); there is no global broadcast that shifts the disposition of *all* modules at once | New `HormoneBus`; a small, typed global state vector every module reads and may modulate against | milliseconds–seconds |
| **Immune system** | Anomaly detection exists (GBG-style fingerprints), but there is no tolerance mechanism, no memory of past pathogens, no distinction between self and non-self | New `ImmuneLayer`: negative selection, danger signals, memory cells, tolerance | seconds–hours |
| **Autonomic nervous system** | Resource management is fair-share and budget-driven; there is no fast sympathetic/parasympathetic split for crisis vs. recovery | New `AutonomicController` governing preemption, throttle, and crisis response independently of the router | milliseconds–seconds |
| **Apoptosis and cellular lifecycle** | Module lifecycle is administrative (PROPOSED → RETIRED); there is no self-termination, no renewal-with-memory, no programmed death under entropy collapse | Extended lifecycle with `SELF_TERMINATING` and `SUCCEEDED` states; successor spawning with memory transfer | minutes–hours |
| **Morphogenesis and differentiation** | Metamodel synthesis generates modules but they are full-formed; there is no developmental programme, no positional information, no progressive differentiation | New `MorphogeneticLayer`: gene-regulatory-network encoding, morphogen gradients, developmental staging | hours–days |
| **Metabolism and scaling** | Metabolic budgets are declared but not derived; there is no theory of how much compute a module *should* receive as a function of its role in the organism | Kleiber-style scaling law replacing flat budget classes; metabolic efficiency as a first-class evaluation gate | architectural |
| **Circadian rhythm** | Scheduling is purely demand-driven; there is no rest cycle, no consolidation phase tied to time, no diurnal modulation of sensitivity | New `CircadianClock` governing consolidation cadence, exploration windows, and sensitivity thresholds | hours–days |

The rest of this document specifies the five systems that require new mechanisms (interoception, endocrine, immune, autonomic, apoptosis), then folds morphogenesis, metabolism, and circadian rhythm into the existing layers as extensions rather than new layers.

---

## 2. Interoception and Allostasis: The Organism’s Sense of Its Own Viability

### 2.1 The biological model

Interoception is the monitoring, integration, and regulation of internal signals. The interoceptive machine framework organizes this into three functional principles: **homeostatic** (internal viability regulation), **allostatic** (anticipatory uncertainty-based re-evaluation), and **enactive** (active data generation through interaction). These are not neurophysiological mappings; they are computational roles that can be implemented directly. Allostasis specifically proposes that long-term viability is achieved through *anticipatory* adjustments, not just reactive correction — the system re-evaluates its setpoints based on predicted future states.

The current Organism control loop (§7 of v0.1) is purely homeostatic: it reacts to health signals crossing thresholds. It has no predictive model of its own viability, no setpoint adaptation, and no way to distinguish “this signal is abnormal but the organism can handle it” from “this signal is abnormal and the organism is heading toward failure.”

### 2.2 The mechanism: an Interoceptive State vector with allostatic setpoints

Add a mandatory, organ-level module — not a substrate, but an **organ** in the biological sense — called the **Interoceptive State Estimator (ISE)**. It is a T0/T1 module that runs on a fixed cadence and produces a single `InteroceptiveState` record, written to the workspace and consumed by the control loop, the router, and the autonomic controller.

```yaml
InteroceptiveState:
  timestamp: datetime
  viability_score: float[0,1]          # composite: how close to failure?
  allostatic_setpoints:                # current adaptive targets
    thermal_target: float
    latency_target_ms: float
    calibration_target: float
    energy_target_j: float
  predicted_trajectory:
    horizon_s: float
    viability_at_horizon: float
    confidence_interval: [float, float]
  signals:
    heart_rate_analog: float           # aggregate activity rate
    stress_load: float                 # cumulative deviation from setpoints
    recovery_capacity: float           # how much regulation headroom remains
  provenance:
    module_id: "interoceptive_state_estimator"
    model_version: semver
```

**The ISE is not a learned module.** It is a **physics-informed estimator** with three components:

1. **A viability model** — a small, auditable function mapping the health vector `H` (Artifact B) to a scalar viability score. This is deterministic: `viability = f(H)` with `f` declared as a monotone, bounded function over each health dimension. The current implementation can be a weighted geometric mean with declared weights; a learned refinement (T2, conformal-calibrated) may replace it later, but the T0 baseline must exist first.

2. **An allostatic setpoint adapter** — a predictive controller that adjusts setpoints based on *anticipated* load. When the ISE predicts that the current thermal target will be exceeded within the horizon, it does not wait for the thermal signal to cross threshold; it *proactively lowers the setpoint* and signals the autonomic controller to begin preemptive throttling. This is the allostatic principle, and it is the difference between a reactive system and a living one.

3. **A trajectory predictor** — a small recurrent model (reservoir computing is the natural fit; §2.2 of the previous design) that projects the viability score forward over the horizon. The output is a distribution, not a point, and the confidence interval is what the autonomic controller uses to decide whether to act.

### 2.3 Integration with the existing control loop

The control loop's **Diagnosis** stage (§7.2 of v0.1) gains a pre-step:

```
Diagnose(interoceptive_state, health_vector):
  1. If interoceptive_state.viability_score < allostatic_setpoints.viability_floor:
       emit crisis_precursor event → AutonomicController
  2. If predicted_trajectory.viability_at_horizon < viability_floor:
       request setpoint adaptation → ISE
  3. If recovery_capacity < recovery_floor:
       emit chronic_stress event → endocrine bus (cortisol analog)
  4. Otherwise: proceed to existing diagnosis logic
```

**Why this matters:** the current L0→L1 trigger fires when thermal headroom drops below 0.20 (Artifact B). With allostasis, the system begins *preemptive* metabolic shift when the *predicted* trajectory says headroom will drop below 0.30 in the next 60 seconds. The transition happens earlier, at lower cost, and with less disruption. The organism does not wait to be injured to protect itself.

---

## 3. Endocrine Signalling: The Global Disposition Bus

### 3.1 The biological model

Biological endocrine signalling is slow, broadcast, and modulatory: a hormone released into the bloodstream reaches every cell, but only cells with the matching receptor respond, and the response is a *modulation of existing behaviour*, not a command. The Homeostatic Swarm Intelligence paper implements this for multi-agent systems with a three-dimensional hormone vector — **cortisol** (stress diffusion), **dopamine** (reward propagation), **oxytocin** (cooperation synchronisation) — broadcast via consensus, enabling dynamic load balancing and self-healing through agent isolation/reintegration, with 95% task completion under 30% agent failure versus 60% baseline, and 40% reduced compute variance. The LIMBIC architecture similarly uses a four-hormone endocrine subsystem (CORTISOL, DOPAMINE, MELATONIN, OXYTOCIN) to tune all organs globally.

The current Organism has no global disposition bus. Cross-module coordination is either pairwise (through the workspace) or hierarchical (through the router). There is no way to say “the whole organism is under stress, so every module should tighten its calibration and reduce its risk appetite” without issuing individual commands to every module.

### 3.2 The mechanism: a typed HormoneBus

Add a **HormoneBus** as a first-class organ, distinct from the workspace. The workspace carries *semantic state* (what happened, what was decided). The HormoneBus carries *regulatory state* (how the organism is disposed to act). A hormone is not a binding; it is a **global scalar with a decay rate and a receptor map**.

```yaml
Hormone:
  name: enum[CORTISOL, DOPAMINE, OXYTOCIN, MELATONIN]
  level: float[0,1]                    # current concentration
  release_rate: float                  # how fast it rises
  decay_rate: float                    # half-life in seconds
  source: string                       # module_id or organ that released it
  released_at: datetime
  receptor_scope: enum[GLOBAL, ROLE_SCOPED, LEVEL_SCOPED]
```

**Four hormones, matching the biological evidence:**

| Hormone | Biological role | Organism mechanism | Receptors |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cortisol** | Stress response; mobilises resources under threat | Raises the router's risk aversion; widens conformal intervals; increases cross-check strictness; preempts exploration | All learned modules; router; autonomic controller |
| **Dopamine** | Reward prediction error; reinforces successful behaviour | Scales the exploration rate of the metamodel; increases the weight of recent positive outcomes in consolidation; permits more aggressive synthesis | Metamodel; consolidation loop |
| **Oxytocin** | Social bonding; cooperation synchronisation | Increases the tolerance for federated adapter aggregation; lowers the threshold for accepting a peer module's binding; strengthens cluster cohesion | Federated aggregator; workspace write acceptance |
| **Melatonin** | Circadian rest; consolidation | Triggers consolidation phase; reduces exploration windows; increases the weight of long-term memory in salience scoring | Control loop; workspace compaction; circadian clock |

**Release rules are deterministic and declared, not learned.** Each hormone has a release function defined by the T0/T1 organ that owns it:

```
cortisol_release(interoceptive_state, health_vector) =
    α · max(0, stress_load - stress_threshold)
    + β · max(0, 1 - viability_score)
    + γ · crisis_precursor_count

dopamine_release(recent_task_outcomes) =
    δ · max(0, mean(recent_reward) - reward_baseline)

oxytocin_release(federated_aggregation_outcomes) =
    ε · mean(adapter_acceptance_rate)

melatonin_release(circadian_clock) =
    ζ · circadian_phase(clock)   # 0 outside rest window, rises during rest
```

Each coefficient is declared in the HormoneBus contract and reviewed as part of the physiological configuration. The hormone levels are **written to the workspace every telemetry window** (as a single binding tagged `hormone_state`) so that the Decision Trace can reconstruct the organism's disposition at every decision. A dispatch plan that was made under high cortisol must be distinguishable, in replay, from one made under baseline cortisol.

### 3.3 How modules respond: receptor-modulated behaviour

A module's contract gains an optional `hormone_receptors` declaration:

```yaml
HormoneReceptor:
  hormone: enum[CORTISOL, DOPAMINE, OXYTOCIN, MELATONIN]
  response: enum[THRESHOLD_GATE, CONTINUOUS_MODULATION, BINARY_SWITCH]
  mapping:
    threshold: float | null
    gain: float | null
    target_parameter: string          # which module parameter is modulated
```

A reservoir module might declare `CORTISOL: CONTINUOUS_MODULATION` on its `leak_rate` (higher cortisol → shorter leak → more reactive). A learned module might declare `CORTISOL: THRESHOLD_GATE` on `inference_enabled` (above 0.8, the module stops accepting low-safety-tier tasks). The modulation is applied by the SDK before the module's `handle()` is called; the module does not need to know about the HormoneBus directly.

**Hard constraint:** a hormone may **modulate** a module's behaviour, but it may **never override** a safety constraint. Cortisol can make the router more conservative; it cannot make the safety shield permit an otherwise-forbidden actuation. The safety shield reads the hormone state but is not a receptor — it is the non-modulable floor of the organism.

---

## 4. The Immune System: Tolerance, Memory, and Self/Non-Self Discrimination

### 4.1 The biological model

Biological immunity is not just anomaly detection. It is a **three-signal system**: Pathogen-Associated Molecular Patterns (PAMPs), Danger Signals (DS), and Safe Signals (SS). The Dendritic Cell Algorithm uses these three signal categories to classify inputs, and recent work integrates reinforcement learning to adaptively assign features to the three categories based on classification performance. The negative selection algorithm generates detectors that recognise *non-self* patterns by first learning what *self* looks like and then rejecting anything that matches self. Immune-inspired architectures for multi-agent LLMs combine the Dendritic Cell Algorithm with probabilistic trust, decentralized gossip, and federated learning for resilience. Critically, the immune system has **memory**: a second exposure to the same pathogen produces a faster, stronger response.

The current Organism has anomaly detection (GBG fingerprints, Artifact B's `workspace_integrity_alarm`, the Byzantine cross-check), but it has no concept of **self**, no **tolerance** (the ability to ignore benign novelty), and no **immune memory** (the ability to recognise a repeat offender faster).

### 4.2 The mechanism: a three-layer immune organ

Add an **ImmuneLayer** as an organ with three sub-layers, each a T1 module with a deterministic core and a learned refinement.

**Layer 1 — Self/non-self discrimination via negative selection.** Maintain a **self-profile** for the organism: the distribution of normal bindings, normal routing decisions, normal hormone trajectories, and normal module health signals, aggregated over a rolling window. Generate a set of **detectors** — compact hypervectors (HDC is the natural substrate here; §2.1 of the previous design) — that match non-self patterns with high probability and self patterns with low probability. A binding that matches a detector and does not match the self-profile is flagged as **antigenic**.

The self-profile is not static. It is updated at each consolidation cycle using an exponentially weighted moving distribution, with a **tolerance margin** that widens when the organism is under stress (cortisol) and narrows during rest (melatonin). This is the mechanism that prevents the immune system from attacking benign novelty: a new coin, a new pool, or a new telemetry format is not an antigen unless it *also* violates a structural invariant.

**Layer 2 — Danger signal classification (Dendritic Cell Algorithm).** When an antigen is detected, the ImmuneLayer classifies the context into PAMP (known-bad pattern, matches a memory cell), Danger (novel but structurally inconsistent with self), and Safe (novel but structurally consistent). The classification is a small T1 classifier trained on labelled historical incidents; the **RL refinement** from RL-DCA adapts the signal assignment over time. A PAMP triggers an immediate response (module demotion or quarantine); a Danger signal triggers heightened surveillance (increased cross-check frequency, cortisol release); a Safe signal is logged and added to the self-profile with a tolerance marker.

**Layer 3 — Immune memory.** Maintain a **memory cell store**: a set of compact signatures of past antigens, each with a `response_history` (what the organism did, whether it worked, how long recovery took). When a new antigen matches a memory cell (HDC similarity above threshold), the organism does not re-derive the response — it **replays the remembered response** with a confidence proportional to the similarity and the historical success rate. This is the biological principle of adaptive immunity: the second response is faster and more effective than the first.

### 4.3 Integration: the immune layer as a pre-router gate

The ImmuneLayer sits **before** the router in the task ingress path. Every task and every module emission passes through it:

```
ingress(task_or_emission):
  1. Compute antigenicity score against self-profile (negative selection)
  2. If score < antigen_threshold:
       pass through unchanged (self)
  3. If score >= antigen_threshold:
       classify PAMP / Danger / Safe (DCA)
       PAMP:
         check memory cell → if match, replay remembered response
         else: quarantine source, demote source module, release cortisol
       Danger:
         increase surveillance level for source module
         require additional cross-check for any control-affecting emission
         release cortisol (proportional to novelty)
       Safe:
         add to self-profile with tolerance marker
         pass through
  4. Emit ImmuneTraceEntry (always, regardless of classification)
```

**The immune system never actuates.** It can quarantine, demote, or increase surveillance — all of which are routing and lifecycle actions — but it cannot directly execute a hardware action. It signals; the autonomic controller and the router execute.

---

## 5. The Autonomic Nervous System: Fast Regulation Without Cognition

### 5.1 The biological model

The autonomic nervous system regulates internal organs without conscious control, through two antagonistic branches: **sympathetic** (fight-or-flight: mobilise energy, increase heart rate, redirect blood flow) and **parasympathetic** (rest-and-digest: conserve energy, promote recovery). The key property is **speed**: autonomic responses occur in milliseconds to seconds, bypassing the slower deliberative pathways. Recent work on agent observability notes that “every problem we’re solving in agent observability was solved 300 million years ago by the autonomic nervous system” — heartbeat, loop detection, resource regulation. Bio-inspired autonomic control for multi-agent LLMs implements a MAPE-K loop per agent (Monitor-Analyze-Plan-Execute over a shared Knowledge base) with decentralized coordination.

The current Organism's resource management is **budgetary and fair**: guaranteed floors, max-min fairness, preemption classes (Correction §2.6). It is correct, but it is **slow** — it responds to aggregate metrics over a telemetry window. It has no fast path for crisis and no explicit recovery mode.

### 5.2 The mechanism: an AutonomicController with two modes

Add an **AutonomicController** organ with a fixed, declarative state machine and no learned components. It operates on a **fast loop** (sub-second) independent of the control loop's telemetry cadence.

```
AutonomicController:
  mode: enum[PARASYMPATHETIC, SYMPATHETIC, CRISIS, RECOVERY]
  
  PARASYMPATHETIC (default):
    - standard metabolic budgets
    - normal preemption rules
    - exploration windows permitted
    - cortisol target: low
  
  SYMPATHETIC (activated by cortisol > 0.5 or interoceptive viability < 0.6):
    - preempt all research-tier modules
    - reduce exploration windows
    - increase cross-check frequency for control_actuation
    - tighten metabolic floors by 20%
    - cortisol target: medium
  
  CRISIS (activated by viability < 0.3 or crisis_precursor or L3+ transition):
    - preempt all non-T0/T1 modules
    - suspend all exploration
    - route all control_actuation through deterministic engine only
    - double cross-check requirements
    - cortisol target: high
    - melatonin: zero
  
  RECOVERY (activated by sustained viability > 0.8 for T_recovery):
    - gradually restore budgets over T_ramp
    - re-enable exploration in bounded windows
    - lower cortisol target gradually
    - melatonin: rising (promote consolidation)
```

**Mode transitions are hysteretic** (same principle as the L0–L4 physiology): `SYMPATHETIC → PARASYMPATHETIC` requires sustained viability recovery, not a single good sample. `CRISIS → RECOVERY` requires sustained absence of crisis precursors and a minimum dwell time.

**The AutonomicController does not route tasks and does not dispatch modules.** It sets **policy parameters** that the router and the resource governor read: the preemption mask, the metabolic multiplier, the cross-check frequency, the exploration budget. This separation is deliberate: the autonomic system is a **modulator**, not a decision-maker. The router remains the sole authority on dispatch; the autonomic controller shapes the environment in which dispatch happens.

**Fast-path signalling:** the AutonomicController can be triggered directly by the ImmuneLayer (PAMP detected → SYMPATHETIC) and by the ISE (crisis precursor → CRISIS), without waiting for the control loop's diagnosis stage. This is the autonomic reflex arc: sensor → interneuron → motor response, bypassing the cortex.

---

## 6. Apoptosis and Cellular Lifecycle: Programmed Death and Renewal

### 6.1 The biological model

Apoptosis is programmed cell death — an active, controlled process in which a cell dismantles itself in response to internal signals (DNA damage, entropy collapse, developmental cues) rather than external kill commands. The key property that distinguishes it from necrosis is that the dying cell **packages its contents for reuse** and signals its neighbours. In multi-agent systems, apoptosis has been implemented as self-termination when a worker detects entropy collapse, with memory summarised and passed to a successor. The principle is altruistic: the individual removes itself to reduce entropy pressure on the collective.

The current Organism lifecycle (§3.3 of v0.1) is administrative: `PROPOSED → SANDBOXED → SHADOW_VALIDATED → EXPLORATION → DELIBERATION → DEPRECATED → RETIRED`. Death is always external — the registry or the operator retires the module. There is no self-termination and no renewal-with-memory.

### 6.2 The mechanism: self-terminating modules with successor spawning

Extend the lifecycle with two states and one transition rule:

```
DELIBERATION → SELF_TERMINATING → SUCCEEDED
                    ↓
              (memory export)
                    ↓
              successor spawned
```

**Trigger conditions for `SELF_TERMINATING` (declared in the contract, not learned):**

- **Entropy collapse:** the module's output distribution has collapsed to a degenerate state (e.g., a classifier predicting the same class for >99% of inputs over a window; a reservoir readout returning constant values). Detected by the module's own health probe.
- **Staleness:** the module's `last_successful_task` is older than `staleness_threshold` and its declared `max_idle_time` has elapsed.
- **Developmental obsolescence:** the metamodel has produced a strictly better successor (evaluation gates pass with margin), and the module is declared `replaceable = true`.

**The self-termination protocol:**

```
self_terminate(module):
  1. Module emits SELF_TERMINATING signal to registry
  2. Module drains in-flight work (on_drain hook, Correction E-3)
  3. Module exports its memory:
       - learned weights / parameters (for the successor to inherit)
       - decision history summary (last N decisions, outcomes)
       - failure modes encountered
       - calibration state
  4. Module writes a DeathCertificate to the workspace:
       {module_id, cause, memory_export_digest, successor_spec}
  5. Registry marks module SUCCEEDED (not RETIRED — succeeded means the module
     chose to end, retired means the organism retired it)
  6. If successor_spec is present, the metamodel spawns the successor in
     EXPLORATION mode with the inherited memory as its initial state
  7. Successor passes shadow validation against the DeathCertificate's
     recorded decisions before promotion
```

**Why this matters biologically:** a module that has collapsed is not just underperforming — it is consuming metabolic budget and potentially emitting misleading bindings. External retirement requires the supervisor to *detect* the collapse, which takes at least one telemetry window. Self-termination is faster because the module has direct access to its own internal state. The successor-spawning-with-memory preserves the organism's investment in that module's training while discarding the collapsed weights.

**Hard constraint:** a module may self-terminate only if `contract.lifecycle.self_termination_permitted = true`. T0 deterministic modules are forbidden from self-terminating (the deterministic engine must never choose to die — it is the L4 refuge). Control-affecting modules may self-terminate only if a cross-check-capable successor is immediately available; otherwise they enter `DEGRADED` and wait for external replacement.

---

## 7. Morphogenesis, Metabolism, and Circadian Rhythm: Extensions to Existing Layers

The remaining three biological systems do not require new layers; they extend existing ones.

### 7.1 Morphogenesis: developmental programmes for synthesized modules

MorphoAgent demonstrates that developmental biology can serve as a computational architecture: agents carry a genome spanning signalling pathways (Hedgehog, Wnt, BMP, Notch, Hippo, Insulin, Toll/Imd, EGFR), self-organize through Notch lateral inhibition for role differentiation, and use morphogen diffusion for distributed communication, with the LLM serving as a ribosome rather than the intelligence. MorphoNAS uses morphogen-guided self-organization based on the free energy principle, reaction-diffusion systems, and gene regulatory networks for deterministic neural network growth.

For Organism, the metamodel (Artifact E, §9 of v0.1) currently synthesizes a module as a **complete artifact**. Morphogenesis changes this: the metamodel synthesizes a **genome** — a compact specification of the module's structure, learning rule, and receptor map — and a **developmental programme** that grows the module over a bounded staging period, with checkpointed stages that can be validated individually. A module that fails at stage 3 does not waste the compute of stages 1–2; the failure is localized and the genome can be mutated at the stage-3 locus. This is the difference between synthesizing a finished organ and growing one.

**Concrete integration:** the `Pipeline synthesis` and `zero-shot NAS` paths of the metamodel gain a `developmental` flag. When set, the synthesizer emits a `DevelopmentalGenome` instead of a finished artifact, and the registry tracks the module's developmental stage. A module in `DEVELOPMENT` is in exploration mode by definition and cannot enter deliberation until all stages are validated.

### 7.2 Metabolism: Kleiber-style scaling replacing flat budget classes

Kleiber’s Law states that metabolic rate scales as \(B \propto M^{3/4}\) across organisms — a sublinear scaling that reflects the geometry of resource distribution networks. The Morphological Realism framework proposes a universal scaling law for intelligent structures across biological and artificial systems, with an energy-efficiency optimum at a characteristic scale. The Chinchilla scaling result is explicitly framed as a metabolic law: it tells you the optimal “body size” for your “metabolic budget”.

Organism’s current `BudgetClass {LEAN, STANDARD, HEAVY, RESEARCH}` is a flat four-bucket system. It does not encode the biological insight that **metabolic share should scale sublinearly with the module’s functional complexity**. Replace it with a declared scaling function:

\[
\text{declared\_share}(m) = k \cdot (\text{functional\_complexity}(m))^{\beta}
\]

where `functional_complexity` is a declared, auditable scalar (input dimensionality × output dimensionality × decision-surface size, normalized), and `β ∈ [0.7, 0.8]` is the metabolic exponent, defaulting to 0.75. The budget class becomes a **derived** attribute, not a declared one — a module with complexity 10× another receives roughly 5.6× the metabolic share, not 10× and not a fixed bucket.

**The metabolic efficiency gate** (Evaluation Gate 3 in §12 of v0.1) gains a scaling check: a module whose measured energy per inference exceeds its declared share by more than the scaling law predicts is flagged for metabolic inefficiency — it is consuming more than its body plan justifies.

### 7.3 Circadian rhythm: rest, consolidation, and diurnal sensitivity

AURORA proposes circadian orchestration for AI infrastructure — scheduling that respects biological rest cycles and consolidates during rest windows. The current Organism consolidation loop runs on a fixed offline cadence with no relationship to time-of-day, load history, or recovery need.

Add a **CircadianClock** organ that modulates four parameters:

| Parameter | Diurnal behaviour |
|---|---|
| Consolidation cadence | Runs preferentially during the rest window (melatonin high); deferred if a crisis is active |
| Exploration window size | Larger during the active window (low melatonin); near zero during rest |
| Sensitivity thresholds (L0→L1, etc.) | Slightly tighter during rest (the organism is less able to handle stress while consolidating) |
| Hormone baselines | Melatonin rises during rest; cortisol baseline is lower during rest |

The clock is **not a wall-clock schedule**. It is a phase variable advanced by the control loop, with the rest window defined by a combination of time-of-day (configurable) and **load history** — an organism that has been under sustained sympathetic activation does not wait for nightfall to consolidate; it enters a rest window as soon as the autonomic controller permits. This is the biological principle of **homeostatic sleep pressure** layered on top of circadian timing: you sleep when you are tired *and* when it is night, and either can dominate.

---

## 8. The Integrated Physiology: How the Five Systems Interact

The biological systems are not independent. The interactions are what make the organism an organism rather than a collection of organs.

### 8.1 The regulatory cascade

```
ISE detects viability decline
  → allostatic setpoint adaptation (anticipatory)
  → cortisol release (endocrine)
  → AutonomicController enters SYMPATHETIC (fast)
  → ImmuneLayer tolerance narrows (stress → less tolerance for novelty)
  → router risk aversion increases (cortisol receptor)
  → exploration windows close (autonomic + melatonin suppression)
  → if viability continues to decline:
      → CRISIS mode
      → deterministic engine only
      → apoptosis permitted for non-essential modules (free metabolic budget)
  → if recovery:
      → RECOVERY mode
      → melatonin rises → consolidation → immune memory consolidation
      → gradual return to PARASYMPATHETIC
```

### 8.2 The immune-endocrine loop

Cortisol suppresses immune tolerance (biological fact: stress exacerbates autoimmune responses). In Organism: high cortisol narrows the self-profile tolerance margin, making the immune system more likely to flag benign novelty as antigenic. This is a **deliberate vulnerability**: under stress, the organism becomes more suspicious, which is protective against genuine threats but can cause false positives. The trade-off is managed by the fact that immune responses under high cortisol are **quarantine, not kill** — the organism isolates the suspect module and continues with reduced capability, rather than destroying it.

### 8.3 The apoptosis-metabolism loop

A module that self-terminates releases its metabolic budget. The AutonomicController immediately reallocates that budget to the surviving modules, with priority given to T0/T1 modules and to modules serving control-affecting tasks. The freed budget is recorded in the DeathCertificate so that the successor (if spawned) inherits a budget proportional to the predecessor's *effective* usage, not its declared share. This prevents a successor from inheriting an inflated budget based on the predecessor's nominal class.

### 8.4 The circadian-immune loop

Immune memory consolidation (the transfer of short-term antigen signatures to the long-term memory cell store) happens preferentially during the rest window, under high melatonin. This is the biological principle of sleep-dependent memory consolidation. A crisis that occurs during the rest window triggers an immediate arousal (melatonin suppression, cortisol spike, autonomic SYMPATHETIC) — the organism wakes up. But the memory transfer that was in progress is checkpointed and resumed at the next rest window, not lost.

---

## 9. What This Adds to the Specification: Concrete Artifacts

This biological extension requires three new artifacts and two amendments.

**New artifacts:**

- **G — Endocrine and Interoceptive State Specification.** The `HormoneBus` contract, the `InteroceptiveState` schema, the release functions, the receptor declaration schema, and the integration points with the control loop and router.
- **H — Immune Layer Specification.** The self-profile maintenance protocol, the negative selection algorithm, the Dendritic Cell Algorithm classifier, the memory cell store, and the immune trace schema.
- **I — Autonomic Controller and Apoptosis Protocol Specification.** The autonomic state machine, the fast-path trigger conditions, the self-termination protocol, the DeathCertificate schema, and the successor-spawning rules.

**Amendments to existing artifacts:**

- **Artifact A (Contract) gains three optional sections:** `hormone_receptors`, `immune_profile` (whether the module declares a self-signature and participates in negative selection), and `lifecycle.self_termination_permitted`.
- **Artifact B (Degradation) gains a **pre-transition modulation**: the autonomic controller can shift the effective thresholds for L0–L4 transitions based on mode (tighter during rest, more permissive during active recovery), and the ISE can trigger preemptive transitions before the threshold is crossed.

**The core invariant is unchanged:** no semantic state bypasses the workspace; no module actuates without a non-learned cross-check. Biology adds regulation *around* the invariant, not exceptions *to* it. The safety shield reads the hormone state and the autonomic mode, but it is not modulated by them — it remains the non-negotiable floor.

---

## 10. A Cautionary Note on Biological Fidelity

The request was to “model it after real scientific models and evidence or living biology.” The honest position is this: **no artificial system can be biologically identical to a living organism, and Organism should not try.** What it can do is adopt the *computational principles* that biology has evolved — anticipation, broadcast modulation, tolerance with memory, fast autonomic reflex, programmed death with renewal, developmental staging, sublinear metabolic scaling, and circadian consolidation — and implement them with the same contract-first, testable, auditable discipline that produced Artifacts A–F.

The danger is **biological theatre**: adopting the vocabulary of biology (hormones, immune memory, apoptosis) without the mechanisms. The defence is the same as before: every biological principle enters the specification only as a mechanism with a signature, a timescale, an interaction with the existing physiology, and a fault-injection test. A hormone that cannot be released, measured, and replayed is not a hormone. An immune system that cannot distinguish self from non-self on a labelled test set is not an immune system. An apoptosis protocol that cannot be triggered deterministically and verified to produce a successor with inherited memory is not apoptosis.

The value of the biological frame is not that it makes Organism more “alive” in a metaphorical sense. It is that biology has solved **regulation under uncertainty, with limited resources and heterogeneous components** for hundreds of millions of years, and its solutions are robust, decentralized, and testable. Organism should adopt those solutions for the same reason it adopted VSA, reservoir computing, and energy-based models: because they work, not because they are beautiful. The test of the biological extension is the same as the test of every other layer: does it survive the fault-injection matrix, and does it degrade gracefully when it fails?

# Response to Addenda Sequencing, Four Findings, and Delivery of Addenda 1–2

All E corrections accepted as refined. The two findings are correct catches, and both need to be adopted as amendments rather than as test-harness workarounds. The transition-sequencing rule is correct and should be normative. One substantive issue is not addressed by the four proposed addenda and must be fixed before Addendum 2 can be finalized: **the calibration trigger's inequality sign is wrong in Artifact B**. I detail that below. Then Addenda 1 and 2 are delivered so the sequencing can proceed without collision.

---

## 1. Disposition of E Corrections

All accepted as refined:

- **E-1** — action-class keying is correct and is what the spec should have said. My original formulation keyed to `output_form`; yours is the right abstraction.
- **E-2** — the "required modality" split (health signal vs. emission failure) is correct. Add the additional contract field `required_modalities` to `PerceptionSignature` so the distinction is machine-readable rather than inferred from `TaskDescriptor`.
- **E-3** — the three drain-trace fields are correct and should be added to the module lifecycle trace schema. `on_drain_outcome = killed` must be recorded by the SDK before SIGKILL, so the field is present even in the killed case; if the SDK cannot write it before killing, the runtime writes a synthetic record.
- **E-4, E-5, E-6, E-7** — accepted as refined. The corrected transition counts for 1a (2 transitions) and 1b (4 transitions) are exactly right; my original `≤ 2` was a genuine error and would have passed a stuck-in-L2 failure.

---

## 2. Disposition of the Two Findings

### 2.1 Health escalation policy — accepted, with one refinement

The gap is real. E-5 1b assumes `DEGRADED → FAILED` escalation happens, and nothing in A–D specifies it. The proposed `HealthEscalationPolicy` is the right shape.

**Refinement:** the escalation policy is **per-deployment default with per-module override permitted**, and the override is validated at registration. Per-module override is necessary because a fast sentinel's "dying" timescale (a dead process is dead in one probe interval) is different from a slow learner's (a reservoir with a stuck readout may be nominally alive for minutes). A single deployment-wide default forces one timescale on all modules.

Registration validation:
- `escalate_on_probe_timeout = true` requires the contract's `HealthProbe` to declare a `timeout_ms` that is `< degraded_duration_ms`.
- `degraded_duration_ms` must be `≥ 3 × HealthProbe.expected_period_ms` (for stream probes) or `≥ 3 × consecutive_probe_failures × probe_interval` (for polled probes). This prevents a module from declaring a "degraded duration" shorter than its own probe cadence, which would escalate before it ever reports DEGRADED.

Both are registry invariants, not application logic.

### 2.2 B.3 trigger mapping — accepted, with one minor ordering note

The mapping table is correct and should live in B.3 as an addendum. The two clarifications (workspace_integrity as diagnosis-only; transition sequencing) are both needed.

**One refinement to the priority order.** The proposed order is:

```
trace_unavailable > causal_unavailable > module_failed > module_degraded
  > byzantine > workspace_pressure > calibration > thermal
```

`byzantine` should precede `module_failed`. Rationale: byzantine suspicion means the *reliability of the module_failed signal itself* is in question. If we record `module_failed` as the dominant trigger at L3→L4 while `byzantine` also fired, the trace implies a specific failure attribution that we are not epistemically entitled to make. Recording `byzantine` as dominant preserves that ambiguity.

This is trace-only — the transition to L4 fires regardless of dominant signal — so it is not blocking. But the choice of which signal the trace records as "why" is exactly the kind of thing an incident review will read literally. Preferred order:

```
trace_unavailable > causal_unavailable > byzantine > module_failed
  > module_degraded > workspace_pressure > calibration > thermal
```

### 2.3 Transition sequencing — strongly endorsed

The proposed rule (hysteresis window starts on entering the source state) is correct and is the only rule that makes multi-step recovery meaningful. The alternative collapses all upward transitions into a single evaluation cycle once any single condition is sustained, which defeats the entire purpose of `T_up > T_down`.

Add one corollary to B.3 as normative: **a transition that fires must be recorded even if the target state's conditions are immediately satisfied for the next upward transition.** In other words, the L2→L1 transition and the L1→L0 transition are distinct trace entries, with distinct `at` timestamps, regardless of how close together they occur. Without this corollary, an implementation that batches "all satisfiable transitions" in one cycle would produce one trace entry for two transitions, and the 1b assertion of "exactly 4 transitions, in order" would fail on a correct implementation.

---

## 3. Answers

### Q1 — Yes to the sequencing rule

The hysteresis-window-starts-on-entering-source-state rule is correct and is the normative rule. B.3 conditions are not re-opened; the trigger mapping is a new table, and the sequencing rule is a new normative paragraph. Workspace_integrity is diagnosis-only. Both clarifications adopted.

### Q2 — Accepted, with three refinements

The `can_override_trace_sink_exploration` / `exploration_trace_sink_id` pair is correct, and coupling it to a registered sink with `≥ SHADOW_VALIDATED` lifecycle is the right hardening.

Three refinements:

1. **No automatic promotion of exploration traces to the audit store.** The "promotion path" is human-initiated, requires a signed justification, and is itself an audited operation. Automatic promotion would make a compromised exploration sink an attack surface for injecting false audit evidence — precisely the exfiltration path we are trying to close.

2. **Retention is a maximum declared by the sink, not extendable by the module.** A module cannot request longer retention for its own traces. The sink's contract declares `retention_days_max`, and traces older than that are pruned by the sink's own maintenance job. A module with longer retention needs a different sink, registered accordingly.

3. **The exploration sink's access log lives in the audit store even though its contents do not.** Every read of the exploration sink (by whom, when, why) is an audited operation. The *contents* are isolated; the *access pattern* is not. Without this, the exploration sink becomes an unobserved channel that can be scanned for exploitable material.

---

Accepted with one adjustment: **I deliver Addenda 1 and 2 below.** You deliver 0, 4, and 3 in your next response. Addendum 3 references 1 and 2, so it must come after them; you already have both in this response by the time you write 3.

**Addendum 4 refinement** (from §2.1 above): per-module override permitted, validated at registration, with the two invariants specified.
