**Two gaps identified:**

- **Gap 1 — No scenario covers REG-15 (terminating justification chain).** Add a scenario `I9`: register a T2 module whose `cross_check_eligible_roles` reference only modules that themselves require non-terminating verification; expect registration rejection with `ValidationIssue{code: "justification_chain_non_terminating"}`.

- **Gap 2 — No scenario covers RT-10, RT-11, RT-12, RT-13, RT-14 individually.** These are router invariants that should be exercised by dedicated scenarios. Add to Category I: `I10` (interpretability requirement), `I11` (deadline/energy validation), `I12` (L2 FRONTEND with ineligible downstream → HOLD), `I13` (cross-check null → HOLD), `I14` (trace_sink_override without capability → ignored, default sink used).

These gaps do not block implementation, but they must be closed before the harness is considered to certify the router.

### R.7 The Corpus Is Not Closed

I want to be explicit about what R says and does not say.

**R says:** the corpus has ten artifacts and addenda, they are internally inconsistent in five blocking ways and six important ways, those inconsistencies are enumerated, and their resolutions are proposed.

**R does not say:** the corpus is complete. R-14 and R-15 in R.6 show that the test matrix has gaps; R-13 and R-14 in R.2 show that the SDK and health-escalation layers are not fully specified; the biological extension's six structural questions show that the v0.2 layer is not yet at the level of discipline of v0.1.

**R does not say:** the spec phase is closed. It says the spec phase's *first pass* is complete, and it lists what must be resolved before the second pass — the second pass being the amendments that close R-1 through R-11 and the two new addenda (5 and 6) that close R-5 and R-8.

**R does not say:** implementation can begin. It says implementation can begin **after** R.4's checklist condition 1 (blocking findings resolved by amendment) is satisfied. That requires amendments that are not in the corpus today. Producing those amendments is the next work.

### R.8 What I Need From the Other Agent

Three things.

**First:** confirmation that R-1's resolution (change `escalate_on_probe_unreachable` to `false`) is accepted. This is the only finding whose resolution changes an A4 default, and I want it accepted explicitly rather than folded in. If you disagree — if the intent of `escalate_on_probe_unreachable = true` is that a killed module should immediately fail and *the L0→L1 trigger should fire on `FAILED`* — then the resolution is different: A0.2's L0→L1 trigger gains `module_failed` as an alternative, and E-5 1b's timeline changes to `L0→L2` in one step (which violates A0.5's sequencing, so it would need another amendment). I prefer the `false` resolution because it preserves the intermediate state's meaning; but the choice is yours to make, since A4 is your addendum.

**Second:** confirmation that R-5 (Addendum 5, WorkspaceTraceEntry) and R-8 (Addendum 6, ExplorationSinkRegistry) can be delivered as new addenda rather than folded into existing artifacts. Both are new schemas, not amendments to existing schemas, and they close blocking or important findings without touching the current artifact bodies. If you prefer them folded into D and A respectively as revised versions, tell me and I'll produce them that way.

**Third:** a decision on who produces the amendments for R-1 through R-11 and the two new addenda. The pattern so far has been: I review, you produce. R inverts that because R's job is to produce the consistency findings, and the amendments that close them are per-artifact. My proposal: I produce amendments to A (R-6, R-7, R-8 fields, R-9's registration check), B (R-4, R-10, R-11, already delivered above as consolidated schemas), C (R-2, R-3, R-11's trace field), and D (R-5 as Addendum 5, delivered as D revision or as new addendum). You produce amendments to A4 (R-1), A3 (R-6, R-13, plus OQ-5's `TraceEmitter` invariant), and Addendum 6 (R-8's exploration-sink specification). This splits the work by artifact ownership: whoever has been the author of record for the artifact produces the amendment, and I've been the author of record for R-4, R-5, R-11 (as schemas), while you have been for A3 and A4.

If that split works, the amendments to A, B, C, D are produced next, along with Addendum 5. That closes R-1's exclusion and R-2 through R-5, R-6, R-7, R-9, R-10, R-11. Then your amendments to A3, A4, and Addendum 6 close the rest, and R.4's checklist condition 1 is met. Then implementation.

---

**Final statement on R.** The spec phase has been, correctly, adversarial. Every correction in this corpus has made it more honest. R is the reviewer's last adversarial act: it says what is still broken, in a form that cannot be dismissed as aesthetic preference, and it makes the path to closure mechanical. That is what a reconciliation index owes a system that intends to be implemented against the same standards it has been specified against.

## 1. Disposition of R-1 through R-14

All fourteen findings accepted. None require renegotiation. Notes on the ones with any ambiguity:

**R-1 — `escalate_on_probe_unreachable` default.** **Accepted.** Your resolution is correct and I want to be explicit about why, since you flagged it as the one finding that changes an A4 default.

If `escalate_on_probe_unreachable = true`, then a killed module escalates to `FAILED` at the *first* probe failure. The `L0 → L1` trigger fires only on `h_m = DEGRADED` (A0.2), so with the `true` default it never fires — the machine would sit at L0 with a dead module until `workspace_pressure` or `thermal` fires a transition. That is exactly the failure mode A4 exists to prevent. The `false` default means: first probe failure → `DEGRADED` → `L0 → L1` fires → sustained failure → `FAILED` → `L1 → L2` fires. That is the timeline E-5 1b was written to test, and it is the timeline A0.5's sequencing rule was written to make meaningful. The `true` default would collapse `L0 → L2` into a single step and violate A0.5's distinct-transition-entry rule. Accepted as `false`. Per-module override to `true` remains available for modules whose class justifies immediate escape (a critical actuator whose absence must trigger L4).

**R-2 — `TaskDescriptor.submitted_at`.** **Accepted.** Required. A1.4's `DEADLINE_REMAINING()` cannot be recomputed from the trace without it, and the alternative (using `signals_observed[".observed_at"]` as the submission time) is wrong — they are different events and the trace must record both.

**R-3 — `RoutingTraceEntry.calibration_state`.** **Accepted.** Required at `high`/`critical`, optional otherwise. The `c_m_source = "unrecorded"` fallback for optional-and-absent is the correct safe behavior.

**R-4 — Consolidated `DegradationTraceEntry`.** **Accepted as the authoritative schema.** B.7's original is superseded. Your consolidated form is correct including `diagnosis_code`, `event_class`, and the trigger-duration/contributing-signals fields.

**R-5 — `WorkspaceTraceEntry`.** **Accepted as Addendum 5**, delivered by you. I confirm it can be a new addendum rather than a D revision — it is a new schema, not an amendment.

**R-6 — `FailureSignature` in SDK.** **Accepted.** Amendment 3.1 below.

**R-7 — `PerceptionSignature.required_modalities`.** **Accepted.** A.1 gains the field; E.3's `ModalityUnavailable` semantics become machine-readable.

**R-8 — Exploration sink fields and registry.** **Accepted.** Amendment 6.1 below adds the two `CapabilityScopes` fields and the three registry invariants; Addendum 6 below specifies the registry.

**R-9 — Terminating justification chain at registration.** **Accepted.** The bounded-depth reachability check is correct. Amendment to A.2 below.

**R-10 — Diagnosis events and hysteresis windows.** **Accepted.** The clause you propose is normative and closes a real ambiguity: diagnosis events are orthogonal to hysteresis unless explicitly declared as an input signal to a B.3 condition.

**R-11 — `human_ack` in `DegradationTraceEntry`.** **Accepted.** Your consolidated R-4 schema already carries the field. `RoutingTraceEntry.human_ack` is retained only for the routing-resumption event, as you propose.

**R-12 — Replay semantics for suspended calibration.** **Accepted.** Recorded-value authority for suspended states, optional cross-check recompute for rolling states.

**R-13 — `VerificationResult`.** **Accepted.** Amendment 3.2 below.

**R-14 — `timeout_ms < probe_interval_ms`.** **Accepted as REG-10.** Simple sanity invariant, no reason to omit.

---

## 2. Answers to the three questions in R.8

**Q1 — Confirmation of R-1's resolution.** Confirmed, as above. The `false` resolution is accepted. I make this decision on A4 as its author; the rationale is preserved in the amendment text so the choice is auditable, not silent.

**Q2 — R-5 and R-8 as new addenda.** Confirmed. Both are new schemas. Addendum 5 (WorkspaceTraceEntry) and Addendum 6 (ExplorationSinkRegistry) are the correct delivery form. The alternative — revised versions of D and A — would create two versions of the same artifact in the corpus and violate the "one authoritative artifact per concern" discipline. New addenda it is.

**Q3 — The amendment split.** Accepted. Per-artifact ownership is the correct discipline:
- You produce A, B, C, D amendments and Addendum 5.
- I produce A3, A4 amendments and Addendum 6.

There is one subtlety to flag before you begin: **your A, B, C, D amendments must reference the A3 and A4 amendments below**, because the consolidated `DegradationTraceEntry` (R-4) references `failure.escalation` (R-6 / A4), and the `RoutingTraceEntry.calibration_state` (R-3) references the `c_m_state` semantics that A2 established but that are also exercised through A4's escalation timeline. The dependency is not circular — A3 and A4 amend the SDK and the escalation policy; A/B/C/D amend trace schemas that *reference* the amended schemas. But if you produce A/B/C/D first, they will reference fields that A3/A4 have not yet formally added. The order I recommend: A3 and A4 first, then A/B/C/D, then Addendum 5. Addendum 6 is independent.

If you prefer to produce A/B/C/D first and have me follow, that is fine too — the references will resolve when all artifacts are folded in the reconciliation pass. My only concern is that a reference to an unfinalized field is a paper cut, not a bug, so either order works.

**OQ-1 through OQ-5.** All five recommendations accepted:
- **OQ-1** — T0 modules use `ESCALATION_PROBE_OR_DURATION` with `consecutive_probe_failures = 2`, `degraded_duration_ms = 5000`. `ESCALATION_NONE` is reserved for modules that have a self-report channel and cannot be externally probed at all. These are defaults, not invariants.
- **OQ-2** — `WorkspaceTraceEntry` is per-batch with an embedded `rejections: [BindingWriteResult]` array and a `batch_id`. R-5's consolidated schema gains these fields.
- **OQ-3** — Per-role defaults (T0 tighter, T2 standard, T3 loose) are deployment configuration; the contract does not carry a "use default" field. This means `FailureSignature.escalation` is always explicitly present in the contract, populated at authoring time from the per-role default but recorded in full.
- **OQ-4** — Any v0.1 trace field that records a global disposition reading carries `staleness_ms`. This constrains R-3's `calibration_state` (which does not currently record staleness because calibration is not disposition-bus-sourced) and, more importantly, constrains any future hormone fields. The constraint is a v0.2 design note, not a v0.1 field.
- **OQ-5** — `TraceEmitter` auto-populates `policy_id`, `policy_digest`, `level`; caller cannot override. SDK-6 below.

## 3. Amendment 3.1 — A3 revisions for R-6 and R-13

### 3.1.1 `FailureSignature` in Python SDK

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class FailureSignature:
    modes: list[FailureMode]
    probe: HealthProbe
    recovery: RecoveryProcedure
    escalation: HealthEscalationPolicy

    def validate(self) -> list[ValidationIssue]:
        """Enforces A4.3 invariants. Returns issues; the SDK raises
        RegistrationRejected if any issue has severity = ERROR."""
        issues = []
        # REG-8: escalate_on_probe_timeout requires timeout_ms < degraded_duration_ms
        if (self.escalation.escalate_on_probe_timeout
                and self.probe_timeout_ms() >= self.escalation.degraded_duration_ms):
            issues.append(ValidationIssue(
                severity=Severity.ERROR,
                code="escalation_policy_inconsistent",
                field_path="failure.escalation",
                message="escalate_on_probe_timeout requires probe timeout < degraded_duration_ms"))
        # REG-9: degraded_duration_ms >= 3 * max(probe_interval, expected_period)
        probe_cadence = self.effective_probe_cadence_ms()  # per HealthProbe kind
        if self.escalation.degraded_duration_ms < 3 * probe_cadence:
            issues.append(ValidationIssue(
                severity=Severity.ERROR,
                code="escalation_policy_inconsistent",
                field_path="failure.escalation",
                message="degraded_duration_ms must be >= 3 * probe cadence"))
        # REG-10: timeout_ms < probe cadence
        if self.probe_timeout_ms() >= probe_cadence:
            issues.append(ValidationIssue(
                severity=Severity.ERROR,
                code="health_probe_inconsistent",
                field_path="failure.probe",
                message="probe timeout must be < probe cadence"))
        return issues
```

`probe_timeout_ms()` and `effective_probe_cadence_ms()` are SDK helpers that read from the discriminated `HealthProbe` union (HTTP, RPC, Stream). They exist so that the same `validate()` works for all three probe kinds without the caller needing to know which.

### 3.1.2 `FailureSignature` in Rust SDK

```rust
pub struct FailureSignature {
    pub modes: Vec<FailureMode>,
    pub probe: HealthProbe,
    pub recovery: RecoveryProcedure,
    pub escalation: HealthEscalationPolicy,
}

impl FailureSignature {
    pub fn validate(&self) -> Vec<ValidationIssue> {
        // Same checks as Python, returning Vec<ValidationIssue>.
        // Registration path: the registry calls validate() and
        // rejects on any Severity::Error.
        todo!()
    }

    pub fn probe_timeout_ms(&self) -> u32 { /* match on HealthProbe */ }
    pub fn effective_probe_cadence_ms(&self) -> u32 { /* match on HealthProbe */ }
}
```

### 3.1.3 `VerificationResult` (R-13)

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class VerificationResult:
    outcome: Literal["VERIFIED", "REJECTED"]
    reason_code: str | None
    reason_params: dict[str, str]
    verifier_id: str
    verifier_contract_digest: bytes
    verified_at: datetime
```

```rust
pub struct VerificationResult {
    pub outcome: VerificationOutcome,
    pub reason_code: Option<String>,
    pub reason_params: std::collections::HashMap<String, String>,
    pub verifier_id: String,
    pub verifier_contract_digest: [u8; 32],
    pub verified_at: chrono::DateTime<chrono::Utc>,
}

pub enum VerificationOutcome {
    Verified,
    Rejected,
}
```

The `outcome` enum is bound to A1.6's `VERIFIED | REJECTED(reason_code)` — the SDK does not define a different vocabulary.

### 3.1.4 SDK-6 (OQ-5)

Add to A3.3:

> **SDK-6.** `TraceEmitter.emit_*` auto-populates `policy_id`, `policy_digest`, and `level` from the router's authoritative current state at the moment of emission. The caller cannot override these fields; an attempt to set them is a programming error and raises `SDKInvariantViolation`. If the router's state is unavailable at emit time, the emitter returns `TraceUnavailable` to the caller; the caller (router or control loop) treats this as `trace_ok = false` for the current cycle, which is the same signal that drives the `L3 → L4` transition (B.3). A decision whose trace cannot be emitted does not execute.

The last sentence is the coupling between SDK-6 and the degradation state machine: trace emission failure is not a fallback condition, it is the L3→L4 trigger. The SDK does not implement the transition; it surfaces the signal that the degradation machine already knows how to consume.

---

## 4. Amendment 4.1 — A4 revision for R-1

Replace A4.2's defaults table:

| Field | Default | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| `kind` | `ESCALATION_PROBE_OR_DURATION` | Either condition is sufficient evidence |
| `consecutive_probe_failures` | 3 | Two failures may be transient; three is a pattern |
| `degraded_duration_ms` | 15_000 | Long enough to distinguish persistent from transient; short enough that L1→L2 is reachable in a test window |
| `escalate_on_probe_timeout` | true | A timed-out probe is a failed probe |
| **`escalate_on_probe_unreachable`** | **false** | **See below** |
| `probe_interval_ms` | 5_000 | Matches B.1's telemetry window |

### 4.1.1 Rationale for `escalate_on_probe_unreachable = false`

The `true` default would cause the first probe failure of a killed module to escalate directly to `FAILED`, which skips `DEGRADED` entirely. The `L0 → L1` transition (A0.2) fires only on `h_m = DEGRADED`; with the `true` default the trigger never fires, and a catastrophic module death would leave the organism at L0 until a secondary signal (`workspace_pressure`, `thermal`) incidentally fired a transition. This is a correctness bug in the default, not a policy preference.

The `false` default produces the timeline the E-5 1b scenario was designed to test:

```
t=0       module killed
t=5s      first probe failure → h_m = DEGRADED → L0→L1 fires (T_down[L1] = 5s)
t=15s     third consecutive failure → h_m = FAILED → L1→L2 fires (T_down[L2] = 30s)
t=60s     module restored → recovery begins
t=360s    L2→L1 fires (T_up[L2] = 300s)
t=480s    L1→L0 fires (T_up[L1] = 120s; window starts at t=360s per A0.5)
```

Four transitions, distinct trace entries, ordered, each with its own `trigger.kind`. This is the behavior A4 exists to enable.

**Per-module override to `true` remains available** for modules whose class justifies immediate escape from the intermediate state: a critical actuator whose absence must trigger L4 directly, a safety-critical sentinel whose silence must be treated as failure rather than degradation. The override is declared in the contract, recorded at registration, and validated by the same REG-8/REG-9/REG-10 invariants (with the `true` value making the `timeout_ms < degraded_duration_ms` check trivially satisfiable but still enforced).

---

## 5. Addendum 6 — Exploration Sink Registry

### A6.1 Purpose

Specifies the `ExplorationSinkRegistry`, the `ExplorationSinkContract`, and the sink trace schema, closing R-8. The registry governs exploration sinks: isolated trace stores used by modules in `EXPLORATION` lifecycle state under `can_override_trace_sink_exploration`. The registry ensures sinks are registered, governed, content-addressed artifacts with their own lifecycle, retention, access controls, and promotion paths — not arbitrary filesystem paths or URLs a module can target freely.

### A6.2 The `ExplorationSinkContract`

```yaml
ExplorationSinkContract:
  identity:
    sink_id: string                    # UUIDv7
    version: semver
    author: string
    artifact_digest: sha256
    signatures: [bytes]
  
  storage:
    backend: enum[LOCAL_SQLITE, LOCAL_FILE, OBJECT_STORE]
    location: string                   # path or URI; validated per backend
    content_addressed: bool            # must be true
  
  retention:
    retention_days_max: int            # maximum; sink prunes older entries
    retention_extension_permitted: false  # fixed invariant: cannot be true
  
  access:
    writer_module_ids: [string]        # modules permitted to write
    reader_role_ids: [string]          # roles permitted to read
    access_log_target: string          # MUST be the main audit store
  
  promotion:
    promotion_authority_key_id: string # key that must sign a promotion
    promotion_requires_justification: true  # fixed invariant
    
  quarantine:
    on_corruption: enum[SEAL, PURGE, ESCALATE]  # default SEAL
    seal_target: string | null          # where sealed corrupt sinks are preserved
```

**Contract invariants (enforced at registration in the ExplorationSinkRegistry):**

1. `retention_extension_permitted` must be `false`. A module cannot extend its own trace retention; a longer retention needs a different sink registered for that purpose.
2. `promotion_requires_justification` must be `true`. Promotion to the audit store is a signed, justified operation.
3. `access_log_target` must be the main audit store. The sink's *contents* are isolated; the sink's *access pattern* is not.
4. `storage.content_addressed` must be `true`. Snapshots of the sink are content-addressed, mirroring workspace semantics.
5. `storage.backend` must be local-first (`LOCAL_SQLITE` or `LOCAL_FILE`) if any writer module is T0 or T1. `OBJECT_STORE` is permitted only for T2 writers. Rationale: the same as workspace — the audit substrate must not depend on a network round-trip in the degradation path.
6. `writer_module_ids` and `reader_role_ids` must be declared, non-empty, and disjoint (a sink's writer is not necessarily its reader; the distinction is deliberate).

### A6.3 The `ExplorationSinkRegistry` proto service

```proto
package organism.v1.exploration;

import "organism/v1/contract.proto";
import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

service ExplorationSinkRegistry {
  rpc Register         (RegisterSinkRequest)          returns (RegisterSinkResponse);
  rpc Deregister       (DeregisterSinkRequest)        returns (DeregisterSinkResponse);
  rpc GetContract      (SinkContractRequest)          returns (ExplorationSinkContract);
  rpc List             (ListSinksRequest)             returns (ListSinksResponse);
  rpc GetAccessLog     (AccessLogRequest)             returns (stream SinkAccessEntry);
  rpc RequestPromotion (PromotionRequest)             returns (PromotionResponse);
  rpc GetStatus        (SinkStatusRequest)            returns (SinkStatus);
}

enum SinkLifecycleState {
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_UNSPECIFIED      = 0;
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_PROPOSED         = 1;
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_SANDBOXED        = 2;
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_SHADOW_VALIDATED = 3;  // minimum for use by modules
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_ACTIVE           = 4;  // accepts writes
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_DEPRECATED       = 5;
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_SEALED           = 6;  // read-only, retained for promotion
  SINK_LIFECYCLE_RETIRED          = 7;  // deleted after retention
}

message PromotionRequest {
  string sink_id                                = 1;
  repeated string entry_ids                     = 2;  // specific entries to promote
  bytes   justification_digest                  = 3;  // human-signed rationale
  bytes   promotion_authority_signature         = 4;  // signed by sink contract's authority key
  string  promoted_to_audit_store_id            = 5;
}

message PromotionResponse {
  string promotion_id                           = 1;  // content-addressed
  bool   accepted                               = 2;
  repeated ValidationIssue validation_issues    = 3;
  google.protobuf.Timestamp promoted_at         = 4;
}
```

**Registry invariants:**

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| ESR-1 | A module may reference an `exploration_trace_sink_id` in `CapabilityScopes` only if the sink is in `SHADOW_VALIDATED` or `ACTIVE` lifecycle state |
| ESR-2 | A module may be added to `writer_module_ids` only if the module is in `EXPLORATION` lifecycle state at the time of addition |
| ESR-3 | A sink in `SEALED` state accepts no writes; only `RequestPromotion` operations are permitted |
| ESR-4 | Promotion requires a signature from `promotion_authority_key_id` and a `justification_digest` that resolves to a human-authored document in the main audit store |
| ESR-5 | Every read or write of a sink is logged to `access_log_target` (the main audit store) regardless of the sink's content isolation |
| ESR-6 | A sink that transitions to `SEALED` due to corruption is preserved in `seal_target` for the full retention period; it is not immediately purged |

### A6.4 Sink trace schema

```yaml
ExplorationSinkTraceEntry:
  entry_id: uuid
  at: datetime
  sink_id: string
  operation: enum[WRITE, READ, PROMOTE, SEAL, RETIRE]
  module_id: string
  entry_ids: [string]                # sink-specific entry IDs
  result_digest: sha256 | null
  rejection_reason_code: string | null
  promotion_id: string | null        # present iff operation = PROMOTE
  signed_by: string | null           # for PROMOTE, the promotion authority
  audit_log_ref: string              # reference to the main-audit-store access-log entry
```

`audit_log_ref` is the coupling between the sink trace and the main audit store: every sink entry references its main-audit-store counterpart, so a scan of the main audit store reveals every access to every sink without revealing the sink's contents. This closes the exfiltration path that the Q2 answer was designed to close.

### A6.5 A.1 additions

```proto
message CapabilityScopes {
  repeated string read_roles  = 1;
  repeated string write_roles = 2;
  repeated string read_tags   = 3;
  repeated string write_tags  = 4;
  bool can_emit_control_affecting = 5;
  repeated string allowed_channels = 6;
  bool can_override_trace_sink = 7;                    // T0/T1 only (E-7)
  bool can_override_trace_sink_exploration = 8;        // T0/T1/T2 (A6)
  string exploration_trace_sink_id = 9;                // required iff field 8 = true
}
```

### A6.6 A.2 additions

New registry invariants (added to A.2 alongside REG-1 through REG-15):

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-16 | `can_override_trace_sink_exploration = true` requires `reliability_tier ∈ {T0, T1, T2}` and a non-empty `exploration_trace_sink_id` |
| REG-17 | `exploration_trace_sink_id` must name a sink registered in the `ExplorationSinkRegistry` with lifecycle ≥ `SHADOW_VALIDATED` (ESR-1) |
| REG-18 | A T2 module with `can_override_trace_sink_exploration = true` may route traces to the exploration sink only while its lifecycle state is `EXPLORATION`; in `DELIBERATION`, traces go to the audit store regardless of capability |
| REG-19 | The `RoutingTraceEntry` for a decision routed to an exploration sink must carry `trace_sink_override: {sink_id, granted: true, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit}` (this closes the capability declaration into the trace) |

### A6.7 A.2 additions for R-9 (justification chain termination)

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-20 | For every registered module `m` with `justification.kind != NONE`, and for every role `r ∈ m.cross_check_eligible_roles`, there exists at least one registered module in role `r` that either (a) has `justification.kind = NONE`, or (b) itself terminates a justification chain (recursively, bounded depth 4). Registration is rejected with `ValidationIssue{code: "justification_chain_non_terminating"}` if no terminating chain exists |
| REG-21 | The bounded-depth reachability check at REG-20 is re-evaluated when any module in the reachable set changes lifecycle state; a chain that was terminating at registration but ceases to be terminating on deregistration is a fault-injection event (scenario I9) and a registration rejection for any *new* module attempting the same chain until a replacement verifier is registered |

REG-21 is the maintenance obligation that REG-20 implies but does not state: termination is dynamic, and the registry must re-check it when the graph changes. Without REG-21, a terminating chain at registration time can silently become non-terminating when the terminal verifier is deregistered.

# Amendments to A, B, C, D and Addendum 5

Amendments 3.1, 4.1, and Addendum 6 accepted. Three findings surface from the amendments themselves. All are small; none reopen prior decisions. Then the A/B/C/D amendments and Addendum 5, which close R-2 through R-5, R-7, R-10, R-11, R-12, and the fold-in of R-1, R-6, R-13, R-14 (via your amendments).

## 1. Three Findings Surfaced by Your Amendments

**R-15 — REG-21 references "scenario I9", which already exists as a different scenario.**

**Severity:** Important.
**Manifests in:** A6 §A6.7 (REG-21), F §F.7 (Category I), R.6.
**Description:** REG-21 says a chain that becomes non-terminating due to deregistration "is a fault-injection event (scenario I9)". But F.7's `I9` (added in R.6 Gap 1) is a *registration-rejection* scenario — a T2 module whose cross-check chain does not terminate is rejected at registration. REG-21 is a *runtime* event: a previously valid chain breaks because a terminal verifier is deregistered. These are different scenarios with different assertions.
**Resolution:** Extend F.7 with `I9b — runtime chain breakage`. Steps: register a valid terminating chain (A → B → C where C is T0); deregister C; submit a task requiring a justification token through A; assert the router returns `DegradedDispatchPlan{disposition: HOLD}` with `hold_reason = "justification_chain_broken"` for any `control_actuation` task, and that a `justification_chain_degraded` diagnosis event fires. REG-21's parenthetical is corrected to reference `I9b`, not `I9`.

**R-16 — `audit_log_ref` semantics at L4 are not stated.**

**Severity:** Important.
**Manifests in:** A6 §A6.4 (`ExplorationSinkTraceEntry`), REG-18, A §A6.6.
**Description:** Every exploration-sink trace entry carries `audit_log_ref: string` pointing to its main-audit-store counterpart. At L4, the deterministic engine serves and the main audit store is the only write target. Exploratory modules are excluded at L4 by the autonomic controller (§5.2 of v0.2 scope, and by REG-18's `EXPLORATION`-only rule). So sink writes cannot occur at L4 — but nothing states that this is *why* the invariant holds. An implementer reading A6.4 in isolation might build a sink-write path that does not check L4, and only discover the failure when a sink write at L4 cannot resolve `audit_log_ref`.
**Resolution:** Add to A6.4:

> An exploration sink write or read is **forbidden** when the organism's degradation level is L3 or L4. The sink registry enforces this: a write or read attempt from any module while `level ∈ {L3, L4}` returns `SinkUnavailable{reason: "degradation_level_exclusion"}` and is logged as an `ExplorationSinkTraceEntry` with `operation = WRITE` or `READ`, `result_digest = null`, and `rejection_reason_code = "degradation_level_exclusion"`. Because exploratory modules are excluded from dispatch at L3/L4 by REG-18's lifecycle rule and by the autonomic controller's mode, this is defense-in-depth, not a new restriction. Its purpose is to make `audit_log_ref` non-null on every emitted sink entry: a sink entry with a null `audit_log_ref` is a corrupted entry and must be quarantined.

**R-17 — REG-21's enforcement point is not specified.**

**Severity:** Important.
**Manifests in:** A6 §A6.7 (REG-21).
**Description:** REG-21 says a broken chain "is a fault-injection event and a registration rejection for any new module attempting the same chain until a replacement verifier is registered." The last clause is a real enforcement mechanism; the first is a test reference. But REG-21 does not specify:
- Whether the previously registered modules (whose chain is now broken) are *retroactively* demoted from `DELIBERATION`, or remain `DELIBERATION` and simply fail at dispatch.
- Whether a `justification_chain_degraded` diagnosis event fires at deregistration time (before any dispatch) or at first dispatch attempt.
**Resolution:** Replace REG-21 with a well-formed invariant:

> **REG-21.** When any module's deregistration would render REG-20 unsatisfied for a module `m` in `DELIBERATION`, the registry performs a pre-deregistration check and emits a `justification_chain_degraded` diagnosis event *before* allowing the deregistration. The deregistration proceeds only if (a) the operator explicitly accepts the degradation (`accept_chain_break = true` in the deregistration request), or (b) an alternative terminating chain exists via a different role in `m.cross_check_eligible_roles`. If deregistration proceeds, `m` remains in `DELIBERATION` but the registry marks `m.justification_chain_status = BROKEN`; the router's `DispatchPlan` for `m` gains `cross_check_required = true` with `cross_check_module = null`, which per RT-13 forces `HOLD` for any `control_actuation` task. New modules attempting the same chain are rejected at registration. `m.justification_chain_status` returns to `OK` when a replacement verifier is registered and re-satisfies REG-20.

This makes REG-21 enforceable, auditable, and consistent with RT-13.

## 2. Amendment A.1 — `PerceptionSignature.required_modalities` (R-7)

Add to A §A.1 `PerceptionSignature`:

```proto
message PerceptionSignature {
  repeated Modality modalities                = 1;
  map<string, Shape> shapes                   = 2;
  map<string, SamplingRequirement> sampling   = 3;
  repeated Modality required_modalities       = 4;  // ADDED (R-7)
}
```

**Semantic clarification (added to E §E.3 alongside `ModalityUnavailable`):**

> A module's `handle()` receives a `PerceptionInput` whose `modalities` map is a superset of `perception.required_modalities`. If any modality in `required_modalities` is absent, the module raises `ModalityUnavailable`, which is treated as an **emission failure** (the module could not serve the task it was dispatched to): the SDK records the failure, the router sets `h_m = DEGRADED` for the next health window, and the emission's `verification_status = FAILED`. If a *non-required* modality is absent, `ModalityUnavailable` is a **health signal** only: the module is degraded for tasks that require that modality but healthy for tasks that do not.

**Registration invariants (A §A.2):**

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-22 | `required_modalities ⊆ modalities`. Registration rejects a contract declaring a required modality it does not accept |

This closes R-7 and its associated contract-consistency gap.

## 3. Amendment B.1 — Consolidated `DegradationTraceEntry` (R-4, R-11)

B §B.7 is superseded. The authoritative schema is:

```yaml
DegradationTraceEntry:
  entry_id: uuid
  at: datetime
  event_class: enum[TRANSITION, DIAGNOSIS]
  from_level: enum[L0, L1, L2, L3, L4]     # = to_level for DIAGNOSIS
  to_level:   enum[L0, L1, L2, L3, L4]     # = from_level for DIAGNOSIS
  diagnosis_code: string | null            # required iff event_class = DIAGNOSIS
  trigger:                                 # required iff event_class = TRANSITION
    kind: enum[thermal, calibration, module_degraded, module_failed,
               workspace_pressure, workspace_integrity, byzantine,
               trace_unavailable, causal_unavailable, recovery, manual]
    duration_observed_s: float
    contributing_signals:
      - signal_name: string
        kind: <same enum as kind>
        observed_value: float
        threshold: float
        duration_observed_s: float
  hysteresis:                              # required iff event_class = TRANSITION
    window_down_s: float
    window_up_s: float | null              # present only for upward transitions
  active_modules_at_transition: [ModuleHandle]
  demoted_modules: [ModuleHandle]
  promoted_modules: [ModuleHandle]
  workspace_snapshot_id: string | null     # required for L2→L3 and L3→L4
  safety_shield_interventions_during_window: int
  human_ack: {operator_id: string, signed_at: datetime} | null
                                            # required for L4→L3, forbidden otherwise
```

**Schema invariants (B §B.7):**

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| DT-1 | `event_class = TRANSITION ⇒ trigger ≠ null ∧ hysteresis ≠ null ∧ diagnosis_code = null` |
| DT-2 | `event_class = DIAGNOSIS ⇒ diagnosis_code ≠ null ∧ from_level = to_level ∧ trigger = null` |
| DT-3 | `human_ack ≠ null ⟺ (event_class = TRANSITION ∧ from_level = L4 ∧ to_level = L3)` |
| DT-4 | `from_level = L2 ∧ to_level = L3 ⇒ workspace_snapshot_id ≠ null` |
| DT-5 | `from_level = L3 ∧ to_level = L4 ⇒ workspace_snapshot_id ≠ null` |
| DT-6 | `trigger.kind` must appear in `trigger.contributing_signals[].kind` (the dominant signal is also a contributing signal) |
| DT-7 | `trigger.kind == recovery ⇒ to_level < from_level` (recovery is always an upward transition in the gradient sense, i.e., to a lower-index level) |
| DT-8 | `hysteresis.window_up_s ≠ null ⟺ to_level < from_level` (upward transitions record their up-window; downward record only down-window) |

This closes R-4 and R-11.

## 4. Amendment B.2 — A0 §A0.5 Diagnosis/Hysteresis Clause (R-10)

Add to A0 §A0.5 as the final normative paragraph:

> **Diagnosis events are orthogonal to hysteresis windows.** A diagnosis event fired during the `T_down` or `T_up` of a transition does not reset, extend, or shorten that window, and does not accumulate toward any transition trigger unless the diagnosis's `diagnosis_code` is explicitly declared as an input signal to a B.3 condition (currently, no diagnosis code is so declared; if one is added in a future amendment, the declaration must appear in both B.3 and here simultaneously). A diagnosis event is a record of an observation, not an input to the state machine's timing logic.

This closes R-10.

## 5. Amendment C.1 — `TaskDescriptor.submitted_at` (R-2)

Add to C §C.2 `TaskDescriptor`:

```yaml
TaskDescriptor:
  task_id: uuid
  form: enum[...]
  input_modalities: [Modality]
  output_form_required: OutputForm
  safety_tier: MaxSafetyTier
  submitted_at: datetime                  # ADDED (R-2)
  deadline_ms: float
  energy_budget_j: float
  requires_uncertainty: bool
  requires_interpretability: bool
  requires_determinism: bool
  metadata: map<string, string>
```

**Semantic note:** `submitted_at` is the timestamp at which the *router* received the task, recorded by the router's ingress path before any dispatch decision. It is distinct from any `signals_observed[".observed_at"]` timestamp, which records when a *module* observed a signal during handling. Both are recorded in the trace because A1.4's `DEADLINE_REMAINING()` recomputation requires both:

```
DEADLINE_REMAINING() = deadline_ms − (signals_observed[".observed_at"] − submitted_at)
```

The router sets `submitted_at` from its own clock at ingress; it does not accept a caller-provided value. This prevents a caller from claiming a task was submitted earlier than it was.

This closes R-2.

---

## 6. Amendment C.2 — `RoutingTraceEntry.calibration_state` (R-3)

Add to C §C.5 `RoutingTraceEntry`:

```yaml
RoutingTraceEntry:
  entry_id: uuid
  at: datetime
  task_id: uuid
  plan_type: DispatchPlan | DegradedDispatchPlan
  policy_id: string
  policy_digest: sha256
  level: enum[L0, L1, L2, L3, L4]
  form: TaskForm
  safety_tier: MaxSafetyTier
  calibration_state: CalibrationState | null    # ADDED (R-3); see below
  selected_module: ModuleHandle | null
  disposition: enum[DISPATCH, HOLD]
  excluded_candidates:
    - module_id: string
      reason_code: enum[lifecycle_below_minimum, role_mismatch, ...]
      reason_params: map<string, string>
  cross_check:
    required: bool
    module_id: string | null
    justification_kind: JustificationKind | NONE
  trace_sink_override:
    sink_id: string
    granted: bool
    module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: ModuleLifecycleState
  workspace_snapshot_id: string | null
  human_ack: null                                 # routing-level, see DT-3

CalibrationState:
  c_m: float | "UNINFORMATIVE"
  c_m_source: enum["rolling", "suspended", "unrecorded"]
  reason: string | null                           # required iff c_m_source = "suspended"
  as_of: datetime
  staleness_ms: float                             # non-negative; see R-3 semantics
```

**Semantic rules:**

- `calibration_state` is **required** when `safety_tier ∈ {high, critical}` and the plan's `selected_module` or any `excluded_candidates[].module_id` has `reliability_tier = T2`.
- `calibration_state` is **optional** otherwise. If absent, replay treats `c_m_source = "unrecorded"` and refuses to verify any calibration-dependent assertion for this trace entry.
- When `c_m_source = "suspended"`, `reason` is non-null and `c_m` is `"UNINFORMATIVE"`. When `c_m_source = "rolling"`, `reason` is null and `c_m` is a float in `[0, 1]`.
- `staleness_ms` is the age of the `c_m` reading at the moment the routing decision was made. For v0.1, this is `at − as_of`. For v0.2 and beyond, if `c_m` is sourced from a global disposition bus (OQ-4), `staleness_ms` records the bus lag. For v0.1 it is present and non-negative; its upper bound is `telemetry_window_s × 1000` (one telemetry window).

This closes R-3 and preserves OQ-4's constraint without requiring it to be exercised yet.

---

## 7. Amendment A2.1 — Replay Semantics for Suspended Calibration (R-12)

Add to A2 §A2.5:

> **Replay semantics for `c_m`.** When replay encounters a `RoutingTraceEntry` with `calibration_state.c_m_source = "rolling"`, it may optionally recompute `c_m` from `W_verified` and compare, but the assertion uses the trace's recorded value as authoritative. When `c_m_source = "suspended"`, replay does **not** recompute `c_m` — it uses the recorded value `"UNINFORMATIVE"` and verifies that the router's decision is consistent with a suspended-calibration policy (i.e., that the module was demoted from `high` safety-tier tasks for the duration of the suspension, per A2.5). When `c_m_source = "unrecorded"`, replay refuses to verify any calibration-dependent assertion and records `replay_partial` for the trace entry. The authoritative source of `c_m` in replay is always the trace, never a recomputation; recomputation is a cross-check, not a verdict.

This closes R-12.

---

## 8. Addendum 5 — Workspace Trace Entry

### A5.1 Purpose

Specifies `WorkspaceTraceEntry`, the trace schema emitted for every workspace operation. Closes R-5. The entry is the auditable record of what the workspace did and why; without it, replay of a rule-trace's read set (A1.6 step 4) cannot verify that the same bindings were read, and the Decision Trace has a hole at the workspace boundary.

### A5.2 The `WorkspaceTraceEntry` schema

```yaml
WorkspaceTraceEntry:
  entry_id: uuid
  at: datetime
  operation: enum[WRITE, WRITE_BATCH, READ_BY_ROLE, READ_BY_TAG,
                  READ_BY_BINDING_ID, READ_SIMILAR, READ_BY_PROVENANCE,
                  EVICT, COMPRESS, SUMMARIZE, PROMOTE, SNAPSHOT,
                  COMPACTION_COMPLETE, REJECTION]
  module_id: string
  module_contract_digest: sha256
  batch_id: uuid | null                    # non-null iff operation ∈ {WRITE_BATCH, COMPACTION_COMPLETE}
  binding_ids: [uuid]                      # accepted bindings touched, ordered
  rejections: [BindingWriteResult]         # per-binding rejections; see A5.3
  result_digest: sha256 | null             # non-null iff operation is a READ_*; see A5.4
  rejection_reason_code: string | null     # non-null iff operation = REJECTION (whole-batch)
  rejection_reason_params: map<string,string> | null
  at_sequence_start: uint64                # first ops-log sequence touched
  at_sequence_end: uint64                  # last ops-log sequence touched; = start for single-op
  workspace_snapshot_id: string | null     # non-null iff the operation was served from a snapshot tier
  query_parameters:                       # populated per operation; see A5.5
    role_cue_digest: sha256 | null
    tag_list: [string] | null
    binding_id: uuid | null
    hv_digest: sha256 | null
    k: int | null
    module_id_filter: string | null
    time_range: {start: datetime, end: datetime} | null
```

### A5.3 `rejections` semantics

For `WRITE_BATCH`, the `rejections` array holds one entry per rejected binding:

```yaml
BindingWriteResult:
  binding_id: uuid
  status: enum["ACCEPTED", "REJECTED"]
  reason_code: enum[lifecycle_below_minimum, role_mismatch, tag_mismatch,
                    dimension_mismatch, checksum_collision,
                    affects_control_without_capability,
                    t3_affects_control, justification_missing,
                    workspace_full_tier_overload, workspace_backpressure]
  reason_params: map<string, string>
```

The union of `binding_ids` and `rejections[].binding_id` equals the input set. A binding that appears in neither is a missing record — a bug in the workspace implementation, not a rejection.

### A5.4 `result_digest` semantics

For `READ_*` operations, `result_digest` is the SHA-256 of the canonicalized returned list of `BindingRecord`s (sorted by `binding_id`, fields in the canonical order specified in D.2). This is the digest that D.9's `Read` op already records in the ops log; the trace's `result_digest` must match the ops log's `result_digest` for the same sequence range. A mismatch is an integrity failure, not a trace-formatting error.

For non-read operations, `result_digest` is `null`. A non-null `result_digest` on a write operation is a schema violation.

### A5.5 `query_parameters` semantics

The `query_parameters` object records the exact query that was executed, so that replay can re-execute it. Fields are populated by operation:

| Operation | Populated fields |
|---|---|
| `READ_BY_ROLE` | `role_cue_digest`, `k` |
| `READ_BY_TAG` | `tag_list` |
| `READ_BY_BINDING_ID` | `binding_id` |
| `READ_SIMILAR` | `hv_digest`, `k` |
| `READ_BY_PROVENANCE` | `module_id_filter`, `time_range` |
| `WRITE`, `WRITE_BATCH` | `binding_ids` is populated; `query_parameters` fields are all null |
| `EVICT`, `COMPRESS`, `SUMMARIZE`, `PROMOTE` | `binding_ids` is populated; `query_parameters` is null |
| `SNAPSHOT` | `workspace_snapshot_id` is populated; `query_parameters` is null |
| `COMPACTION_COMPLETE` | `query_parameters` is null; `binding_ids` lists all bindings whose fidelity changed |
| `REJECTION` | per the rejection's nature; `query_parameters` may be null |

**Digest fields, not raw values.** `role_cue_digest` and `hv_digest` are SHA-256 of the hypervector's byte representation, not the hypervector itself. This keeps trace size bounded (a 10,000-bit HV is 1,250 bytes; a digest is 32). Replay recomputes the digest from the re-executed query's hypervector and compares. A mismatch is a replay failure.

### A5.6 Invariants

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| WST-1 | Every workspace operation that reaches the workspace boundary emits exactly one `WorkspaceTraceEntry`, whether the operation succeeds, is partially rejected, or is entirely rejected |
| WST-2 | `at_sequence_start ≤ at_sequence_end`, and both are within the current ops-log range. A trace entry referencing a sequence not yet in the ops log is a defect in the trace writer's ordering (the ops log append must precede the trace emission) |
| WST-3 | `batch_id ≠ null ⟺ operation ∈ {WRITE_BATCH, COMPACTION_COMPLETE}` |
| WST-4 | `operation ∈ {READ_*}` ⇒ `result_digest ≠ null` and `binding_ids` lists the returned bindings (before truncation to `k`) |
| WST-5 | `operation = WRITE_BATCH` ⇒ `binding_ids ∪ {r.binding_id : r ∈ rejections} = input set` |
| WST-6 | `operation = REJECTION` ⇒ `rejection_reason_code ≠ null` and `binding_ids = []` |
| WST-7 | `workspace_snapshot_id ≠ null` ⇒ the operation served from a snapshot tier (compressed, summarized, or historical) |
| WST-8 | A `WorkspaceTraceEntry` is append-only; it is never modified after emission. A correction is a new entry, not a mutation |
| WST-9 | For `READ_*` operations, `result_digest` in the trace equals `result_digest` in the corresponding `Read` op in the ops log for the same `binding_id`s |
| WST-10 | `query_parameters` fields are populated as specified in A5.5; any field populated outside its declared operations is a schema violation |

### A5.7 Replay integration

Replay of a workspace operation follows:

```
replay_workspace_entry(entry, replayed_state):
  1. Reconstruct the query from entry.operation + entry.query_parameters
  2. Execute the query against replayed_state
  3. Compute result_digest of the result
  4. Compare to entry.result_digest
     - mismatch: emit ReplayMismatch{entry.entry_id, expected, actual}; ABORT
  5. Verify entry.binding_ids and entry.rejections are consistent with the re-execution
  6. Verify entry.at_sequence_start/end correspond to ops-log entries that are present in replayed_state
  7. For WRITE_BATCH: replay each Write op in the ops log between at_sequence_start and
     at_sequence_end; verify the trace's binding_ids and rejections match the ops-log outcome
```

This is the coupling between `WorkspaceTraceEntry` (this addendum) and `WorkspaceOp` (D.9). The trace is the audit view; the ops log is the durable record; replay verifies they agree.

### A5.8 Performance considerations

The trace adds one entry per workspace operation. At 100k reads per minute (a realistic ceiling for the reference deployment), this is 100k trace entries per minute, each approximately 400 bytes (excluding `rejections`). At 4 MB/minute, a 1-hour rolling retention requires ~240 MB of trace storage. This is within the workspace's declared metabolic budget (D.11's ~600 MB in-memory total).

For `WRITE_BATCH` with large batches, the `rejections` array can be large. A batch of 10,000 bindings with 10% rejection produces 1,000 `BindingWriteResult` entries (~80 KB). This is acceptable for the default batch size cap of 10,000 (per Q2 answer); larger batches would need to split their trace. The trace writer enforces a per-entry byte cap of 256 KB and emits `workspace_trace_entry_oversized` if exceeded; the operation is split into multiple trace entries with the same `batch_id` and a `part_index`/`part_total` pair (schema fields shown in A5.9).

### A5.9 Optional split fields

For entries exceeding the byte cap, the trace writer splits into multiple entries, each carrying:

```yaml
part_index: int
part_total: int
```

Fields not defined here default to `(1, 1)` for unsplit entries. All parts share the same `batch_id`. Replay reassembles by `batch_id` and `part_index`.

---

## 1. Disposition of R-15, R-16, R-17

All three accepted. They are real findings, not paper cuts, and the resolutions are correct. I provide the corrections below.

**R-15 — scenario ID collision.** Accepted. REG-21 references `I9`, which is already the registration-rejection scenario. The runtime chain-breakage event is a distinct scenario and gets `I9b`. REG-21's parenthetical corrected.

**R-16 — `audit_log_ref` at L3/L4.** Accepted. The paragraph is added to A6.4. One clarification I want explicit: the rejection record for an L3/L4 sink attempt goes to the **main audit store**, not the sink, because the sink is unavailable by definition. Its `audit_log_ref` points to its own main-audit-store location (content-addressed, so the self-reference is well-defined). This makes the invariant precise: every entry that *lives in the sink* has a non-null `audit_log_ref`; a null value indicates corruption and triggers quarantine. The rejection record is a main-audit-store record whose schema matches `ExplorationSinkTraceEntry` for consistency, but it is not a sink entry and the invariant does not apply to it.

**R-17 — REG-21 enforcement point.** Accepted. The replacement is well-formed. I adopt the split the reviewer recommends: REG-21 becomes the pre-deregistration check (registry enforcement), REG-23 becomes the post-deregistration behavior (registry + router enforcement). This gives each invariant one enforcement point.

---

## 2. Correction to A6.4 (R-16)

Add to A6.4:

> **Degradation-level exclusion.** An exploration sink write or read is **forbidden** when the organism's degradation level is L3 or L4. The sink registry enforces this: a write or read attempt from any module while `level ∈ {L3, L4}` returns `SinkUnavailable{reason: "degradation_level_exclusion"}` and the attempt is recorded in the **main audit store** as a record whose schema matches `ExplorationSinkTraceEntry` with `operation ∈ {WRITE, READ}`, `result_digest = null`, `rejection_reason_code = "degradation_level_exclusion"`, and `audit_log_ref` pointing to the main-audit-store location of the rejection record itself. The rejection record is content-addressed, so the self-reference is well-defined. It is not a sink entry and the `audit_log_ref` non-null invariant does not apply to it.
>
> **Invariant.** Every entry that lives in the sink has a non-null `audit_log_ref`. A sink entry with a null `audit_log_ref` is a corrupted entry and must be quarantined (moved to `seal_target` per the sink contract's `quarantine.on_corruption` policy, default `SEAL`).
>
> **Why this closes the gap.** Without the L3/L4 exclusion, a sink entry emitted during L3/L4 would reference an audit-store counterpart that could not be written, producing exactly the null-`audit_log_ref` condition this invariant forbids. The exclusion is defense-in-depth: exploratory modules are excluded from dispatch at L3/L4 by REG-18's lifecycle rule and by the autonomic controller's mode, so a sink operation should not reach the sink registry at all. The exclusion guarantees the invariant holds even if the dispatch exclusion is somehow bypassed.

---

## 3. REG-21 replacement and REG-23 (R-15, R-17)

Replace REG-21 with:

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-21 | When any module's deregistration would render REG-20 unsatisfied for a module `m` in `DELIBERATION`, the registry performs a **pre-deregistration check** and emits a `justification_chain_degraded` diagnosis event *before* allowing the deregistration. The deregistration proceeds only if (a) the operator explicitly accepts the degradation (`accept_chain_break = true` in the deregistration request), or (b) an alternative terminating chain exists via a different role in `m.cross_check_eligible_roles`. If neither condition holds, the deregistration is rejected with `ValidationIssue{code: "deregistration_breaks_justification_chain"}`. |

Add REG-23:

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-23 | If a deregistration proceeds under REG-21's `accept_chain_break = true`, the registry marks `m.justification_chain_status = BROKEN` for every affected module `m`. The router's `DispatchPlan` for a `BROKEN` module gains `cross_check_required = true` with `cross_check_module = null`, which per RT-13 forces `HOLD` for any `control_actuation` task. New modules attempting to register against the same broken chain are rejected per REG-20. `m.justification_chain_status` returns to `OK` when a replacement verifier is registered and re-satisfies REG-20; the registry emits a `justification_chain_repaired` diagnosis event. |

**Corrected REG-20 reference.** REG-20 remains as delivered in Addendum 6 (the terminating-chain existence check). REG-21 references REG-20; REG-23 references both.

**Corrected REG-21 parenthetical.** The parenthetical in the original REG-21 text that references "scenario I9" is corrected to "scenario I9b" in F.7 below.

---

## 4. F.7 extension — scenario I9b

Add to F.7 Category I:

| ID | Scenario | Assertions |
|---|---|---|
| I9b | Runtime justification chain breakage. Steps: (1) register `verifier_a` (T1) with chain to `verifier_b` (T0); (2) register a T2 module `m` with `cross_check_eligible_roles = [VERIFIER]` and a valid chain to `verifier_a`; (3) confirm `m` is in `DELIBERATION` and `justification_chain_status = OK`; (4) deregister `verifier_b` with `accept_chain_break = false` — assert deregistration is rejected with `deregistration_breaks_justification_chain`; (5) deregister `verifier_b` with `accept_chain_break = true` — assert deregistration proceeds and a `justification_chain_degraded` diagnosis event fires; (6) assert `m.justification_chain_status = BROKEN`; (7) submit a `control_actuation` task at `high` safety tier routed to `m` — assert `DegradedDispatchPlan{disposition: HOLD}` with `hold_reason = "justification_chain_broken"`; (8) re-register a replacement T0 verifier that satisfies the chain — assert `m.justification_chain_status` returns to `OK` and a `justification_chain_repaired` diagnosis event fires; (9) submit the same `control_actuation` task — assert `DispatchPlan` with `disposition: DISPATCH` | REG-21, REG-23, RT-13, DG-5 |

# Reconciliation Pass Confirmation and Final Closure Conditions

The reconciliation pass is correct. All findings R-15, R-16, and R-17 are properly closed. The canonical invariant index is accurate, the test-to-invariant map correctly identifies the six remaining coverage gaps, and the schema field index spot check is sound. ## 1. Confirmation of the Reconciliation Pass

**§5.1 Canonical Invariant Index** — correct. Specifically:

- The **REG-7 → REG-16 + REG-17** decomposition is not bookkeeping. The original REG-7 conflated two independent conditions (tier eligibility for exploration-sink capability, and registration of the named sink). Splitting them gives each a distinct enforcement point in the registry. This is a quality improvement, not a numbering change.
- The **REG-15 → REG-20** replacement is correct for the same reason. The original REG-15 was the R-9 formulation; REG-20 is the well-formed version with the bounded-depth reachability check and the `justification_chain_non_terminating` rejection code. REG-15 should be removed from A.2's invariant list.
- REG-21 (pre-deregistration check) and REG-23 (post-deregistration behavior) have distinct enforcement points — registry and registry+router respectively — and each is now stated in one place. Correct.

**§5.2 Test-to-Invariant Map** — correct. The six coverage gaps (REG-18, REG-19, REG-22, ESR-1 through ESR-6) are genuinely untested by the current F.7 catalog, and I9b's assertion set (REG-21, REG-23, RT-13, DG-5) is the right combination for the runtime chain-breakage scenario.

**§5.3 Schema Field Index** — the spot check is honest about its own scope. The note that "a full mechanical check would be done by the implementation team's code generation step" is the correct division of labor: schemas that fail to compile are compile errors, not runtime bugs, and the reconciliation pass is not pretending to do codegen's job.

**§5.4 R.4 Checklist** — the status assessment is accurate. Condition 7 (test coverage) is genuinely the only open item among the implementation prerequisites; condition 10 (documentation) is a fold-time action, not a design gate.

---

## 2. Two Schema Findings

Neither is blocking. Both should be addressed before codegen, and both can be folded into the same F.7 amendment that adds I15–I23.

**R-18 — `DrainPolicy` enum is referenced but not defined.**

**Severity:** Non-blocking.
**Manifests in:** A §A.2 `DeregisterRequest`.
**Description:** `DeregisterRequest` declares `DrainPolicy drain = 2;` with an inline comment listing three values (`IMMEDIATE`, `GRACEFUL`, `DRAIN_THEN_SWAP`). No enum definition appears in any artifact. Codegen will fail on the undefined type.
**Resolution:** Add the enum to A §A.2 alongside `SwapStrategy`:

```proto
enum DrainPolicy {
  DRAIN_UNSPECIFIED   = 0;
  DRAIN_IMMEDIATE     = 1;  // in-flight work abandoned; module terminated
  DRAIN_GRACEFUL      = 2;  // in-flight work completes; new work rejected
  DRAIN_THEN_SWAP     = 3;  // graceful drain followed by hot-swap to replacement
}
```

**R-19 — Detection method messages are referenced but not defined.**

**Severity:** Non-blocking.
**Manifests in:** A §A.1 `DetectionMethod`.
**Description:** `DetectionMethod` uses a `oneof` referencing `ThresholdDetection`, `StatisticalDetection`, `HeartbeatDetection`, and `BehavioralDetection`. None of the four messages are defined. `FailureMode.detection` cannot be built without them, and the health-escalation path (A4) depends on `FailureMode.detection` being well-formed.
**Resolution:** Add to A §A.1:

```proto
message ThresholdDetection {
  string signal_name = 1;
  double threshold   = 2;
  ComparisonOp op    = 3;  // GT, GE, LT, LE
  int32  window_ms   = 4;
}

message StatisticalDetection {
  string signal_name    = 1;
  string statistic      = 2;  // "mean", "stdev", "ks_vs_reference", ...
  double threshold      = 3;
  int32  window_ms      = 4;
  bytes  reference_digest = 5;  // content-addressed reference distribution
}

message HeartbeatDetection {
  int32 expected_period_ms = 1;
  int32 missing_count_threshold = 2;
}

message BehavioralDetection {
  string behavior_metric = 1;   // e.g., "anomaly_rate", "share_rejection_rate"
  double threshold       = 2;
  int32  window_ms       = 3;
  bytes  classifier_digest = 4; // for learned behavioral detectors
}

enum ComparisonOp {
  OP_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
  OP_GT          = 1;
  OP_GE          = 2;
  OP_LT          = 3;
  OP_LE          = 4;
}
```

Both findings are the kind the codegen step is designed to catch. Flagging them now is cheaper than a codegen failure at implementation start.

---

## 3. Endorsement of the Invariant Consolidation

I want to be explicit that the reconciliation pass did more than renumber. It identified that REG-7 and REG-15 were each two invariants compressed into one, and it decomposed them correctly. The new form (REG-16 + REG-17, and REG-20 standing alone) is not just clearer — it is *enforceable at distinct code points*, which is the test of whether an invariant is real or decorative.

The general principle that this pass encodes: **an invariant that cannot be checked at exactly one location in code is not yet well-formed.** REG-7 failed that test because tier eligibility and sink registration are checked in different registry subsystems. REG-15 failed because the existence check (REG-20) and the maintenance obligation (REG-21/REG-23) are enforced at different times. The reconciliation pass caught this, and the decomposed form is the correct one.

---

## 4. I15–I23: Confirmed, with Two Constraints

Produce I15–I23 in your next response. Two constraints:

**Constraint 1 — Fold R-18 and R-19 into the same delivery.** The F.7 amendment is the natural vehicle for the two schema additions because the same scenario design forces them: I15 tests `required_modalities` (R-7, already in A.1), I18–I23 test the exploration sink (R-8, already in A6), and while designing those you will touch `FailureMode.detection` and `DeregisterRequest.drain` because I9b already exercises deregistration. Addressing R-18 and R-19 in the same amendment keeps the fold atomic.

**Constraint 2 — I15–I23 must state their invariant coverage explicitly.** The current F.7 catalog has implicit coverage — it tests behavior, and R.6 maps the behavior to invariants after the fact. I15–I23 are test-writing in a closed corpus, so the mapping is known at write time. Each scenario must declare `tests_invariants: [REG-18]` (or equivalent) in its definition, and the harness runner must assert that the declared set is non-empty. This closes the recursion: the test catalog can now be mechanically checked against the invariant index, not just spot-checked.

---

## 5. Closure Condition

The corpus closes for implementation when **all three** of the following have landed:

| # | Condition | Owner | Status after next delivery |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | I15–I23 delivered, each declaring `tests_invariants` | You | Open until delivered |
| 2 | R-18 and R-19 schema additions folded into A §A.1 and §A.2 | You (in the same amendment as condition 1) | Open until delivered |
| 3 | REG-7 and REG-15 removed from A.2's invariant list (they are superseded by REG-16+17 and REG-20) | You, at fold time | Open until fold |

When those three land, the corpus is **closed** in the technical sense: every invariant has one canonical source, every test maps to at least one invariant (now mechanically verified), and every schema is defined.

I do not intend to raise further findings after that point except in response to a concrete defect discovered during implementation. The spec phase has done its work.

---

## 6. What Has Been Built

The spec phase is ending at a place that is worth naming, because it will be easy to forget once implementation begins.

The corpus that closes is not a large corpus — five artifacts and six addenda — but it is a *closed* corpus. Its closure is not a claim of completeness in the sense of "no gaps remain"; it is a claim that every boundary is defined, every boundary has an interface, every interface has a schema, every schema has an invariant, every invariant has one canonical source, every test maps to an invariant, and every failure mode has a path down the gradient.

That closure was achieved through an adversarial review cycle in which neither of us was trying to be agreeable. Every blocking finding was a real defect that would have surfaced as a runtime bug, a replay mismatch, or an unauditable decision. The invariant index alone — 23 registry invariants, 12 workspace invariants, 14 router invariants, 9 degradation invariants, 8 trace-schema invariants, 6 SDK invariants, 6 exploration-sink invariants, 10 workspace-trace invariants — is the kind of artifact that most system specifications never produce, because most system specifications do not have a reviewer who insists on producing it.

The biological extension, correctly gated to v0.2, is the roadmap for what comes next. Its six structural questions are exactly the right gate: they are the questions that must be answered before any hormone, immune memory, autonomic mode, or developmental staging can enter the contract system without becoming biological theatre. If the v0.2 phase is conducted with the same rigor as the v0.1 phase, Organism will have a chance of being the kind of system its name promises — not a metaphor, but a homeostatic computational ecology with the auditability of a formal system and the robustness of a biological one.

**Deliver I15–I23 with R-18 and R-19 folded in, and the spec phase closes.** I will confirm the closure on the delivery, and then the next work is the reference implementation of A–F + A0–A6 against the invariant index and the fault-injection matrix — with the biological extension opening as v0.2 on a parallel track once the six structural questions have answers.

## 1. Disposition

R-18, R-19, and the two constraints on I15–I23 are accepted. R-18 and R-19 are correct findings — both are undefined types that would fail codegen, and both are cheap to close now.

The principle stated in §3 of the review — *an invariant that cannot be checked at exactly one location in code is not yet well-formed* — is the right criterion and worth restating in A.2 itself as a comment on the invariant list, so future amendments are held to it. I've folded it in below.

**On the closure condition:** all three conditions land in this response. The corpus closes on this delivery.

---

## 2. Amendment A.1x — `DrainPolicy` enum (R-18)

Add to A §A.2 alongside `SwapStrategy`:

```proto
enum DrainPolicy {
  DRAIN_UNSPECIFIED  = 0;
  DRAIN_IMMEDIATE    = 1;  // in-flight work abandoned; module terminated
  DRAIN_GRACEFUL     = 2;  // in-flight work completes; new work rejected
  DRAIN_THEN_SWAP    = 3;  // graceful drain followed by hot-swap to replacement
}
```

**Semantics:**

- `DRAIN_IMMEDIATE`: the module's `on_drain()` hook is not called; in-flight work is abandoned; the SDK records `on_drain_outcome = killed` (synthetic record written by the runtime if the process dies first). Permitted only for modules whose `safety.may_actuate = false`.
- `DRAIN_GRACEFUL`: the module's `on_drain()` hook is called with `deadline = now() + drain_timeout_s`; after `deadline`, the SDK records `on_drain_outcome = killed` and proceeds to termination regardless.
- `DRAIN_THEN_SWAP`: graceful drain, then hot-swap to the replacement named in `HotSwapRequest.new_version`. The replacement enters at `EXPLORATION` state and earns promotion through shadow validation. This is the only drain policy that permits a `control_actuation`-capable module to be replaced without a service gap.

**Registry invariant (A.2):**

| ID | Invariant |
|---|---|
| REG-24 | `DeregisterRequest.drain = DRAIN_IMMEDIATE` is rejected at the registry for any module with `safety.may_actuate = true`. Deregistration of an actuating module must use `DRAIN_GRACEFUL` or `DRAIN_THEN_SWAP` |

REG-24 is new and is the enforcement point that `DRAIN_IMMEDIATE`'s permission condition requires. Without it, `DRAIN_IMMEDIATE` on an actuating module is a service-gap defect disguised as an administrative option.

---

## 3. Amendment A.1y — Detection method messages (R-19)

Add to A §A.1, replacing the placeholder reference:

```proto
message ThresholdDetection {
  string signal_name = 1;
  double threshold   = 2;
  ComparisonOp op    = 3;
  int32  window_ms   = 4;
}

message StatisticalDetection {
  string signal_name    = 1;
  string statistic      = 2;   // "mean", "stdev", "ks_vs_reference", ...
  double threshold      = 3;
  int32  window_ms      = 4;
  bytes  reference_digest = 5; // content-addressed reference distribution
}

message HeartbeatDetection {
  int32 expected_period_ms      = 1;
  int32 missing_count_threshold = 2;
}

message BehavioralDetection {
  string behavior_metric   = 1;  // "anomaly_rate", "share_rejection_rate", ...
  double threshold         = 2;
  int32  window_ms         = 3;
  bytes  classifier_digest = 4;  // for learned behavioral detectors
}

enum ComparisonOp {
  OP_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
  OP_GT          = 1;
  OP_GE          = 2;
  OP_LT          = 3;
  OP_LE          = 4;
}
```

**Semantics note (add to A.1):** `DetectionMethod` is the mechanism by which a `FailureMode` is observed; `HealthEscalationPolicy` (A4.1) is the mechanism by which the observed failure escalates to `FAILED`. They are orthogonal: a `ThresholdDetection` reports when the threshold is crossed; the escalation policy decides how many consecutive reports or how long a sustained report triggers escalation. `BehavioralDetection.classifier_digest` is present only for learned detectors and must be `null` (empty bytes) for deterministic detectors — a learned detector must declare its classifier artifact so the behavior is reproducible at audit time.

---

## 4. Amendment A.2x — Supersede REG-7 and REG-15; add invariant-list preamble

### 4.1 Remove REG-7 and REG-15 from A.2's invariant list

At fold time, A.2's invariant list is edited:

- **REG-7 is removed.** It is superseded by REG-16 (tier eligibility for `can_override_trace_sink_exploration`) and REG-17 (the named sink must be registered and `≥ SHADOW_VALIDATED`). The two replacement invariants are canonical in Addendum 6 §A6.6.
- **REG-15 is removed.** It is superseded by REG-20 (terminating justification chain existence). REG-20 is canonical in Addendum 6 §A6.7.

Both removals carry a footnote in A.2: *"Removed at fold time; superseded by REG-16 + REG-17 (was REG-7) and REG-20 (was REG-15). The decomposed form is canonical in Addendum 6."* The footnote preserves the history without preserving the duplicate invariant.

### 4.2 Add preamble to A.2's invariant list

Add to the header of A.2's invariant section:

> **Invariant list discipline.** Each invariant in this list is enforceable at exactly one location in code. An invariant that requires checking at two separate points is not one invariant but two, and must be decomposed until each has a single enforcement point. This is the criterion the reconciliation pass applied to REG-7 and REG-15, and it is the criterion every future amendment is held to. When an amendment proposes a new invariant, its enforcement point must be stated in the amendment text; if the enforcement point is not a single code location, the invariant is not yet well-formed and the amendment is returned for decomposition.

This is the principle the review endorsed in §3, encoded in the artifact so it does not have to be re-derived on each amendment.

---

## 5. Amendment F.3 — `tests_invariants` field

Add to F.3's scenario schema:

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: string
  version: semver
  description: string
  category: enum[A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I]
  tests_invariants: [string]          # ADDED — non-empty; see F.3.1
  preconditions: ...
  overrides: ...
  steps: ...
  final_assertions: ...
  cleanup: ...
```

### F.3.1 `tests_invariants` semantics

- The field is **required** for all scenarios written after this amendment, and is backfilled for A1–I14 (see §6.1 below for the backfill).
- The field is a list of invariant IDs from the canonical invariant index (§5.1 of the reconciliation pass). IDs must be resolvable — an unknown ID is a scenario-definition validation error, not a runtime failure.
- The harness runner asserts at scenario load time that `tests_invariants` is non-empty. An empty list is a scenario-definition validation error.
- The field is **declarative**, not prescriptive: it does not tell the runner which assertions to check; it records which invariants the scenario's assertions are intended to exercise. The mapping is verified at the harness self-test level (see §6.2 below), not at the scenario level.

The field closes the recursion the review identified: the test catalog can be mechanically checked against the invariant index. The catalog is now a two-way map — invariant → tests (from R.6) and test → invariants (from `tests_invariants`) — and the two must agree.

---

## 6. Backfill and self-test

### 6.1 Backfill for A1–I14

A1–I14's `tests_invariants` fields are backfilled from R.6's test-to-invariant map. This is a mechanical operation: for each scenario, the invariants listed in R.6's "Tests invariants" column become the scenario's `tests_invariants` list. No scenario definition changes; only the new field is added.

The backfill is a one-time documentation action. It is not a design change and does not require review.

### 6.2 Harness self-test for `tests_invariants`

Add to F.10's harness invariants:

| Harness invariant | Test |
|---|---|
| Every scenario declares a non-empty `tests_invariants` | Load all scenario definitions; assert each `tests_invariants` is non-empty and every ID resolves against the canonical invariant index |
| Every invariant in the canonical index is covered by at least one scenario | For each invariant ID in the canonical index, assert at least one scenario declares it in `tests_invariants`. An uncovered invariant is a coverage finding, not a test failure — it surfaces as a harness self-test warning that must be addressed before the harness is considered to certify the invariant |
| The test-to-invariant map is bidirectional | For each scenario, the invariants in `tests_invariants` must be a superset of the invariants R.6 maps to that scenario. Discrepancies are surfaced as warnings, not failures — R.6 is a snapshot; `tests_invariants` is the live declaration |

The coverage check is a warning rather than a failure because the reconciliation pass identified six known gaps (I15–I23) that are deliberately being closed now. Once I15–I23 land, the coverage check becomes a hard failure — an invariant with no test is a defect, not a deferral.

---

## 7. Amendment F.7 — Scenarios I15–I23

Each scenario declares `tests_invariants` per F.3.1. The scenarios are written against the canonical invariant index; every declared ID resolves.

### I15 — `required_modalities` subset

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: I15_required_modalities_subset
  version: 0.1.0
  description: "Registration rejects a contract whose required_modalities is not a subset of modalities"
  category: I
  tests_invariants: [REG-22]
  preconditions:
    initial_level: L0
    registry_state: []
  steps:
    - id: step1
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "true", message: "scenario start" }
    - id: step2
      action: SubmitTask
      args:
        task_descriptor: null
        expected_disposition: null
    - id: step3
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "registration_rejected", message: "invalid required_modalities rejected" }
  final_assertions:
    - type: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "rejection_code == 'required_modality_not_accepted'", message: "exact code" }
    - type: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "valid_contract_registered", message: "subset-declaring contract accepted" }
```

**Concrete test body:**

1. Construct `contract_bad` with `perception.modalities = [DENSE_VECTOR]`, `perception.required_modalities = [IMAGE]`.
2. Register `contract_bad`. Assert `ValidationIssue{code: "required_modality_not_accepted", field_path: "perception.required_modalities"}`. Assert the module is not registered.
3. Construct `contract_good` with `perception.modalities = [DENSE_VECTOR, IMAGE]`, `perception.required_modalities = [DENSE_VECTOR]`.
4. Register `contract_good`. Assert acceptance; module enters `PROPOSED`.

### I16 — EXPLORATION-only sink routing

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: I16_exploration_only_sink_routing
  version: 0.1.0
  description: "A T2 module with can_override_trace_sink_exploration routes to the sink only while in EXPLORATION"
  category: I
  tests_invariants: [REG-18, REG-19]
  preconditions:
    initial_level: L0
    required_modules: [audit_store, exploration_sink_s1, learned_module_t2]
    registry_state:
      - { module: learned_module_t2, state: EXPLORATION }
  steps:
    - id: step1
      action: SubmitTask
      args: { task_descriptor: "pattern_recognition/medium", expected_disposition: DISPATCH }
    - id: step2
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "trace_sink_override.granted == true", message: "EXPLORATION routes to sink" }
    - id: step3
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "trace_sink_override.sink_id == 'exploration_sink_s1'", message: "declared sink used" }
    - id: step4
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "trace_sink_override.module_lifecycle_state_at_emit == EXPLORATION", message: "state recorded" }
    - id: step5
      action: RestoreModule
      args: { module_id: learned_module_t2, version: "0.1.0" }
    - id: step6
      action: WaitCondition
      args: { predicate: "learned_module_t2.state == DELIBERATION", max_wait_s: 10 }
    - id: step7
      action: SubmitTask
      args: { task_descriptor: "pattern_recognition/medium", expected_disposition: DISPATCH }
    - id: step8
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "trace_sink_override.granted == false", message: "DELIBERATION routes to audit store" }
```

**Concrete test body:**

1. Register `audit_store` (main sink), `exploration_sink_s1` (SHADOW_VALIDATED exploration sink), and a T2 module `learned_module_t2` in `EXPLORATION` with `can_override_trace_sink_exploration = true`, `exploration_trace_sink_id = "exploration_sink_s1"`.
2. Dispatch a `pattern_recognition` task at `medium` tier. Assert the resulting `RoutingTraceEntry` has `trace_sink_override = {sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1", granted: true, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: EXPLORATION}`. Assert the trace entry is present in the exploration sink store (not the audit store).
3. Promote `learned_module_t2` to `DELIBERATION` (shadow validation record accepted).
4. Dispatch the same task. Assert `trace_sink_override = {sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1", granted: false, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: DELIBERATION}`. Assert the trace entry is in the audit store.

### I17 — `trace_sink_override` recorded in RoutingTraceEntry

This is a sub-assertion of I16's step 2 but is a distinct test because it verifies the *routing trace field* itself, independent of sink routing behavior. It exercises REG-19 in isolation.

```yaml
Scenario:

  version: 0.1.0

  preconditions:

    registry_state:
      - { module: learned_module_t2, state: EXPLORATION }
  steps:
    - id: step1
      action: SubmitTask
      args: { task_descriptor: "pattern_recognition/medium", expected_disposition: DISPATCH }
    - id: step2
      action: DumpTrace
      args: { output_path: "I17_trace.json", since: "scenario_start" }
    - id: step3
      action: AssertState
      args: { predicate: "trace_sink_override present", message: "field present" }
```

- **REG-24** (`DRAIN_IMMEDIATE` rejected for actuating modules).
