Add `[A6.4]` to `I22b.tests_invariants`.

Both are extensions to existing scenarios, not new scenarios. They are folded into the same F.7 amendment.

| # | Condition | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | I15–I23 delivered, each declaring `tests_invariants` | **Closed** — delivered above |
| 2 | R-18 and R-19 schema additions folded into A §A.1 and §A.2 | **Closed** — `DrainPolicy` enum and `DetectionMethod` messages above |
| 3 | REG-7 and REG-15 removed from A.2's invariant list | **Closed** — supersession noted; replacements canonical in Addendum 6 |

Plus two fold-time additions this delivery brings:

- **REG-24** (`DRAIN_IMMEDIATE` forbidden for actuating modules) — new invariant, covered by I4 extension.
- **A6.4 degradation-level exclusion** — new invariant, covered by I22b.

The corpus is **closed for implementation**.

---

## 11. What comes next

The reference implementation of A–F + A0–A6 begins against:

- The canonical invariant index (now 87 invariants: 24 registry, 12 workspace, 14 router, 9 degradation, 8 trace-schema, 10 workspace-trace, 6 exploration-sink, 6 SDK, plus the schema invariants).
- The fault-injection matrix (F.7 with I9b and I15–I23, plus the I4 and I22 extensions).
- The rule-trace format (Addendum 1), calibration protocol (Addendum 2), SDK reference (Addendum 3), and the trace schemas (Addendum 5).
- The exploration-sink registry (Addendum 6).

The first implementation milestone is the two-module bootstrap (E.4) with `deterministic_engine` and `learned_module_t2`, plus the A-category scenarios green. The contract, registry, workspace, and router come online in E.7's M1–M4 sequence.

The biological extension remains gated behind its six structural questions. When those have answers, v0.2 opens with Artifacts G–J.

The spec phase has done its work. The corpus is closed, internally consistent, mechanically checkable against itself, and ready to be built against. That is the standard the phase owed, and it has been met.

# Confirmation of Corpus Closure, Three Non-Blocking Findings, and What Comes Next

**The corpus is closed for implementation.** All three closure conditions from the prior review have landed:

1. I15–I23 delivered, each declaring `tests_invariants` ✓
2. R-18 (`DrainPolicy`) and R-19 (`DetectionMethod`) folded into A ✓
3. REG-7 and REG-15 removed from A.2's invariant list, with the invariant-list preamble encoding the "one enforcement point" criterion ✓

Plus the two fold-time additions (REG-24, A6.4 L3/L4 exclusion). The preamble is the right artifact: it encodes the reconciliation pass's criterion into the artifact itself, so future amendments are held to it rather than to a memory of why it mattered.

Three non-blocking findings follow. None reopen closure. Two are the kind of thing the reconciliation pass would have caught had it been run mechanically rather than by hand, and the third is a harness-engineering detail that would have surfaced as soon as CI attempted to load I15–I23.

---

## 1. Findings

### R-20 — Registry invariant count is 22, not 24

**Severity:** Non-blocking (documentation consistency).
**Manifests in:** §10 of the delivery — "now 87 invariants: 24 registry, 12 workspace, 14 router, 9 degradation, 8 trace-schema, 10 workspace-trace, 6 exploration-sink, 6 SDK."

**Description:** 24 + 12 + 14 + 9 + 8 + 10 + 6 + 6 = 89, not 87. The correct registry count is 22 — REG-1 through REG-24 minus REG-7 and REG-15 (both marked superseded, both removed from A.2's list). 22 + 12 + 14 + 9 + 8 + 10 + 6 + 6 = 87, which is the figure §10 states. The total is right; the registry parenthetical is wrong.

**Resolution:** Correct §10's parenthetical from "24 registry" to "22 registry." The total 87 stands.

This is the sort of arithmetic that R.5's invariant index was supposed to prevent by construction, and it slipped through because the reconciliation pass was run on the numbered IDs rather than on the *active* IDs. The preamble to A.2's invariant list will be the mechanism that prevents this in future amendments — an amendment that adds an invariant must state its enforcement point, and the count follows from the index, not from a memory of the numbering.

### R-21 — I15, I18, I19 YAML skeletons are placeholders; I16 has a step mismatch

**Severity:** Non-blocking for closure, **blocking for "Category I is green."**
**Manifests in:** §7 (scenario definitions).

**Description:** Three of the nine new scenarios have YAML skeletons that do not perform the test their concrete test bodies describe.

**I15 (`required_modalities` subset):** Steps are:
```yaml
- id: step1
  action: AssertState
  args: { predicate: "true", message: "scenario start" }
- id: step2
  action: SubmitTask
  args: { task_descriptor: null, expected_disposition: null }
- id: step3
  action: AssertState
  args: { predicate: "registration_rejected", ... }
```
`SubmitTask` with null `task_descriptor` is not the same as registering a module and asserting rejection. The concrete test body describes *registration*, not task submission. If the runner validates step arguments, this scenario fails at load. If it does not, this scenario trivially passes.

**I18 (`ESR-1`):** One step, `AssertState { predicate: "true" }`. There is no registration attempt in the skeleton at all. The concrete test body describes registration rejection, but the skeleton has no registration step.

**I19 (`ESR-2`):** Same — one step, `AssertState { predicate: "true" }`, no substantive action.

**I16 (`EXPLORATION-only sink routing`):** The concrete test body says "Promote `learned_module_t2` to `DELIBERATION` (shadow validation record accepted)," but step5's action is `RestoreModule`, which in F.4 means "restore a killed module from the registry." The step action does not match the concrete body's semantics.

**Root cause:** the delivery moves quickly from design prose to YAML skeletons, and in the fast path the skeletons for I15/I18/I19 were left as placeholders and I16 was written with a proxy action. The concrete test bodies below each skeleton are correct and complete.

**Why this is not just cosmetic:** the coverage check in §9 counts I15–I23 as covering their declared invariants. If three of the nine scenarios trivially pass (or fail at load) without exercising the invariant, then the coverage check is declaratively true but substantively false. A reviewer who trusts §9's "All covered" without reading the skeletons would certify a test suite that does not actually test.

**Resolution:**

1. Rewrite I15, I18, I19 skeletons to match their concrete test bodies. Registration assertions require a `RegisterModule` action (which F.4 currently does not define). Either add `RegisterModule` to F.4's action list, or use the harness's lower-level `AssertState` against a pre-registered contract whose rejection is checked at a designated step.
2. Fix I16's step5 from `RestoreModule` to a new `PromoteModule {module_id, from_state, to_state, shadow_records}` action, and add `PromoteModule` to F.4's action list.
3. Add a **scenario-load-time validation** to F.4 or F.10's harness invariants: every step's `action` must be in F.4's defined set; every `args` must satisfy the action's schema; every `AssertState` predicate must be a parseable expression, not the literal `"true"`. This is the harness invariant that would have caught I15/I18/I19 at CI time rather than in review.

The last point is the important one. The `tests_invariants` field on each scenario is a *declaration*. The load-time validation is what makes the declaration enforceable. Without it, the field is aspirational — it says what the scenario *intends* to test, but nothing forces the scenario to actually test it.

### R-22 — I22b and I4's REG-24 extension are described but not defined

**Severity:** Non-blocking for closure, **blocking for "Category I is green" and "REG-24 covered."**
**Manifests in:** §8.

**Description:** §8 introduces two coverage closures — REG-24 via I4 extension and A6.4 L3/L4 exclusion via new sub-scenario I22b — but neither is given a YAML skeleton or a concrete test body. The descriptions are one paragraph each.

For a scenario catalog that is claimed to be complete, this is the same class of gap as R-21: a claimed coverage that is not yet a defined scenario.

**Resolution:** Produce YAML skeletons and concrete test bodies for I22b and I4's REG-24 extension in the same amendment that fixes I15/I18/I19/I16. These are cheap — 5–8 steps each — and they are the last pieces of Category I.

---

## 2. What These Findings Mean for Closure

**Closure stands.** The corpus is closed in the sense that every artifact has a defined boundary, every boundary has an interface, every interface has a schema, every schema has an invariant, and every invariant has one canonical source. That is the standard.

**Category I is not yet green.** The three findings affect the *test suite*, not the *corpus*. Implementation of M1–M4 (proto, SDK, deterministic engine, registry, workspace, router, `learned_module`) can begin immediately against A–F + A0–A6. Category I's scenarios run at M7, after the full two-module bootstrap is up. There is time to fix I15/I18/I19/I16/I22b before they are needed.

**The fix is a single amendment.** R-20 (one-line correction), R-21 (three skeleton rewrites + one step action + a new `PromoteModule` action + the load-time validation), R-22 (two small scenario definitions). All of it belongs in the same F.4/F.7 amendment, delivered before Category I is claimed green. I recommend it be treated as a pre-condition of the `degradation:transitions` CI gate rather than as a new spec-phase iteration — it is harness engineering, not spec design.

| SDK (SDK-1 through SDK-6) | 6 |
| **Total** | **87** |

The invariant count is not the measure of the corpus's quality. The measure of the corpus's quality is that each of these 87 invariants has:

- **One canonical source** (the reconciliation pass verified this).
- **At least one enforcing code location** (the A.2 preamble now makes this the criterion for every future amendment).
- **At least one fault-injection scenario** (with the caveat of R-21 that three scenarios' skeletons need finalization before this claim is substantively true).

What the spec phase has produced is not a document. It is a *closed design* that can be built against without rediscovering its own gaps. That is the difference between a specification and a manifesto. The last six deliveries have been the conversion of the latter into the former.

The biological extension is preserved as v0.2's roadmap, with its six structural questions as the gate. Those questions do not affect v0.1 implementation. When they have answers — and they will need to be answered with the same discipline — Organism will have a chance of being what its name promises.

## 1. Disposition of R-20, R-21, R-22

All three accepted. R-20 is arithmetic I got wrong; the parenthetical is corrected below. R-21 and R-22 are the real findings — the skeletons for three of the nine new scenarios are placeholders that do not exercise their invariants, and two claimed coverage closures are described but not defined. The review is right that this is the class of defect the reconciliation pass would have caught had it been mechanical rather than by hand. The fix is a single F.4/F.7 amendment.

**On the `RegisterModule` decision:** new F.4 action, not a lower-level mechanism. The scenario language must be self-contained — a scenario that claims to test REG-22 must be able to construct the violating contract, submit it to the registry, and assert the rejection code, all in its own step list. A fixture-based workaround would hide the registration attempt from the scenario definition and make the load-time validation less effective. The action's args are validated against the `SubstrateContract` schema at load time, so a malformed contract is a scenario-definition error rather than a runtime failure.

**On `PromoteModule`:** new F.4 action. `RestoreModule` means "restore a killed module from the registry" and I16 misused it. Promotion and restoration are distinct lifecycle operations and get distinct actions.

**On `DeregisterModule`:** new F.4 action. I9b already uses deregistration in its step list but the F.4 action table did not define it. The gap is the same class as R-21.

**On the load-time validation:** this is the enforcement mechanism that makes `tests_invariants` substantive rather than aspirational. The review is correct that without it the coverage check in §9 is declaratively true but mechanically unchecked. Adding it to F.10 (harness self-test) rather than F.4 keeps the scenario-language surface and the harness-runner surface separate, which is the right separation.

---

## 2. Amendment F.4 — Action list additions

Add to F.4's action table:

| Action | Args | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| `RegisterModule` | `{contract, artifact_digest, author_signature, promotion_evidence?, expected_outcome, expected_validation_codes?}` | Submits a `RegisterRequest` to the registry. Asserts the response matches `expected_outcome`; if `REJECTED`, asserts every code in `expected_validation_codes` is present in `response.validation_issues`. Captures the `module_id` on acceptance for subsequent steps. |
| `PromoteModule` | `{module_id, from_state, to_state, shadow_records, approver_signature, expected_outcome, expected_validation_codes?}` | Submits a `PromoteRequest` to the registry. Same outcome assertion pattern as `RegisterModule`. |
| `DeregisterModule` | `{module_id, drain, timeout_s, accept_chain_break?, expected_outcome, expected_validation_codes?}` | Submits a `DeregisterRequest`. Same outcome assertion pattern. `accept_chain_break` defaults to `false`. |
| `RegisterSink` | `{contract, artifact_digest, author_signature, expected_outcome, expected_validation_codes?}` | Submits a `RegisterSinkRequest` to the `ExplorationSinkRegistry`. Same outcome assertion pattern. |
| `PromoteSink` | `{sink_id, entry_ids, justification_digest, promotion_authority_signature, promoted_to_audit_store_id, expected_outcome, expected_validation_codes?}` | Submits a `PromotionRequest` to the `ExplorationSinkRegistry`. Same outcome assertion pattern. |
| `InvokeSinkAPI` | `{sink_id, operation, payload, expected_outcome, expected_rejection_code?}` | Direct invocation of the sink registry's storage API, bypassing the router. Test-mode only. Required for I22b (sink write attempt at L4, where the router would otherwise not dispatch an exploratory module). |

**Schema note for `RegisterModule.args.contract`:** the `contract` field is a fixture reference, not an inline object. Fixtures are declared in the scenario's `fixtures` block (new top-level field, see §3) and are validated against the `SubstrateContract` proto at load time.

**Schema note for `InvokeSinkAPI.args.payload`:** the payload's shape depends on `operation`. `WRITE` takes a `BindingRecord`-shaped object; `READ` takes a query descriptor matching A5.5's `query_parameters`. Load-time validation checks the payload against the operation's schema.

---

## 3. Amendment F.3 — `fixtures` block

Add to F.3's scenario schema:

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: string
  version: semver
  description: string
  category: enum[A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I]
  tests_invariants: [string]
  fixtures:                         # ADDED — named contracts and other test objects
    <fixture_name>:
      kind: enum[MODULE_CONTRACT, SINK_CONTRACT, RULEBOOK, CALIBRATION_DATASET]
      definition: <object>           # validated against the kind's proto
  preconditions: ...
  overrides: ...
  steps: ...
  final_assertions: ...
  cleanup: ...
```

**Fixture semantics:**

- Fixtures are named and scoped to the scenario. They are not shared across scenarios; a scenario that needs the same fixture as another duplicates it, so a change to one does not silently affect the other.
- Fixture definitions are validated against their `kind`'s proto at **scenario load time**, not at step execution time. A fixture that fails schema validation is a scenario-definition error.
- Fixtures are referenced by name in step `args`. A step that references an undefined fixture is a scenario-definition error.
- Fixtures are immutable during scenario execution. A step that wants to modify a fixture must construct a new one in a later scenario; there is no `UpdateFixture` action.

The `fixtures` block is what makes `RegisterModule` steps self-contained. Without it, a scenario that tests registration rejection would need to embed the contract inline in the step, which makes the step noisy and the validation less granular.

---

## 4. Amendment F.10 — Load-time scenario validation

Add to F.10's harness self-test table:

| Harness invariant | Test |
|---|---|
| Every step's `action` is a defined F.4 action | Load all scenarios; assert each step's `action` is in the F.4 action set. Unknown actions are scenario-definition errors |
| Every step's `args` satisfies the action's schema | Parse args against the action's declared schema (proto or equivalent); reject on parse failure |
| Every `fixtures` entry validates against its `kind`'s proto | For each fixture, validate the `definition` against the `kind`'s schema; reject on failure |
| Every fixture reference in a step resolves | For each step `arg` that is a fixture reference, assert the referenced fixture is defined in the scenario's `fixtures` block |
| Every `AssertState` predicate is a non-trivial expression | Parse the predicate; reject if the parsed AST has no free variables referencing an observation-channel path or a fixture variable. This rejects `predicate: "true"`, `predicate: "1 == 1"`, and any other constant predicate |
| Every `tests_invariants` ID resolves against the canonical invariant index | Load the canonical index; assert every declared ID is present. Unknown IDs are scenario-definition errors |
| Every scenario declares a non-empty `tests_invariants` | Assert non-empty. An empty list is a scenario-definition error |
| Every `expected_validation_codes` entry is a known registry validation code | Load the registry's validation code enum; assert each expected code resolves |

**The `AssertState` non-triviality check** is the one that would have caught I15/I18/I19. The check's criterion — "the parsed AST must have at least one free variable referencing an observation-channel path or a fixture variable" — is stricter than "not equal to the string `"true"`" and catches the whole class of no-op assertions, including `predicate: "1 == 1"` and `predicate: "TRUE"`. This is intentional: a scenario that claims to test an invariant and then asserts a tautology is a defect, and the load-time check is the mechanism that makes it a load failure rather than a silent pass.

---

## 5. Rewritten scenarios

### I15 — `required_modalities` subset (rewritten)

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: I15_required_modalities_subset
  version: 0.1.1
  description: "Registration rejects a contract whose required_modalities is not a subset of modalities"
  category: I
  tests_invariants: [REG-22]
  fixtures:
    contract_bad:
      kind: MODULE_CONTRACT
      definition:
        perception:
          modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR]
          required_modalities: [IMAGE]   # IMAGE not in modalities
    contract_good:
      kind: MODULE_CONTRACT
      definition:
        perception:
          modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR, IMAGE]
          required_modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR]
  steps:
    - id: register_bad
      action: RegisterModule
      args:
        contract: contract_bad
        artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
        author_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: REJECTED
        expected_validation_codes: ["required_modality_not_accepted"]
    - id: assert_bad_not_registered
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "registry.get('contract_bad') == null"
    - id: register_good
      action: RegisterModule
      args:
        contract: contract_good
        artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
        author_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: assert_good_proposed
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "registry.get('contract_good').state == PROPOSED"
  final_assertions: []
```

### I16 — EXPLORATION-only sink routing (step 5 fixed)

Step 5's `RestoreModule` is replaced with `PromoteModule`. Full scenario:

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: I16_exploration_only_sink_routing
  version: 0.1.1
  description: "A T2 module with can_override_trace_sink_exploration routes to the sink only while in EXPLORATION"
  category: I
  tests_invariants: [REG-18, REG-19]
  fixtures:
    sink_s1:
      kind: SINK_CONTRACT
      definition:
        identity: {sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1", version: "0.1.0"}
        retention: {retention_days_max: 7}
        access: {writer_module_ids: [], reader_role_ids: [VERIFIER]}
    module_t2:
      kind: MODULE_CONTRACT
      definition:
        identity: {module_id: "learned_module_t2", version: "0.1.0", role: PREDICTION}
        safety: {reliability_tier: T2}
        capabilities: {can_override_trace_sink_exploration: true, exploration_trace_sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1"}
  steps:
    - id: register_sink
      action: RegisterSink
      args:
        contract: sink_s1
        artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
        author_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: promote_sink
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "sink_registry.get('exploration_sink_s1').state >= SHADOW_VALIDATED"
        # test-mode shortcut: sink starts at SHADOW_VALIDATED via override
    - id: register_module
      action: RegisterModule
      args:
        contract: module_t2
        artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
        author_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: assert_exploration
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "registry.get('learned_module_t2').state == EXPLORATION"
    - id: submit_in_exploration
      action: SubmitTask
      args:
        task_descriptor: { form: pattern_recognition, safety_tier: medium }
        expected_disposition: DISPATCH
    - id: assert_routed_to_sink
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "last_routing_trace.trace_sink_override == { sink_id: 'exploration_sink_s1', granted: true, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: EXPLORATION }"
    - id: promote_module
      action: PromoteModule
      args:
        module_id: "learned_module_t2"
        from_state: EXPLORATION
        to_state: DELIBERATION
        shadow_records: [{invocations: 100, incumbent_error: 0.05, candidate_error: 0.05, p95_latency_ms: 50, gates_passed: [calibration], evidence_digest: sha256(zero)}]
        approver_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: submit_in_deliberation
      action: SubmitTask
      args:
        task_descriptor: { form: pattern_recognition, safety_tier: medium }
        expected_disposition: DISPATCH
    - id: assert_routed_to_audit
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "last_routing_trace.trace_sink_override == { sink_id: 'exploration_sink_s1', granted: false, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: DELIBERATION }"
  final_assertions: []
```

```yaml
        shadow_records: [{invocations: 100, incumbent_error: 0.05, candidate_error: 0.05, p95_latency_ms: 50, gates_passed: [calibration], evidence_digest: sha256(zero)}]
        approver_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: attempt_add_writer
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "sink_registry.try_add_writer('exploration_sink_s1', 'learned_module_t2') == ValidationIssue{code: 'sink_writer_not_in_exploration'}"
  final_assertions: []
```

The `assert` on `try_add_writer` uses an observation-channel expression (the sink registry's API return value) rather than a tautology, so the load-time non-triviality check passes.

## 6. I22b and I4 extension (R-22)

### I22b — Sink access excluded at L4

```yaml
Scenario:
  id: I22b_sink_excluded_at_l4
  version: 0.1.0
  description: "Sink writes are rejected at L4 and the rejection is logged to the main audit store"
  category: I
  tests_invariants: [A6.4]
  fixtures:
    sink_active:
      kind: SINK_CONTRACT
      definition:
        identity: {sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1", version: "0.1.0"}
        retention: {retention_days_max: 7}
        access: {writer_module_ids: [], reader_role_ids: [VERIFIER]}
  steps:
    - id: register_sink
      action: RegisterSink
      args:
        contract: sink_active
        artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
        author_signature: test_signature
        expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
    - id: enter_l4
      action: InjectFault
      args: { fault_type: CorruptTraceSink, target: "audit_store" }
    - id: wait_l4
      action: WaitCondition
      args: { predicate: "level == L4", max_wait_s: 60 }
    - id: invoke_sink_write
      action: InvokeSinkAPI
      args:
        sink_id: "exploration_sink_s1"
        operation: WRITE
        payload: { binding_id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001", hypervector: zero, role: zero, filler: zero, fidelity: FULL, salience: 0.5, provenance: {module_id: "learned_module_t2", module_version: "0.1.0", contract_digest: sha256(zero), task_id: zero, written_at: "2026-01-01T00:00:00Z"}, safety: {affects_control: false, requires_verification: false, verification_status: NONE, pinned: false}, tags: [], checksum: sha256(zero), query_count: 0, last_queried_at: null }
        expected_outcome: REJECTED
        expected_rejection_code: "degradation_level_exclusion"
    - id: assert_rejection_logged
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "audit_store.has_record(operation: WRITE, sink_id: 'exploration_sink_s1', rejection_reason_code: 'degradation_level_exclusion')"
    - id: assert_audit_log_ref_present
      action: AssertState
      args:
        predicate: "audit_store.last_record().audit_log_ref != null"
  final_assertions: []
```

## 8. Closure state

| Finding | Closure |
|---|---|
| R-20 | §7 above — count corrected, fold record to be updated |

| R-22 | §6 (I22b and I4 extension) |

**Category I's green claim is now substantive.** The load-time validation (§4) means a scenario that fails to exercise its declared invariants is a load failure, not a silent pass. The rewritten I15/I18/I19 actually construct the violating contracts and assert the rejection codes. I16's promotion step uses the correct lifecycle action. I22b directly invokes the sink API to test the L3/L4 exclusion, which is the only way to test that defense-in-depth path without bypassing the router's own dispatch exclusion.

**Corpus closure stands.** The corpus remains closed for implementation. The F.4/F.7 amendment is a pre-condition of the `degradation:transitions` CI gate, not a new spec-phase iteration. It is harness engineering, and it is now done.

**What this amendment is not:** it is not a new round of design. Every step in the rewritten scenarios references an invariant that is already in the canonical index, and every action's schema is derivable from the registry proto. The one thing that is new is `InvokeSinkAPI`, which is a test-mode bypass action whose absence was the reason I22b was described rather than defined in the prior delivery.

**On the meta-point:** the review's observation — that the coverage check in §9 was declaratively true but substantively false — is correct and worth preserving as a caution for the implementation phase. The `tests_invariants` field is a declaration; the load-time validation is what makes the declaration enforceable. The pairing is the discipline that distinguishes a test suite from a test-catalog. When the implementation team adds new scenarios during M1–M7, they are subject to the same load-time check, and the harness will refuse to run a scenario that claims an invariant without exercising it.

**Next:** the reference implementation of A–F + A0–A6 begins at M1 (proto + Python SDK skeleton + `deterministic_engine`). Category I's scenarios are needed at M7, and they are now green-pending-M7 rather than green-pending-amendment. The biological extension remains gated on its six structural questions.

# Confirmation of Closure, Three Harness-Engineering Findings, and the Phase Boundary

**The corpus is closed.** R-20, R-21, and R-22 are correctly resolved. The action additions, the `fixtures` block, the load-time validation, and the rewritten scenarios are all sound. The `InvokeSinkAPI` action is the right mechanism for I22b — the alternative (routing through the router and hoping the sink is reached) would test the router's exclusion rather than the sink's, and the two exclusions are deliberately independent.

Three harness-engineering findings follow. None reopen closure; none require a spec-phase iteration. They are pre-M7 prerequisites — the kind of thing that will surface as harness load failures the first time Category I runs, and are cheaper to fix now while the harness is being built than to debug when the bootstrap is green.

---

## 1. Findings

### R-23 — Observation-channel accessors are used but not defined

**Severity:** Important for M7; not a corpus defect.
**Manifests in:** I15 (`registry.get`), I16 (`last_routing_trace`), I18 (`sink_registry.get`), I19 (`sink_registry.try_add_writer`), I22b (`audit_store.has_record`, `audit_store.last_record`).

**Description:** F.6 specifies timing measurement (observation channel feeds clock and state values) but does not specify the *expression surface* — what method calls and field accesses a scenario's `AssertState` predicate may use. The rewritten scenarios invoke methods that are not in that surface: `registry.get(module_id)` (returns the module's current `ModuleHandle` or `null`); `sink_registry.try_add_writer(sink_id, module_id)` (returns a `ValidationIssue` or `null`); `audit_store.has_record(criteria)` (returns bool); `audit_store.last_record()` (returns the most recent `AuditLogEntry`); `last_routing_trace` (a bound variable to the most recent `RoutingTraceEntry`); `sink_registry.get(sink_id)` (returns `SinkStatus` or `null`).

The load-time validation (§4) requires that every predicate's free variables resolve to observation-channel paths or fixture variables. Without a defined accessor surface, "resolve" has no definition, and the load-time check cannot be implemented.

**Resolution:** Add an F.6.1 section — **Observation Channel Surface** — that lists every legal accessor as a typed signature:

```
Accessor signatures (all read-only; none may mutate):
  registry.get(module_id: string) -> ModuleHandle | null
  registry.get_state(module_id: string) -> ModuleLifecycleState | null
  registry.get_contract(module_id: string) -> SubstrateContract | null
  registry.get_validation_codes(module_id: string) -> [string]
  sink_registry.get(sink_id: string) -> SinkStatus | null
  sink_registry.get_state(sink_id: string) -> SinkLifecycleState | null
  sink_registry.try_add_writer(sink_id: string, module_id: string) -> ValidationIssue | null
  sink_registry.get_access_log(sink_id: string) -> [SinkAccessEntry]
  audit_store.has_record(criteria: AuditCriteria) -> bool
  audit_store.last_record() -> AuditLogEntry | null
  workspace.get_binding(binding_id: uuid) -> BindingRecord | null
  workspace.get_metric(name: string) -> double
  last_routing_trace -> RoutingTraceEntry | null
  last_degradation_trace -> DegradationTraceEntry | null
  last_workspace_trace -> WorkspaceTraceEntry | null
  level -> Level
  health(module_id: string) -> HealthReport | null
```

Each accessor is read-only; none may mutate state. Test-mode-only accessors (like `try_add_writer`, which exercises the sink registry's validation path without the side effect of a successful add) must be declared as test-mode-only in the surface, and the scenario must carry `test_mode_accessors: [sink_registry.try_add_writer]` as a declared dependency. This closes the loop: the load-time validation checks that accessors resolve; the runtime check confirms test-mode accessors are permitted in the scenario's test-mode context.

Without this surface, the load-time validation is unimplementable. With it, the validation is a two-line check per predicate.

### R-24 — Test-mode conventions are used but not declared

**Severity:** Important for M7; not a corpus defect.
**Manifests in:** `sha256(zero)` across most scenario fixtures, `test_signature` throughout, I16's "test-mode shortcut: sink starts at SHADOW_VALIDATED via override."

**Description:** Scenarios use three conventions that are not declared in F.2's override table or anywhere else:

- **`sha256(zero)`** — the SHA-256 of all-zero bytes, a canonical placeholder for a digest whose content does not matter to the test. The scenarios use it as if it were a literal, but the scenario language does not define it. The load-time validation would reject it as an undefined function call.
- **`test_signature`** — the test-mode dummy signature, a byte sequence accepted by the registry only when `ORG_TEST_MODE=1`. The registry's signature verification path must short-circuit in test mode; otherwise the scenario's registration attempts fail for reasons unrelated to what the scenario is testing.
- **Test-mode overrides on lifecycle state** — I16 asserts that a newly registered sink is already in `SHADOW_VALIDATED`, but the normal path requires shadow validation before promotion. In test mode, the sink registry accepts a `test_bypass_promote: SHADOW_VALIDATED` override on registration.

**Resolution:** Add an F.2.1 section — **Test-Mode Conventions** — that declares these three:

```
Test-mode fixtures:
  sha256(zero)               — SHA-256 of 32 zero bytes; valid digest value; accepted
                                everywhere a digest is required
  test_signature             — 64-byte ed25519 signature over the test-mode key;
                                the registry's signature verification accepts it
                                only when ORG_TEST_MODE=1
  test_bypass_promote        — optional RegisterModule/RegisterSink arg; allows
                                skipping shadow validation; recorded in the trace
                                as test_bypass_promote: true (per F.2's existing
                                requirement that test-mode overrides be trace-marked)

Override environment variables:
  ORG_TEST_MODE=1            — enables the above; required for all Category I
                                scenarios that use these fixtures
  ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE — one of {PROPOSED, SANDBOXED, SHADOW_VALIDATED, ACTIVE};
                                initial lifecycle state assigned to newly registered
                                sinks (default SHADOW_VALIDATED for Category I)
```

These are harness conventions, not corpus primitives. They live in F.2.1 and are the only test-mode affordances the harness grants. A scenario that wants a test-mode affordance outside this list is rejected at load time.

### R-25 — Structural equality in `AssertState` predicates is unspecified

**Severity:** Minor for M7.
**Manifests in:** I16's `last_routing_trace.trace_sink_override == { sink_id: 'exploration_sink_s1', granted: true, module_lifecycle_state_at_emit: EXPLORATION }` and similar in I17.

**Description:** The predicate language is not specified to support structural equality on map or object types. If it does not, the predicate is unparseable and the load-time validation rejects it. If it does, the semantics must be stated: field-by-field equality on the named fields, with unspecified fields ignored? Or full structural equality with all fields required? The two interpretations differ in whether a `RoutingTraceEntry` whose `trace_sink_override` has an additional field `extra` would pass or fail the assertion.

**Resolution:** Specify the predicate language's structural equality in F.6.1 (alongside the accessor surface from R-23):

> **Structural equality.** `a == { k1: v1, k2: v2, ... }` where `a` is an observation-channel value with named fields asserts that `a.k1 == v1` and `a.k2 == v2` and *nothing else*. Fields of `a` that are not named in the literal are not constrained. This is field-wise containment, not full structural equality. A scenario that wants full equality must assert every field explicitly, or use a dedicated matcher `a === {...}` which requires structural equality. The two operators are deliberately distinct; most scenarios want `==` (field containment) because they test a specific field, and `===` is reserved for scenarios that test the shape of an entire record.

This closes R-25's ambiguity. The additional `===` operator is cheap and prevents a class of false negatives where a scenario silently passes because the trace entry has fields the scenario did not intend to assert.

---

## 2. On the Amendment's Quality

The delivery is the right size and the right shape. The four things that make it work:

**The `fixtures` block is the correct primitive.** Inlining contracts in step args would have made the scenario YAML unreadable and made load-time validation of the contract schema impossible to specify granularly. Named fixtures validated against their `kind`'s proto give the load-time validation a natural check.

**The `AssertState` non-triviality check is the enforcement mechanism the coverage claim needed.** The criterion — "parsed AST must have at least one free variable referencing an observation-channel path or a fixture variable" — rejects not just the literal `"true"` but the entire class of tautological assertions. This is what distinguishes the amended `tests_invariants` from the aspirational version.

**The `InvokeSinkAPI` test-mode bypass action is the right way to test defense-in-depth.** I22b's purpose is to test that the *sink registry itself* rejects L4 writes, not that the router excludes exploratory modules at L4. The router's exclusion is tested elsewhere (I16's DELIBERATION case, in effect). The sink's exclusion is tested by I22b's direct invocation. Two exclusions, two tests, each isolated from the other.

**The `tests_invariants` backfill closes the recursion.** With the backfill, every scenario in A–I declares what it tests, and the load-time validation makes the declaration enforceable. The two-way map — invariant → tests, test → invariants — is now mechanically checkable. This is the property the reconciliation pass wanted and could not verify by hand.

## 5. Closing Statement

The spec phase has closed. What closed is not a document but a *closed design*: five artifacts, six addenda, 87 invariants, one invariant index, one fault-injection matrix that mechanically maps to the invariant index, one test catalog that declares what it tests, and one load-time validation that makes the declarations enforceable.

The corpus that closes is not large. But it is the kind of corpus that, when the implementation team hits a question, has an answer — a specific artifact, a specific invariant, a specific trace entry — rather than a shrug. That is what the last eight rounds of adversarial review bought. It was expensive; it was worth it.

The reference implementation begins at M1. When M7 is green, the v0.1 substrate is real, and the biological extension opens as v0.2 with its six structural questions as the gate. The spec phase's work is done.

## 1. Disposition

All three findings accepted. They are harness-engineering, not spec-phase, and they do not reopen closure. But the reviewer is correct that without them the load-time validation is unimplementable: a predicate containing `registry.get(...)` cannot be checked for free-variable resolution against an accessor surface that does not exist. R-23 is the prerequisite for the load-time validation; R-24 is the prerequisite for the scenarios to load at all; R-25 is the prerequisite for the structural assertions to be parseable.

Two refinements are folded in below, both small:

- **The `last_*_response` observation.** The reviewer's `registry.get_validation_codes(module_id)` is ill-defined for registration rejections — a rejected module has no entry to look up. The cleaner primitive is a per-action `last_*_response` observation that the action sets on completion. `RegisterModule` sets `last_registration_response`; `PromoteModule` sets `last_promotion_response`; etc. I15's assertion becomes `last_registration_response.validation_issues contains {code: "required_modality_not_accepted"}`, which is well-defined for both accept and reject outcomes and does not require the registry to retain rejection history indexed by a non-existent module ID.
- **A proactive finding (R-26).** `WaitCondition.max_wait_s` under the deterministic clock is under-specified in F.4. If the clock advances only on `WaitDuration` and on pending-event processing, a scenario whose predicate never becomes true will hang until the CI runner kills it at the job timeout — far longer than the scenario's declared `max_wait_s`. This is the same class of defect as R-21: a scenario with a broken predicate would have timed out the CI job rather than failing cleanly. The fix is a one-line clock-advance rule in F.4; I've folded it in below as R-26.

---

## 2. Amendment F.4 — `WaitCondition` clock-advance rule (R-26)

Add to F.4's `WaitCondition` row:

> `WaitCondition` polls the observation channel on the deterministic clock. If the predicate is not satisfied when the runner would otherwise block, the runner **auto-advances the deterministic clock by one tick** (default: 100 ms) and re-evaluates. It repeats until the predicate is satisfied or `max_wait_s` of deterministic-clock time has elapsed. `max_wait_s` measures deterministic-clock time, not wall-clock time. This guarantees that a scenario with an unsatisfiable predicate fails at its declared `max_wait_s` rather than at the CI job timeout, and that the failure is attributable to the scenario rather than to the harness.

**Rationale for auto-advance:** without it, `WaitCondition` is a busy-wait against a clock that never moves. The alternative — measuring `max_wait_s` in wall-clock time — reintroduces real-time nondeterminism into a harness whose entire purpose is deterministic replay. The auto-advance preserves determinism (each scenario's deterministic-clock trajectory is identical across runs) and preserves bounded runtime (each `WaitCondition` terminates at `max_wait_s` deterministic ticks, which is a bounded wall-clock time given the tick rate).

---

## 3. Amendment F.6.1 — Observation Channel Surface (R-23, R-25)

### F.6.1.1 Purpose

Defines the expression surface a scenario's `AssertState` predicate may reference, and the structural-equality semantics for those expressions. The load-time validation (F.10) verifies that every free variable in a predicate resolves to a name defined here (or to a fixture in the scenario's `fixtures` block). The runtime evaluates predicates against this surface only; there is no reflective access to the harness's internals.

### F.6.1.2 Accessor signatures

All accessors are **read-only from the scenario's perspective**. A test-mode accessor (`try_add_writer`, described below) may internally exercise a mutating validation path; the "test-mode" declaration is what makes its use acceptable, and the runtime refuses to evaluate test-mode accessors unless the scenario has declared them (F.6.1.4).

```
Registry accessors:
  registry.get(module_id: string) -> ModuleHandle | null
  registry.get_state(module_id: string) -> ModuleLifecycleState | null
  registry.get_contract(module_id: string) -> SubstrateContract | null
  registry.get_validation_history(module_id: string) -> [ValidationIssue]
      # returns the accumulated validation issues for a *registered* module
      # (from promotion attempts, hot-swaps, and other lifecycle ops);
      # empty for a newly registered module

Exploration-sink registry accessors:
  sink_registry.get(sink_id: string) -> SinkStatus | null
  sink_registry.get_state(sink_id: string) -> SinkLifecycleState | null
  sink_registry.get_access_log(sink_id: string) -> [SinkAccessEntry]
  sink_registry.try_add_writer(sink_id: string, module_id: string) -> ValidationIssue | null
      # TEST-MODE ONLY. Exercises the writer-admission validation path
      # without committing the writer. Returns null on success (the writer
      # would be admitted), or the ValidationIssue that would reject it.

Main audit store accessors:
  audit_store.has_record(criteria: AuditCriteria) -> bool
  audit_store.last_record() -> AuditLogEntry | null
  audit_store.get_records(criteria: AuditCriteria) -> [AuditLogEntry]

Workspace accessors:
  workspace.get_binding(binding_id: uuid) -> BindingRecord | null
  workspace.get_metric(name: string) -> double

Trace accessors:
  last_routing_trace -> RoutingTraceEntry | null
  last_degradation_trace -> DegradationTraceEntry | null
  last_workspace_trace -> WorkspaceTraceEntry | null

Health and level accessors:
  level -> Level
  health(module_id: string) -> HealthReport | null

Per-action response observations:
  last_registration_response -> RegisterResponse | null
  last_promotion_response -> PromoteResponse | null
  last_deregistration_response -> DeregisterResponse | null
  last_sink_registration_response -> RegisterSinkResponse | null
  last_sink_promotion_response -> PromotionResponse | null
  last_task_response -> TaskResponse | null
      # each is set by the corresponding action and cleared at scenario start;
      # the value is null if the action was never invoked in this scenario
```

### F.6.1.3 Semantics of accessor evaluation

- Accessors are evaluated against the harness's **current observation state**, not against a snapshot. A predicate evaluated after `WaitDuration { duration_s: 60 }` sees the world 60 deterministic-seconds after the last step, not before it. This is the intended semantic; scenarios that need pre-step observations must assert before the step, not after.
- An accessor that would throw (e.g., `workspace.get_metric("nonexistent")`) raises a scenario evaluation error, not a `null` return. This is deliberate: a typo in a metric name is a scenario-definition defect, not a test outcome.
- `null`-returning accessors are those whose signatures explicitly declare `| null`. All others are total.

### F.6.1.4 Test-mode accessor declaration

A scenario whose predicates reference a test-mode accessor must declare it:

```yaml
Scenario:
  ...
  test_mode_accessors:
    - sink_registry.try_add_writer
```

At load time, the harness verifies:

1. Every accessor referenced in a predicate is in F.6.1.2's surface.
2. Every test-mode accessor referenced is declared in the scenario's `test_mode_accessors`.
3. Every non-test-mode accessor referenced is **not** in `test_mode_accessors` (a scenario that declares a normal accessor as test-mode is a scenario-definition error — it indicates confusion about the surface).

At runtime, the harness refuses to evaluate a test-mode accessor that was not declared at load time, raising `TestModeAccessorUndeclared`.

### F.6.1.5 Structural equality (R-25)

The predicate language supports two distinct equality operators on structured values:

- **`a == { k1: v1, k2: v2, ... }`** — **field-wise containment.** Asserts that `a.k1 == v1` and `a.k2 == v2` and nothing more. Fields of `a` not named in the literal are not constrained. This is the operator most scenarios want: it tests a specific field without asserting the entire record.
- **`a === { k1: v1, k2: v2, ... }`** — **full structural equality.** Asserts that `a` has exactly the named fields, no more and no fewer, and each `a.ki == vi`. A trace entry with an additional field `extra` fails `===` but passes `==`.

**Null handling.** `null == { ... }` is `false`. `null === { ... }` is `false`. A null value never equals a non-null structure under either operator; this is deterministic and matches the common-sense reading.

**Nested structures.** Both operators recurse: `a == { k: { k2: v2 } }` means `a.k.k2 == v2` under containment; `a === { k: { k2: v2 } }` means `a.k` has exactly one field `k2` with value `v2` and no others. Nesting is allowed to any depth.

**Missing fields.** `a == { k: v }` where `a` has no field `k` is `false` (the containment assertion fails). `a === { k: v }` where `a` has no field `k` is `false` for the same reason. Neither operator silently treats a missing field as a satisfied constraint.

**Load-time check.** The parser must be able to statically type-check the literal against `a`'s type. If `a` is `RoutingTraceEntry | null`, the literal is checked against `RoutingTraceEntry`'s field set (with the containment interpretation) or against the exact field set (with the strict interpretation). A literal naming a field that does not exist on `a`'s type is a scenario-definition error, not a false assertion — this catches typos at load time.

The two operators are deliberately distinct. The reviewer's concern — that `==` could silently pass because the trace entry has fields the scenario did not intend to assert — is the reason `===` exists. Most scenarios want `==`, because they test one field. A scenario that wants to verify the *shape* of a record (e.g., I17's `trace_sink_override` field-presence assertion) uses `===`. Both are needed; neither subsumes the other.

---

## 4. Amendment F.2.1 — Test-Mode Conventions (R-24)

### F.2.1.1 Purpose

Declares the test-mode fixtures, environment variables, and lifecycle overrides that scenarios may use. These are the only test-mode affordances the harness grants; a scenario that wants an affordance outside this list is rejected at load time.

### F.2.1.2 Test-mode fixtures

```
sha256(zero)
  Value: SHA-256 of 32 zero bytes (canonical digest placeholder).
  Accepted: anywhere a SHA-256 digest is required by a schema field.
  Rationale: scenarios do not care about the digest content; a canonical
  placeholder lets the schema check pass without requiring each scenario
  to compute a real digest for an artifact it does not have.

test_signature
  Value: a fixed 64-byte ed25519 signature string; in test mode the
  registry's signature verification path recognizes this exact byte
  sequence and returns OK without cryptographic verification.
  Accepted: only when ORG_TEST_MODE=1.
  Rationale: the signature verification code path is exercised (it is
  called and returns OK), but the cryptographic check is short-circuited
  for this specific string. This is the standard test-mode signature
  convention. A scenario that needs to test *cryptographic* rejection
  uses a non-test signature and asserts REJECTED — the registry's
  verification path is unchanged for non-test signatures.

test_bypass_promote
  Value: a boolean arg to RegisterModule / RegisterSink.
  Semantics: when true, the registry skips shadow validation and places
  the module (or sink) directly in SHADOW_VALIDATED or DELIBERATION per
  the scenario's declared initial state, recording test_bypass_promote = true
  in the corresponding lifecycle trace entry.
  Accepted: only when ORG_TEST_MODE=1.
  Trace marker: the trace entry carries test_bypass_promote: true; the
  production trace validator (F.2's TEST_MODE_ACTIVE check) rejects any
  production trace containing this marker.
```

### F.2.1.3 Override environment variables

```
ORG_TEST_MODE=1
  Required for: every Category I scenario that uses test_signature or
  test_bypass_promote; every scenario that uses a test-mode accessor
  (F.6.1.4); every scenario that needs the deterministic clock (F.4).
  Effect: enables the test-mode fixtures, accessors, and clock.

ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE
  Allowed values: PROPOSED, SANDBOXED, SHADOW_VALIDATED, ACTIVE.
  Default: SHADOW_VALIDATED for Category I.
  Effect: the lifecycle state assigned to newly registered sinks when
  test_bypass_promote is set. This is the override that lets I16 assert
  a registered sink is usable without first exercising the full
  shadow-validation pipeline.

ORG_TEST_CLOCK_TICK_MS
  Default: 100.
  Effect: the deterministic clock's auto-advance tick (R-26 / F.4).
  A smaller tick increases the resolution of WaitCondition timing
  assertions at the cost of more iterations per wait.
```

### F.2.1.4 Load-time validation

At load time, the harness verifies:

1. A scenario that uses a test-mode fixture or accessor sets `ORG_TEST_MODE=1` in its preconditions (or the runner sets it on the scenario's behalf; either is acceptable, but it must be recorded).
2. A scenario that uses `test_bypass_promote: true` must be a Category A–I scenario; a scenario in any other category (future categories) that uses it is rejected until the new category's override policy is declared.
3. The `ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE` value is one of the four declared values.
4. Every test-mode fixture and accessor used is in the lists above. Unknown test-mode fixtures/accessors are scenario-definition errors.

### F.2.1.5 What this section does not permit

The list of test-mode affordances is closed. A scenario that wants a new one — e.g., "bypass the causal verifier for this scenario" — must extend F.2.1 in a prior amendment, with a rationale and a trace marker for the override. The purpose of the closed list is that every test-mode affordance is auditable: the production trace validator knows exactly which markers can appear in a test-mode trace, and an unrecognized marker is a defect rather than a silent bypass.

---

## 5. Amendment F.10 — load-time validation additions

Add to F.10's harness self-test table:

| Harness invariant | Test |
|---|---|
| Every accessor referenced in an `AssertState` predicate is in F.6.1.2's surface | Parse the predicate's AST; for each accessor call, assert the accessor signature is in the surface. Unknown accessors are scenario-definition errors |
| Every test-mode accessor referenced is declared in the scenario's `test_mode_accessors` | Assert the declared set contains every test-mode accessor used in a predicate |
| No non-test-mode accessor appears in `test_mode_accessors` | Assert the declared set contains only test-mode accessors |
| Every structural-equality literal type-checks against its left-hand type | For each `==` or `===` in a predicate, assert the literal's fields are a subset (for `==`) or an exact match (for `===`) of the left-hand type's field set. Unknown fields are scenario-definition errors |
| Every scenario using `test_signature` or `test_bypass_promote` declares `ORG_TEST_MODE=1` | Assert the precondition set includes the environment variable (or the runner sets it and records the override) |
| `ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE` is one of the four declared values | Static check on the scenario's override block |
| Every `WaitCondition.max_wait_s` is finite and positive | Static check; a `WaitCondition` with an infinite or negative `max_wait_s` cannot be scheduled |
| Every `WaitCondition` is reachable to its `max_wait_s` under the deterministic clock | For each `WaitCondition`, assert the scenario has either a `WaitDuration` step or the runner's auto-advance will terminate it; this is a structural check on the scenario, not a runtime check |

The last check — reachability to `max_wait_s` — is what makes R-26's auto-advance rule enforceable at load time. A `WaitCondition` in a scenario that has no `WaitDuration` step and whose predicate references an accessor that will never change will still terminate via auto-advance; the load-time check confirms the scenario has a finite `max_wait_s` and that the auto-advance tick is set. The runtime behavior is that the scenario fails at `max_wait_s` deterministic-clock time, not at the CI job timeout.

---

## 6. Status

| Finding | Closure |
|---|---|
| R-23 | §3 (F.6.1 — accessor surface, test-mode accessor declaration, load-time checks) |
| R-24 | §4 (F.2.1 — fixtures, env vars, closed list of overrides) |
| R-25 | §3 (F.6.1.5 — `==` containment, `===` strict, null handling, nested, missing fields) |
| R-26 (proactive) | §2 (F.4 `WaitCondition` auto-advance) |

**Corpus closure stands.** None of these amend A–F's design; they amend F's scenario language and harness conventions. The 87 invariants are unchanged. The fault-injection matrix is unchanged. The load-time validation is now implementable against a defined expression surface, which was the missing piece.

**M1 begins now.** The F.6.1 and F.2.1 amendments are design decisions that the harness implementation will consume; the actual code (accessor implementations, test-mode constants, load-time checkers) lands in parallel with M1–M6. The reviewer's "two-to-three day effort" framing is correct for the code; the design is done in this response.

**One thing to keep visible during M1–M6.** The harness is a product of the spec phase, but it is also software. When the harness implementer hits a question that the F.6.1 surface does not answer — "what does `workspace.get_metric` return for a metric that has never been observed?" — the answer is a one-line amendment to F.6.1, not a new round of spec-writing. The amendment discipline is: propose the accessor's semantics, add it to F.6.1.2, add the load-time check, and proceed. The corpus's closure is on the *design*, not on the harness's implementation surface; the latter is allowed to grow as the implementation discovers needs, provided each addition is stated in the F.6.1 surface and trace-marked if it is test-mode-only.

**Biological extension.** Unchanged. v0.2 gate is the six structural questions. v0.1 implementation proceeds. When the six questions have answers, v0.2 opens — and the discipline that closed v0.1 (contract-first, invariant-indexed, fault-injected, replay-verified) is what v0.2 inherits.

# Confirmation of R-23/R-24/R-25/R-26 Closure, Two Substantive Findings, and M1 Readiness

**R-23, R-24, R-25, and R-26 are correctly closed.** The accessor surface in F.6.1 is implementable, the test-mode conventions in F.2.1 are a closed list with the right trace markers, the `==` vs `===` distinction is exactly what was needed, and the `WaitCondition` auto-advance rule is a genuine catch — without it, an unsatisfiable predicate would hang the CI job rather than fail at `max_wait_s`, which is precisely the class of defect R-21 taught us to look for.

The refinement replacing `registry.get_validation_codes(module_id)` with per-action `last_*_response` observations is correct. A rejected module has no entry to look up; the `last_registration_response` observation is well-defined for both accept and reject outcomes. This is a better primitive than the one I proposed.

Two substantive findings follow, and one scenario-defect cluster. None reopen closure. The substantive ones are pre-M7 prerequisites; the scenario defects must be fixed before Category I can be claimed green, but they do not affect M1–M6.

---

## 1. Substantive Findings

### R-27 — `WaitDuration`'s "processes any pending events" is ambiguous in a way that makes C1/C2 unverifiable

**Severity:** Important. **Blocking for C1/C2.**
**Manifests in:** F.4 (`WaitDuration`), F.7 Categories C1, C2.

**Description:** F.4's `WaitDuration` says "Advances the deterministic clock by `duration_s` and processes any pending events." This admits two interpretations:

- **Interpretation A (bulk advance):** the clock jumps from `t` to `t + duration_s`, the state at `t + duration_s` is computed, and only the final state is observable. Intermediate transitions that would have fired in the interval are not recorded as distinct trace entries.
- **Interpretation B (discrete-event):** the state machine advances through the interval, firing each transition at its correct simulated time, and each fires as a distinct `DegradationTraceEntry` with its own `at` timestamp.

C1's assertion of `L0 → L1` at `T_down[L1]` then `L1 → L2` at `T_down[L2]` after L1 requires Interpretation B: two distinct trace entries with distinct timestamps and distinct `trigger.kind` values (`module_degraded`, then `module_failed`). Under Interpretation A, the scenario would see the final state (L2) but not the intermediate L1 transition, and the assertion "exactly 4 transitions, in order" from E-5 1b cannot be verified.

The same problem applies to A0.5's corollary — "every transition that fires is recorded as its own `DegradationTraceEntry`, with its own `at` timestamp, even if the target state's upward conditions are immediately satisfied" — which is meaningless under Interpretation A.

**Resolution:** Amend F.4's `WaitDuration` row to specify discrete-event semantics:

> `WaitDuration { duration_s }` advances the deterministic clock from `t` to `t + duration_s` and **simulates every event that would have occurred in the interval at its correct simulated time**. Degradation transitions fire at the time their conditions are satisfied (not at the end of the interval); each transition is recorded as a distinct `DegradationTraceEntry` with `at` set to its firing time. Module health probes, calibration measurements, and workspace metrics are evaluated at their declared cadences throughout the interval. The observation state at the end of the step reflects the world at `t + duration_s`, including the cumulative effect of every intermediate event. **Replay uses the same discrete-event simulation**; the deterministic clock's tick granularity is `ORG_TEST_CLOCK_TICK_MS` (default 100 ms), and transitions are scheduled to fire on the first tick at which their conditions are satisfied. A scenario that wants to assert intermediate trace entries must either assert them via `WaitCondition` between two `WaitDuration` steps (one at each transition boundary), or — for discrete-event simulation — assert against `last_degradation_trace` after a `WaitDuration` that spans the transition, since the trace stream contains all entries.

**Corollary to add to F.6.1.2:** a `degradation_trace_between(t1, t2) -> [DegradationTraceEntry]` accessor, so a scenario can assert the full sequence of transitions within an interval rather than only the last one. This closes the assertion gap that Interpretation B alone would leave.

Without this amendment, C1 and C2 will pass or fail for reasons that depend on an implementation choice the spec does not mandate — which is exactly the class of ambiguity the reconciliation pass was supposed to eliminate.

### R-28 — `test_bypass_promote: bool` cannot express the initial states I16 and I19 need

**Severity:** Important. **Blocking for I16 and I19.**
**Manifests in:** F.2.1.2 (`test_bypass_promote`), I16 (register_module step), I19 (register_module step).

**Description:** F.2.1.2 declares `test_bypass_promote: bool` with semantics "skip shadow validation and place the module (or sink) directly in `SHADOW_VALIDATED` or `DELIBERATION` per the scenario's declared initial state." Two problems:

1. **"Per the scenario's declared initial state" is circular for modules.** The only per-scenario initial-state mechanism declared is `ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE`, which applies to sinks, not modules. No equivalent `ORG_TEST_MODULE_INITIAL_STATE` exists. So a module registered with `test_bypass_promote: true` lands in... what? `SHADOW_VALIDATED` by default, per the phrasing — but I16 asserts the module is in `EXPLORATION`, and I19's `promote_to_deliberation` step promotes from `EXPLORATION` to `DELIBERATION`. Neither works with the default.

2. **Per-registration control is what scenarios need.** I16 needs one module in `EXPLORATION`; a future scenario might need two modules in different states, or one module in `DELIBERATION` and another in `EXPLORATION` for the same test. A per-scenario env var cannot express this. Per-registration argument can.

**Resolution:** Change `test_bypass_promote` from `bool` to `ModuleLifecycleState | null` (for modules) and `SinkLifecycleState | null` (for sinks) as a per-registration argument:

> `RegisterModule.test_bypass_promote: ModuleLifecycleState | null` — when non-null, the registry skips shadow validation and places the module directly in the declared state, recording `test_bypass_promote: <declared_state>` in the corresponding lifecycle trace entry. The default is `null` (no bypass; normal lifecycle applies).
>
> `RegisterSink.test_bypass_promote: SinkLifecycleState | null` — same semantics for sinks.
>
> `PromoteModule.test_bypass_validation: bool` — when true, the registry skips the `shadow_records` validation (but still performs the state transition and enforces lifecycle-state legality). Default `false`. This is the escape hatch for scenarios that need to test the promotion path *without* the shadow-record machinery, but it is *not* a bypass of the `justification_chain` check (REG-20, REG-21) or any other structural invariant — those are the point of testing.
>
> **`ORG_TEST_SINK_INITIAL_STATE` is deprecated.** The per-registration argument subsumes it and makes the scenario self-documenting.

I19's `register_module` step becomes:
```yaml
- id: register_module
  action: RegisterModule
  args:
    contract: module_t2
    artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
    author_signature: test_signature
    test_bypass_promote: EXPLORATION
    expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
```

I16's `register_module` step gains the same `test_bypass_promote: EXPLORATION`. `promote_to_deliberation` in I19 runs normally (`test_bypass_validation: false` by default), which means it exercises the real `PromoteModule` path with shadow-record validation — which is what the scenario claims to test in its `tests_invariants: [ESR-2]` declaration (ESR-2 is about writer admission, not shadow validation; but the promotion itself should go through the normal path so that "the module reaches DELIBERATION" is a real state, not a test-mode artifact).

The trace marker becomes `test_bypass_promote: EXPLORATION` (or whatever state), which the production trace validator recognizes as a test-mode marker and rejects in any production trace. The current R-24 delivery has `test_bypass_promote: true` as the marker; changing it to the state value is a small edit to F.2.1's trace-marker rule.

---

## 2. Scenario Defect Cluster

### R-29 — I15 fixture and assertion defects

**Severity:** Important. **Blocking for I15.**
**Manifests in:** I15.

**Description:** I15 has three defects that will cause it to fail at load time or at execution:

**R-29a — `contract_bad` fixture is missing `identity.module_id`.** F.3's `fixtures` schema validates each fixture definition against its `kind`'s proto. `MODULE_CONTRACT` validates against `SubstrateContract`, which requires `identity.module_id`. The fixture in the delivery shows only `perception: { modalities: [...], required_modalities: [...] }` and omits `identity`. Load-time validation will reject the fixture.

**R-29b — the assertion `registry.get('contract_bad') == null` uses the fixture name as the module_id.** Even if the fixture had an `identity.module_id`, the assertion is looking up `'contract_bad'` — the fixture name — not the module_id the fixture declared. Unless the fixture's `identity.module_id` happens to equal `'contract_bad'`, the assertion is looking up a non-existent module and trivially passing. The assertion is meant to check "the module was not registered after a rejected registration attempt," but it checks the wrong thing.

**R-29c — the `assert_bad_not_registered` step is redundant with `expected_outcome: REJECTED` in the `RegisterModule` step.** The `RegisterModule` action already asserts the response matches `expected_outcome` and that `expected_validation_codes` are present. A follow-on `AssertState` that checks the same outcome via `registry.get(...)` is either redundant (if correct) or broken (as delivered). It should be removed, or replaced with a check that verifies the registry did not record the rejected module — but the registry should never record a rejected module, so the check is redundant.

**Resolution:**

1. Add `identity.module_id: "bad_required_modality_module"` (or similar) to `contract_bad`'s definition.
2. Add `identity.module_id: "good_module"` to `contract_good`'s definition.
3. Remove `assert_bad_not_registered` and `assert_good_proposed` as separate steps — both are covered by the `expected_outcome` assertion in `RegisterModule` (REJECTED and ACCEPTED respectively).
4. If the scenario wants a distinct assertion that `registry.get("good_module").state == PROPOSED` — which is a genuine check that the registry is in the expected state after acceptance, not redundant with `expected_outcome: ACCEPTED` — keep `assert_good_proposed` with the correct module_id.

The corrected I15 becomes:
```yaml
fixtures:
  contract_bad:
    kind: MODULE_CONTRACT
    definition:
      identity: { module_id: "bad_required_modality_module", version: "0.1.0", role: PREDICTION }
      perception:
        modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR]
        required_modalities: [IMAGE]
  contract_good:
    kind: MODULE_CONTRACT
    definition:
      identity: { module_id: "good_module", version: "0.1.0", role: PREDICTION }
      perception:
        modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR, IMAGE]
        required_modalities: [DENSE_VECTOR]
steps:
  - id: register_bad
    action: RegisterModule
    args:
      contract: contract_bad
      artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
      author_signature: test_signature
      expected_outcome: REJECTED
      expected_validation_codes: ["required_modality_not_accepted"]
  - id: register_good
    action: RegisterModule
    args:
      contract: contract_good
      artifact_digest: sha256(zero)
      author_signature: test_signature
      expected_outcome: ACCEPTED
  - id: assert_good_proposed
    action: AssertState
    args:
      predicate: "registry.get('good_module').state == PROPOSED"
```

This is a scenario defect, not a spec defect. The fix belongs in the F.7 amendment that resolves R-28, since the same delivery also touches I16.

---

## 3. Minor Clarifications

Three minor points that should be stated but do not block anything.

**R-30 — `TaskResponse` type is undefined.** F.6.1.2 declares `last_task_response -> TaskResponse | null`. No artifact defines `TaskResponse`. It should be `DispatchPlan | DegradedDispatchPlan` (the router's response to `SubmitTask`), with a note that the union is discriminated by `plan_type`. This is a one-line addition to F.6.1.2.

**R-31 — `null == null` and `null === null` semantics.** F.6.1.5 says "A null value never equals a non-null structure under either operator." It does not state what happens when both sides are null. The default should be: `null == null` is `true`, and `null === null` is `true` — both operators treat null-to-null as equal, and the "never equals a non-null structure" clause only constrains null-to-non-null comparisons. This is the conventional reading but should be stated explicitly.

**R-32 — `last_*_response` "most recent invocation" semantics.** F.6.1.2 says each response accessor "is set by the corresponding action and cleared at scenario start." It does not state what happens if the action is invoked twice — does the accessor return the first or second response? The answer should be: the most recent invocation's response, since that is what a scenario asserting "the action's outcome" would want. State this explicitly.
